Stability of complement value problems for nonlocal p p -Lévy operators https://doi.org/10.1007/s00030-024-01006-6 Financial support by the DFG via the Research Group Unit FOR 3013: “Vector-and Tensor-Valued Surface PDEs”(project number 417223351) is gratefully acknowledged.
Guy Foghem

Abstract
We set up a general framework tailor-made to solve complement value problems governed by symmetric nonlinear nonlocal integrodifferential p p -Lévy operators. A prototypical example of integrodifferential p p -Lévy operators is the well-known fractional p p -Laplace operator. Our main focus is on nonlinear Integro-Differential Equations (IDEs) in the presence of Dirichlet, Neumann and Robin conditions and we show well-posedness results. Several results are new even for the fractional p p -Laplace operator but we develop the approach for general translation-invariant nonlocal operators. We also bridge the gap from nonlocal to local, by showing that solutions to the local Dirichlet and Neumann boundary value problems associated with p p -Laplacian are strong limits of the nonlocal ones.



keywords
Integro-Differential Equations(IDEs), Nonlinear nonlocal operators,
p
-Lévy operators, Nonlocal Sobolev spaces, Stability of weak solutions

1991 Mathematics Subject Classification

35D30, 35J20, 34B15, 35J60, 35J66, 35B35, 46E35

Introduction

In this article, we study certain classes of nonlinear Integro-Differential Equations (IDEs) associated with symmetric nonlinear nonlocal
p
-Lévy operators,
1<p<\infty
which are integro-differential operators of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}Lu(x)&=2\pv\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-2}(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}y,\qquad\quad(x\in\R^{d})\\
&=2\pv\int_{\R^{d}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}y,\end{split}

(1.1)

defined for a measurable function
u:\R^{d}\to\R
whenever the right hand side makes sense.
Throughout, we assume that
d\geq 1
,
\psi(t)=|t|^{p-2}t,\,t\in\R
and the function
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty)
is the density of a symmetric
p
-Lévy measure, i.e.,

\nu
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu(h)=\nu(-h)\quad&\text{ \,\, and\,\, }\qquad\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h<\infty.\end{split}

(L)

Moreover, if
\nu
is radial, we use the same notation for its radial profile, i.e.,
\nu(h)=\nu(|h|)
. The notation
\pv
stands for the principal value, while
a\land b
denotes
\min(a,b)
,
a,b\in\R
. More succinctly, given an open set
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
, we are interested in nonlinear nonlocal problems of the forms

\displaystyle Lu=f\,\,\text{ in }\,\,\Omega\quad\text{ and }\quad\tau\mathcal{N}u+(1-\tau)\beta|u|^{p-2}u=g\,\,\text{ on }\,\,\Omega^{c},

(
P_{\nu,\tau}
)

where for simplicity
1
1

1

At the risk of being pedantic, the general case
\tau\in(0,1)
readily reduces to the case
\tau=\frac{1}{2}
by renaming the data
\beta
and
g
.

\tau\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},1\}
and the data
f:\Omega\to\R
,
g,\beta:\Omega^{c}\to\R
are given. The operator
u\mapsto\cN u
is the nonlocal
p
-normal derivative across the boundary of
\Omega
,

\displaystyle\cN u(y)=2\int_{\Omega}\psi(u(y)-u(x))\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}x\qquad\quad(y\in\Omega^{c}).

(1.2)

The nonlinear operator
u\mapsto\cN u
appears naturally while deriving the nonlocal Gauss-Green formula
(see Appendix
B.2
), which was introduced in the linear setting in
[
36
]
. We point out that another type of such an operator appeared earlier in literature see for instance
[
39
]
.

In view of problem (

P

ν
,
τ

), it is necessary and sufficient to prescribe the complement condition on
\Omega_{e}:=\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
with
\Omega_{\nu}=\Omega+\supp\nu
which we name as
the nonlocal boundary
or
the exterior boundary
of
\Omega
with respect to
\nu
. As well, we also call
\Omega_{\nu}^{*}=\Omega\cup\Omega_{\nu}
to be
the nonlocal hull
of
\Omega
with respect to
\nu
. Note that if
0\in\supp\nu
then
\Omega\subset\Omega_{\nu}
hence
\Omega_{\nu}^{*}=\Omega_{\nu}
; see Section
2
and Section
8
for more details. Broadly speaking, PDEs oriented folks might regard view the problem (

P

ν
,
τ

) as the nonlocal counterpart of the nonlinear local problem of the form

\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}u=f\text{ in }\Omega\quad\text{ and }\quad\tau\partial_{n,p}u+(1-\tau)\beta|u|^{p-2}u=g\text{ on }\partial\Omega,

(
P_{\tau}
)

where
\Delta_{p}u=\operatorname{div}(|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u)
denotes the
p
-Laplace operator and when
\Omega
is smooth,
\partial_{n,p}u=|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot n
is the
p
-normal derivative on the boundary
\partial\Omega
of
\Omega
, viz., at a point
x\in\partial\Omega
,
n(x)
is the outward normal derivative on the boundary
\partial\Omega
. Of course, one immediately recognizes that the complement (resp. boundary) condition the problem (

P

ν
,
τ

) (resp. (

P
τ

)) is the Neumann condition
\cN u=g
(resp.
\partial_{n,p}u=g
) for
\tau=1
, the Robin condition
\cN u+\beta|u|^{p-2}u=2g

(resp.
\partial_{n,p}u+\beta|u|^{p-2}u=2g
) for
\tau=\frac{1}{2}
and the Dirichlet condition
|u|^{p-2}u=g
or equivalently
u=|g|^{p^{\prime}-2}g
for
\tau=0
and
\beta=1
where we have
pp^{\prime}=p+p^{\prime}
. Actually, our second goal is to show that weak solutions to the problem (

P
τ

) with
\tau=0,1
are strong limits of those of problems of the forms (

P

ν
,
τ

). Let us first discuss the assumption in (
L
) which is utterly decisive and, afterwards, give a prototypical example.

It remarkably appears that the assumption in (
L
) is unavoidable. Indeed, on the one hand, the symmetry condition is natural in the sense that one has

\displaystyle\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu^{\operatorname{sym}}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

where
\nu^{\operatorname{sym}}(h)=\frac{1}{2}(\nu(h)+\nu(-h))
is the symmetric part of
\nu
. On the other hand, it appears that
\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)<\infty
for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
if and only if
\nu
is
p
-Lévy integrable, i.e.,
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
; cf. Section
4
for the details. It turns out that the symmetry and the
p
-Lévy condition in (
L
) can be self-generated through the energy form
\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u).
Furthermore, a heuristic computation reveals that the first variation of the operator
u\mapsto Q(u)=\frac{1}{p}\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)
on the Banach space
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})=\{u\in L^{p}(\R^{d})\,:\,\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)<\infty\}
with the norm

\|u\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})}=\big(\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}+\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)\big)^{1/p}
gives rise to the
p
-Lévy operator
L
, which is also the subdifferential of
Q
. Moreover, we have

\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)=\langle Lu,u\rangle=Q^{\prime}(u)(u)
,
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
. Note in passing that under mild assumptions, the space
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
embeds in an Orlicz space; this is implied by the Sobolev type inequality established in
[
51
]
. The density
\nu
is somewhat the “order” of the operator
L
, which becomes apparent in the case of fractional kernel
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
,
s\in(0,1)
is fixed. For case
p=2
, one immediately recognizes the so-called Lévy condition in (
1.7
) while the operator
L
is translation invariant and generates a symmetric Lévy process
[
93
]
. It is important to emphasize that our main assumption here is only the condition (
L
), i.e., the symmetry and the
p
-Lévy integrability of
\nu
. Moreover, we do not compare
\nu
to the fractional kernel
|h|^{-d-sp}
, this makes the investigations in our framework very general and more challenging. In general, the pointwise evaluation of
Lu(x)
and
-\Delta_{p}u(x)
for
u\in C^{2}_{b}(\R^{d})
is warranted in the degenerate case (also often called the superquadratic case), i.e.
p\geq 2
. However, in the singular case (also often called the subquadratic case), i.e.,
1<p<2
, the pointwise definition of
Lu(x)
and
-\Delta_{p}u(x)
might not exist even for
a bona fide
function
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
. In many situations, the operators
L
and
-\Delta_{p}
do not always act on functions in a reasonable pointwise sense. We refer interested readers to Appendix
B.1
for some sketchy examples. In both cases, i.e.,
1<p<\infty
, a reasonable alternative is rather to evaluate
Lu
and
\Delta_{p}u
in the generalized sense, i.e., in the weak sense or via their respective associated energies forms. For instance by duality (see Section
3.1
), one finds that the nonlinear operators
L:W^{p}_{\nu}(\R^{d})\to(W^{p}_{\nu}(\R^{d}))^{\prime}
and
\Delta_{p}:W^{1,p}(\R^{d})\to(W^{1,p}(\R^{d}))^{\prime}
are well-defined.
In particular,
Lu
and
\Delta_{p}u
are distributions. Morally, the nonlocal operator
L
may be as good or as bad as the local operator
-\Delta_{p}
. The operator
L
can be seen as a prototype of a nonlinear nonlocal operator of divergence form just as the
-\Delta_{p}
is a prototype of a nonlinear local operator of divergence form.

For prototypical example, consider
s\in\R
then an effortless computation reveals that
\nu(h)=\frac{C_{d,p,s}}{2}|h|^{-d-sp}
belongs to
L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
, i.e.,
\nu
satisfies (
L
) if and only if
s\in(0,1)
. In this case, the resulting space
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})=W^{s,p}(\R^{d})
is the usual fractional Sobolev-Slobodeckij space, while the associated integro-differential operator
L
is the well-known fractional
p
-Laplace operator
(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}
,

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u(x)

\displaystyle=C_{d,p,s}\pv\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-2}(u(x)-u(y))}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y,

where
C_{d,p,s}
is a normalizing constant of the fractional
p
-Laplacian which we define by

\displaystyle C_{d,p,s}=\frac{s(1-2s)\Gamma(\frac{d+sp}{2})}{\pi^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\Gamma(\frac{sp+1}{2})\Gamma(p(1-s))\cos(s\pi)}=\frac{2^{2s}\,\Gamma(\frac{d+sp}{2})\Gamma(\frac{2s+1}{2})\Gamma(2(1-s))}{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}\,\,|\Gamma(-s)|\,\Gamma(\frac{sp+1}{2})\Gamma(p(1-s))}.

Our choice of the constant
C_{d,p,s}
, (see Section
9.4
where the asymptotic near
s=0
is also considered), guaranties the following properties:

•

For
p=2
, by
[
54
, Proposition 2.21]
,
C_{d,2,s}
is the unique normalizing constant of the fractional Laplacian such that
\widehat{(-\Delta)^{s}u}(\xi)=|\xi|^{2s}\widehat{u}(\xi)
,
\xi\in\R^{d}
for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
, where
\widehat{u}(\xi)=\int_{\R^{d}}e^{-ix\cdot\xi}u(x)\mathrm{d}x
denotes the Fourier transform of
u
. Namely, we have

\displaystyle C_{d,2,s}=\frac{s(1-2s)\Gamma(\frac{d+2s}{2})}{\pi^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\Gamma(\frac{2s+1}{2})\Gamma(2(1-s))\cos(s\pi)}=\frac{s2^{2s}\Gamma(\frac{d+2s}{2})}{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}\Gamma(1-s)}.

•

For any
u\in L^{\infty}(\R^{d})\cap C^{2}(B_{1}(x))
,
\nabla u(x)\neq 0
we have
(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u(x)\xrightarrow{s\to 1}-\Delta_{p}u(x)
.

•

For all
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
we have
\|u\|_{W^{s,p}(\R^{d})}\xrightarrow{s\to 1}\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}
; see Section
9.5
.

•

Moreover we have the following asymptotic behaviors

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{C_{d,p,s}}{s(1-s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\Gamma(p)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{s\to 1}\frac{C_{d,p,s}}{s(1-s)}=\frac{2p}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|K_{d,p}}.

(1.3)

Here we emphasize that the constant
K_{d,p}
, see
[
55
,
54
]
for the computation, plays a crucial role in our asymptotic analysis and is given by

\displaystyle K_{d,p}

\displaystyle=\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w_{d}|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{p+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+p}{2}\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}\big)}.\vskip-8.61108pt

(1.4)

The asymptotic
s\to 1
, highlighting the factor
K_{d,p}
is already anticipated in
[
54
, Eq: 2.38]
for the case
p=2
since
K_{d,2}=\frac{1}{d}
. The above asymptotic in (
1.3
) perfectly aligns with the case
p=2
, as obtained in
[
34
]
.
Despite the amusing fact of this asymptotic, it is important for the reader to keep in mind that
C_{d,p,s}
is purely artificial and that only the case
p=2
naturally appears as the unique constant for which
\widehat{(-\Delta)^{s}u}(\xi)=|\xi|^{2s}\widehat{u}(\xi)
. Other different normalizing constants for the fractional
p
-Laplacian are proposed in
[
31
,
97
]
wherein, one also finds other representations of the fractional
p
-Laplace operator. Additional special examples of
p
-Lévy operators are obtained by considering
p
-Lévy kernels of the form
\nu(h)=\frac{C_{d,p,s}}{2}|h|^{-d-sp}+\frac{C_{d,q,t}}{2}|h|^{-d-tq}
with
s,t\in(0,1)
and
1<q\leq p
; the particular case
q=p
yields the mixed nonlocal operator
Lu=(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u+(-\Delta)^{t}_{p}u
.

The main purpose of this article is twofold. The primary objective is to study the well-posedness of the nonlocal problem (

P

ν
,
τ

) under additional mild assumptions.
For instance, as an
avant-goût
, let us illuminate what we do with the particular case of the Neumann problem, i.e.,
\tau=1
and for the particular fractional kernel
\nu(h)=\frac{C_{d,p,s}}{2}|h|^{-d-sp}
.
We refer the reader to Section
8
for the general setting and more details. We point out that
u
is a weak solution to Neumann problem
(P_{\nu,1})
if
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and satisfies

\displaystyle\vskip-17.22217pt\cE(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y\qquad\text{for all $u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})$}.\vskip-8.61108pt

(1.5)

Here,
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})=\big\{u:\R^{d}\to\R\,\text{meas.}:\,u|_{\Omega}\in L^{p}(\Omega),\,\,\cE(u,u)<\infty\big\}

where the energy form
\cE(\cdot,\cdot)
is defined as

\displaystyle\cE(u,v)=\frac{C_{d,p,s}}{2}\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\frac{\psi(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

In Theorem
8.12
, we establish the well-posedness up to additive constants of the variational problem (
1.5
) and hence of the Neumann problem
(P_{\nu,1})
on the space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
whenever
f\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
and
g\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}})
with
\omega(h)=(1+|h|)^{-d-sp}
are compatible, i.e.,

\int_{\Omega}f(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)\mathrm{d}y=0.

Let us highlight two important observations regarding the weak formulation (
1.5
) at this stage.
First of all, one observes in this particular case that the weight
\omega^{1-p^{\prime}}
is rapidly increasing, hence the Neumann data
g\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}})
is required to decay rapidly at infinity. Although this may seem restrictive, it is however counterintuitive since the space

L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}})
is in fact the largest admissible function space for the Neumann data
g
. Indeed, we establish a non-existence result in Theorem
8.14
where we exhibit some examples of Neumann data
g
not belonging to
L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}})
and compatible with
f=0

and such that the variational Neumann problem (
1.5
) does not have a weak solution in
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.
As the second observation, one notices that
[
36
, Definition 3.6]
and subsequent definitions like
[
79
, Definition 2.7]
look very similar to (
1.5
) at first glance; for the general case, we refer the reader to Definition
8.6
. However, the test space defined in
[
36
, Eq. (3.1)]
,
[
79
, Section 2]
depends on the Neumann data
g
, which is somewhat unaccustomed. We emphasize that our test space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
in the weak formulation (
1.5
) does not depend on the Neumann data
g
. Next, we would like to mention that our strategy of studying the problem (

P

ν
,
τ

) in the general setting, including Dirichlet and Robin complement problems, requires bringing into play certain crucial tools. These include nonlocal function spaces,
nonlocal Poincaré types inequalities and nonlocal trace spaces.
On the one hand, the Poincaré inequalities encoding the coercivity of the nonlocal energies which we establish in Section
7
include the Poincaré type inequality when
\Omega
is not necessarily connected but has finitely many connected components and the Poincaré-Friedrichs type inequality when
\Omega
is bounded only in one direction. To the best of our knowledge, such generalization of the Poincaré inequalities do not exist hitherto in the literature. On the other hand, the nonlocal trace spaces truly embody the Dirichlet, Neumann and/or Robin complement data
g
of the problem (

P

ν
,
τ

). There are several new findings on nonlocal trace spaces, we refer interested reader for instance to
[
60
,
41
,
13
,
91
,
44
]
. It is worth mentioning that the result
[
60
]
provides a robust nonlocal fractional trace space, viz., one is able to recover the classical local trace from the nonlocal one.

The secondary objective is to prove the
L^{p}
-convergence of nonlocal to local weak solutions; cf Section
11
. Let us explain for which family of problems we are able to prove the convergence. Given a family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
,
\nu_{\varepsilon}\geq 0
, of radial
p
-Lévy kernels, we prove in Theorem
9.6
that, there holds the formula

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=K_{d,p}\int_{\R^{d}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x

(1.6)

for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
if and only if the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=1\,\,\text{and for all $\delta>0,$}\,\,\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|>\delta}\hskip-8.61108pt\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=0.

(1.7)

In other words, condition (
1.7
) is the sharp condition regarding the validity of the formula (
1.6
), a.k.a., the BBM-formula. The aforementioned family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
was introduced in
[
55
,
54
]
. Note in passing that a typical example of family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfying (
1.7
), is to consider the normalized fractional kernels
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=a_{\varepsilon,d,p}|h|^{-d-(1-\varepsilon)p}
with
\varepsilon=1-s
and
a_{\varepsilon,d,p}=\frac{p\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}
; see Example
9.4
. Another fascinating example of
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfying (
1.7
) is obtained from any radial function
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty)
that is
p
-Lévy normalized, i.e.,
\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\,\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h=1
, by defining
\nu_{\varepsilon}
the rescaled version of
\nu
, as follows

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\begin{cases}\varepsilon^{-d-p}\nu\big(h/\varepsilon\big)&\text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ |h|\leq\varepsilon,\\
\varepsilon^{-d}|h|^{-p}\nu\big(h/\varepsilon\big)&\text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ \varepsilon<|h|\leq 1,\\
\varepsilon^{-d}\nu\big(h/\varepsilon\big)&\text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ |h|>1.\end{cases}\end{split}

Let
L_{\varepsilon}
and
\cN_{\varepsilon}
be the nonlocal operators associated with
\nu_{\varepsilon}
and put
\mu=K_{d,p}^{-1}
. Consider the problem

\displaystyle\mu L_{\varepsilon}u=f_{\varepsilon}\,\,\text{ in }\,\,\Omega\quad\text{ and }\quad\tau\mu\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}u+(1-\tau)|u|^{p-2}u=g_{\varepsilon}\,\,\text{ on }\,\,\Omega^{c}.

(
P_{\nu_{\varepsilon},\tau}
)

Surprisingly, under the condition in (
1.7
) and some mild conditions on
\Omega
,
f_{\varepsilon}
and
g_{\varepsilon}
we prove, cf. Section
11
, that weak solutions to the problem
(

P

ν
ε

,
τ

)
with
\tau\in\{0,1\}
strongly converge in
L^{p}(\Omega)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to the weak solutions of the corresponding local problem
(

P
τ

)
. We also prove the convergence of weak associated with regional type operators. Another appealing effect of the approximation family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is that for
u\in L^{\infty}(\R^{d})\cap C^{2}(B_{1}(x))
with
\nabla u(x)\neq 0
there holds,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}2\pv\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-2}(u(x)-u(y))\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y=-K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u(x).

In particular we find that
(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u(x)\to-\Delta_{p}u(x)
as
s\to 1
. A similar pointwise asymptotic from the fractional
p
-Laplacian to the
p
-Laplacian
-\Delta_{p}
can be found in
[
24
,
32
,
67
]
. We refer to Section
9
for more details. In our strategy of proving the convergence of solutions we need, cf. Section
10
, to establish the robust Poincaré type inequalities
à la
A. Ponce
[
84
]
including the situation where
\Omega
is only bounded in one direction.

Overall, the main results of the article can be summarized as follows.

(1)

We introduce appropriate nonlocal Sobolev spaces adapted for the problem (

P

ν
,
τ

). In this framework, we establish completeness and compact embeddings in Theorem
3.9
, Theorem
6.2
and Theorem
6.6
.

(2)

In Theorem
5.9
and Theorem
5.15
we establish a nonlocal trace theorem.

(3)

In Theorem
7.2
and Theorem
7.4
we establish Poincaré-Friedrichs inequalities for a domain
\Omega
bounded in one direction or
|\Omega|<\infty
. In Theorem
7.8
and Theorem
7.12
we establish Poincaré type inequalities for a general domain
\Omega
which is only finite union of connected components.

(4)

In Theorem
8.12
, Theorem
8.14
, Theorem
8.18
and Theorem
8.23
we establish the well-posedness of the problem (

P

ν
,
τ

).

(5)

In Theorem
4.1
and Theorem
4.3
provide an analytic characterization of
p
-Lévy integrability of the kernel
\nu
. Analogously, in Theorem
9.6
we provide a characterization of a
p
-Lévy approximation family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
.

(6)

In Theorem
9.9
and we establish the pointwise convergence of
L_{\varepsilon}u(x)\to-K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u(x)
, a fortiori the convergence
(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u(x)\to-\Delta_{p}u(x)
in Corollary
9.12
.

(7)

In Theorem
10.1
, Theorem
10.7
and Theorem
10.8
we establish robust Poincaré type inequalities; including the situation where
\Omega
is only bounded in one direction.

(8)

Finally, in Theorem
11.5
and Theorem
11.2
we establish convergence of weak solutions to the problem (

P

ν
ε

,
τ

) to those of (

P
τ

),
\tau=0,1
.

We emphasize that some of our aforementioned findings are also new for the fractional
p
-Laplacian. In general, the results we deem innovative for fractional kernels of the form
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}\mathds{1}_{B_{r}(0)}(h),s\in(0,1)
for some
r\in(0,\infty]
include the trace theorem in (b), Poincaré-Friedrichs inequalities in (a) and (g), Poincaré type inequalities in (c) and (g) and, the well-posedness and convergence of the Neumann and/or Robin problem in (d) and (h).

Let us comment on related and existing works in the literature. For an outstanding reference on basics related to the
p
-Laplace operator, we refer the reader to the classical lecture notes
[
74
]
. The study of nonlocal problems driven by
p
-Lévy kernels is becoming popular. We refer the reader to
[
54
,
52
,
55
,
51
]
for studies of nonlocal functions spaces generated by
p
-Lévy kernels. For recent studies of Integro-Differential Equations (IDEs) involving the Lévy operator of type
L
(for the case
p=2
) see
[
52
,
53
,
37
,
68
]
and also
[
4
]
where the authors compare their interaction kernel to the fractional one; we emphasize that such comparison is not required in our setting. See also
[
90
,
91
]
and
[
89
]
for the studies of Dirichlet problems associated with Lévy operators driven by singular Lévy measures. For problems related to general nonlocal elliptic operators of Lévy type see
[
54
,
96
]
. We also point out
[
12
,
91
]
where the nonlinear Douglas identity for
p
-Lévy type operators are investigated. The fractional
p
-Laplacian is one of the most studied
p
-Lévy operator in the framework of IDEs. For instance, the study of Dirichlet problems can be found in
[
57
,
33
,
64
,
82
,
87
,
19
,
58
,
95
]
. For the study of Neumann problems see
[
79
,
80
]
. Note that
[
79
]
is somewhat the extension of the set-up from
[
36
]
to the nonlinear setting. We emphasize that in both works
[
36
,
79
]
, the test spaces in the weak formulation of the Neumann problem depend on the Neumann data, which is not the case in ours. We also refer to
[
75
,
3
,
97
]
for the studies of problems related to regional type operators. For regularity of solutions associated to the fractional
p
-Laplacian and its alike see
[
65
,
66
,
18
,
26
,
27
]
. Several different other models of nonlocal nonlinear problems such as porous medium equations are listed in
[
70
]
. It is noteworthy to emphasize that our setting is sufficiently general and includes kernels with bounded support, integrable kernels and non-integrable kernels. Nonlocal problems involving regional type operators with integrable kernels are addressed for instance in
[
2
,
3
,
25
]
. Note however, that one of the downsides of integrable kernels is that associated function spaces are not rich enough, as they do not differ much from the underlying
L^{p}
-space. This contrasts with non-integrable kernels yielding strict refinement of
L^{p}
-Lebesgue spaces whose additional structure are of great importance. The class of kernels with bounded support are popular in peridynamic. Indeed, nonlocal problems driven by integro-differential operators associated with kernels of bounded support appear as core models to several problems, wherein, the nonlocal boundary
\Omega_{e}=\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
is also called volume constrain or finite horizon. We explain in Section
8
how to deal with such types of kernels. For recent studies of nonlocal problems aiming at the application to peridynamics see
[
4
,
52
,
59
,
42
]
, several additional references can be found therein. Additional works in peridynamic involving nonlocal operators of regional type are dressed in
[
77
,
9
]
.

Let us comment about the convergence from nonlocal to local. In fact, under the assumption in (
1.7
),
the convergence in (
1.6
) remains true with
\R^{d}
replaced by any extension domain
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
; see for instance
[
55
]
. Interestingly, the latter convergence also holds in the sense of the gamma convergence or Mosco convergence; see for instance
[
54
,
56
,
85
]
. This type of limit was originally studied for Lipschitz bounded domains in
[
15
]
and several generalizations have recently emerged, see for instance
[
5
,
43
,
38
,
55
,
86
,
78
]
see also the variants
[
81
,
72
]

along with the references contained there. It is worth mentioning that, in the framework of fractional Sobolev spaces with
p=2
, the earlier work
[
1
, Section 4]
by R. Adams, N. Aronszajn, and T. Smith, which is now largely overlooked in the current literature, also addresses the problem of convergence from nonlocal to local energy forms, as subsequently studied by Bourgain-Brezis-Mironescu
[
15
]
and the asymptotic compactness result as studied by A. Ponce
[
84
]
. Of course, just as
[
15
]
, the work
[
1
]
also highlights the necessity of the rectifying factor
(1-s)^{1/p}
in front of the fractional seminorm when addressing theses asymptotic properties; this is the cornerstone of the question addressed in the work
[
15
]
.
To the best of our knowledge, rigorous proofs of convergence of weak solutions to nonlocal problems with complement Neumann condition to the local ones appear in
[
54
,
52
,
60
]
. A heuristic proof of the convergence for fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{s}
was investigated in
[
36
]
. Note however that the convergence of nonlocal Neumann problems associated with regional type operators can be found in
[
2
,
3
,
30
]
. In contrast to the Neumann problems, there is a substantial amount of works treating the convergence from nonlocal to local of weak solutions to homogeneous Dirichlet problems associated with the fractional
p
-Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}
; we refer interested reader for instance to
[
20
,
48
,
10
,
11
,
92
]
. The convergence of weak solutions of Dirichlet problems for fractional
g
-Laplacian where
g
is an Orlicz function see
[
14
]
. For convergence of solutions to elliptic problems, see
[
54
,
96
]
. Last, uniform convergence of viscosity solutions to the constrained fractional
p
-Laplace operator is established in
[
67
]
. For more on viscosity solutions associated with the
p
-Laplacian and the fractional
p
-Laplacian see for instance the recent results
[
73
,
74
,
71
,
32
]
as well as the references therein.

The rest of this article is organized as follows. We introduce some basic concepts regarding the support of
\nu
in Section
2
. In Section
3
we study nonlocal Sobolev spaces, whereas Section
5
is dedicated to the study of the corresponding nonlocal trace spaces. Meanwhile, we give an analytic characterization of
p
-Lévy integrability in Section
4
. In Section
6
we give some compact embeddings. Nonlocal Poincaré and nonlocal Poincaré-Friedrichs inequalities are established in Section
7
, while their robust versions are provided in Section
10
. Section
9
and Section
11
focus on convergences from nonlocal to local objects; this includes convergence of forms, weak solutions and nonlinear operators. We achieve the convergence of weak solutions by implicitly establishing the Gamma convergence of related nonlocal forms to the local ones. We also characterize the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfying (
1.7
). In Appendix
A
and Appendix
B
we provide some basic results such as nonlocal Gauss-Green formula, pointwise evaluations of
Lu
and
\cN u
, elementary inequalities that are useful for the study of nonlinear operators under consideration such as
L
and
-\Delta_{p}
.

2.
Basic concepts and notations

2.1.
Notations

Throughout this article unless otherwise stated, we assume that
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is an open set,
1<p^{\prime},p<\infty
with
p+p^{\prime}=pp^{\prime}
and
\nu
is a symmetric kernel satisfying (
L
). We frequently use the convex inequality
(a+b)^{p}\leq 2^{p-1}(a^{p}+b^{p})
for
a>0,b>0
. If there is no specific mention, all functions and sets are assumed to be at least Borel measurable and are understood in almost everywhere sense. Given two Banach spaces
X
and
Y
,
Y\subset X
we write
Y\hookrightarrow X
to indicate that
Y
is continuously embedded in
X
. Given two quantities
F
and
G
the relation
F\asymp G
indicates that there are positive constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that
C_{1}\leq F/G\leq C_{2}
. In general,
C>0
denotes a generic constant depending on the local and
\varepsilon>0
is a small quantity tending to
0
. The Euclidean scalar product of
x=(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{d})\in\R^{d}
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{d})\in\R^{d}
is
x\cdot y=x_{1}x_{1}+x_{2}y_{2}+\cdots+x_{d}y_{d}
and denote the norm of
x
by
|x|=\sqrt{x\cdot x}
. For
r>0

B_{r}(x)=B(x,r)=\{y\in\R^{d}\,:\,|y-x|<r\}
. The distance between two sets
A,B\subset\R^{d}
is given by
\dist(A,B)=\inf\{|x-y|\,:\,x\in A\,y\in B\}
. In addition for
x\in\R^{d}
we denote
\dist(x,B)=\dist(\{x\},B)
. Furthermore,
|A|
denotes the Lebesgue measure of
A
. We also abuse the notation
|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|
to denote
d-1
-Hausdorff surface measure of the
d-1
-dimensional sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

2.2.
Support and nonlocal boundary

We introduce some basic definitions in connection with the measure theoretic support of a measurable function.

Definition 2.1
.

The support of a measurable function
\omega:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty)
is defined as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\supp\omega&=\R^{d}\setminus\mathcal{O}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathcal{O}=\bigcup\big\{O\,:\,\text{$O$ is open and $\omega=0$ a.e. on $O$}\big\}\\
&=\big\{x\in\R^{d}\,:\,\text{$\omega>0$ a.e. on any open set $O$ s.t. $x\in O$}\big\}.\end{split}

Note that
\supp\omega
is a closed set and
\omega=0
a.e.
\R^{d}\setminus\supp\omega
. In particular,
\omega=0
a.e. if and only if
\supp\omega=\emptyset.
If
\omega
is a continuous function then
\supp\omega=\overline{\big\{h\in\R^{d}\,:\,\omega(h)\neq 0\big\}}.

The latter is not true in general. For instance, on the real line we put
\omega=\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, where
\mathbb{Q}
is the set of rational numbers, then
\overline{\big\{h\in\R\,:\,\omega(h)\neq 0\big\}}=\R
but
\supp\omega=\emptyset
.

Definition 2.2
.

We say that
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty)
has full support if
\supp\nu=\R^{d}
, equivalently
\nu(h)>0
for almost every
h\in\R^{d}
that is
|\{\nu=0\}|=0
.

Remark 2.3
.

It is very tempting to think that the set of zeros of
\nu
in
\supp\nu
is a null set, i.e.,
|\supp\nu\cap\{\nu=0\}|=0
. In other words, is it true that

\nu>0
a.e. on
\supp\nu
? This is not always true, indeed consider
O\subset\R^{d}
a non-empty open set whose boundary has positive measure, i.e.,
|\partial O|>0
. The function
g(x)=\dist(x,\R^{d}\setminus O)

is continuous. Note that
\{g\neq 0\}=O
and thus
\supp g=\overline{O}
. However, we have

|\supp g\cap\{g=0\}|=|\partial O|>0
.

Definition 2.4

(Nonlocal hull and nonlocal boundary)
.

Given a measurable set
S\subset\R^{d}
:

(1)

We define the nonlocal hull with respect to
\nu
(or simply the
\nu
-nonlocal hull) of
S
to be the set
S_{\nu}^{*}=S\cup S_{\nu}
where
S_{\nu}=S+\supp\nu
. Note that if
0\in\supp\nu
then
S\subset S_{\nu}
that is
S_{\nu}^{*}=S_{\nu}=S+\supp\nu
.

(2)

We define the exterior boundary (or the nonlocal boundary) of
S
with respect to
\nu
denoted
S_{e}
or
\partial_{\nu}S
, to be the set
S_{e}=S_{\nu}^{*}\setminus S=S_{\nu}\setminus S=(S+\supp\nu)\setminus S
(or
\partial_{\nu}S=S_{\nu}\setminus S
).

The terminologies are justified by the following facts.
From an analysis point of view, the set
S_{\nu}=S+\supp\nu
is smallest set among the sets
A
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=:L_{A}u(x)\quad\quad\text{for all $x\in S$}.

That is
S_{\nu}\subset A
and we have
Lu(x)=L_{S_{\nu}}u(x)
for all
x\in S
. In this respect, the nonlocal hull
S_{\nu}^{*}
is smallest set containing
S
such that
Lu(x)=L_{S_{\nu}}u(x)
for all
x\in S
. In other words, the values
Lu(x)
for
x\in S
solely depend on the values of
u
in
S_{\nu}
. Therefore to solve a nonlocal equation of the form
Lu=f
in
S
, it is sufficient to prescribe exterior boundary condition on
S_{\nu}\setminus S
. For instance, if we consider
S=\Omega
then the Neumann condition
\cN u=g
, only makes if
g=0
on
\R^{d}\setminus\Omega_{\nu}
; see Section
8
for additional details. Indeed, for
x\in\Omega
and
y\in\R^{d}\setminus\Omega_{\nu}
we have
x-y\not\in\supp\nu
so that
\nu(x-y)=0
. Hence,

\displaystyle\cN u(y):=\int_{\Omega}\psi(u(y)-u(x))\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}x=0\qquad\text{for all $y\in\R^{d}\setminus\Omega_{\nu}$.}

From a probabilistic point of view, when
0\in\supp\nu
so that
S\subset S_{\nu}
, the nonlocal hull
S_{\nu}^{*}=S_{\nu}
can be seen as the reachable area of a jump process starting from a point in
S
. In some sense, any jump of the process starting in
S
cannot reach farther beyond the set
S_{\nu}
. Therefore, if a jump process is censored (restricted) at the set
S
then the whole universe of the process is
S_{\nu}
. In other words, any censored process on
\Omega
is never aware of anything happening on
\R^{d}\setminus\Omega_{\nu}
.

Example 2.5
.

Let us mention two classes of examples that are well-known in the literature.

(1)

If
\nu
has full support, i.e.
\supp\nu=\R^{d}
then
S_{\nu}=S+\R^{d}=\R^{d}
and
S_{e}=\R^{d}\setminus S
. That is the exterior boundary of
S
with respect to
\nu
coincides with its whole complement. A typical example include
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
. Integro-Differential Equation (IDEs) associated with nonlocal operators driven kernels
\nu
with
\supp\nu=\R^{d}
are often called complement values problems in the modern literature.

(2)

If
\supp\nu=\overline{B_{\delta}(0)}
,
\delta>0
then
S_{\nu}=S+\overline{B_{\delta}(0)}=\{x\in\R^{d}:\dist(x,S)\leq\delta\}
, i.e.,
S_{\nu}
is the
\delta
-tubular thickening neighborhood of
S
. On other hand we have
S_{e}=S_{\nu}\setminus S=\{x\in\R^{d}\setminus S:\dist(x,S)\leq\delta\}
.

For instance, if
S=B_{r}(a)
,
r>0
and
a\in\R^{d}
then
S_{\nu}=B_{r+\delta}(a)
and
S_{e}=B_{r+\delta}(a)\setminus\overline{B_{r}(a)}
. For a concrete examples we have
\nu(h)=\mathds{1}_{B_{\delta}(0)}(h)
or
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}\mathds{1}_{B_{\delta}(0)}(h)
. These types of kernels often appear in the area of peridynamics, wherein the exterior boundary
S_{e}=S_{\nu}\setminus S
is also known as the volume constraint or the finite horizon. Thus, Integro-Differential Equation (IDEs) associated with nonlocal operators driven by kernels
\nu
with
\supp\nu=\overline{B_{\delta}(0)}
, are called in the area of peridynamic as volume constrained problems or finite horizon problems. See for instance the recent works
[
4
,
52
,
59
,
42
]
and several additional references therein.

Remark 2.6
.

Let us highlight some remarks concerning
\supp\nu
and
S_{\nu}
.

(1)

If
0\in\supp\nu
then
S\subset S_{\nu}
. This property is sometimes important to make sense of many IDEs.

(2)

If
S
and
\supp\nu
are compact so is
S_{\nu}
. But if both are closed, and one is compact then
S_{\nu}
is a closed set.

(3)

If
S
is open then
S_{\nu}=S+\supp\nu=\bigcup_{h\in\supp\nu}S+h
is also open, since each
S+h
is open.

(4)

If each
S
and
\supp\nu
is of positive measure then
S_{\nu}
has a non-empty interior.

3.
Nonlocal function spaces

In this section we introduce generalized Sobolev-Slobodeckij-like function spaces with respect to a
p
-Lévy integrable function
\nu
and an open subset
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
, in particular the space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and its nonlocal trace space
T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
. The function spaces are tailor-made for Integro-Differential Equations (IDEs) with complement value problems.
Recall that
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty)
is the density of a symmetric
p
-Lévy measure, that is,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu(h)=\nu(-h)\quad&\text{ and}\qquad\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h<\infty.\end{split}

(L)

In fact
\nu
is said to be
p
-Lévy integrable, i.e.
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
.

3.1.
Nonlocal energy forms

Recall
\psi(t)=|t|^{p-2}t
. Given a symmetric kernel
k:(\R^{d}\times\R^{d})\setminus\diag\to[0,\infty)
we consider the energy form
\cE^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
by

\displaystyle\cE^{k}(u,v)=\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\,k(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

We begin with the following proposition.

Proposition 3.1
.

The following assertions hold true.

(1)

If
\cE^{k}(u,u)
and
\cE^{k}(v,v)
are finite so is
\cE^{k}(u,v)
and we have

|\cE^{k}(u,v)|\leq\cE^{k}(u,u)^{1/p^{\prime}}\cE^{k}(v,v)^{1/p}.

(2)

Let the Banach space
W^{p}_{k}(\R^{d})=\{u\in L^{p}(\R^{d})\,:\,\cE^{k}(u,u)<\infty\},
with the norm

\|u\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{k}(\R^{d})}=\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}+\cE^{k}(u,u)
. Then the nonlinear operator

Lu:=\cE^{k}(u,\cdot):W^{p}_{k}(\R^{d})\to(W^{p}_{k}(\R^{d}))^{\prime}
is bounded, with

\displaystyle|\langle Lu,v\rangle|=|\cE^{k}(u,v)|\leq\cE^{k}(u,u)^{1/p^{\prime}}\cE^{k}(v,v)^{1/p},

\displaystyle\|Lu\|_{(W^{p}_{k}(\R^{d}))^{\prime}}\leq\cE^{k}(u,u)^{1/p^{\prime}}\leq\|u\|^{p-1}_{W^{p}_{k}(\R^{d})}.

Proof.

(ii)
follows from
(i)
whereas,
(i)
is an immediate consequence of the Hölder inequality.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

Formally, the operator
L
is denoted as

\displaystyle Lu(x):=2\pv\int_{\R^{d}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))k(x,y)\mathrm{d}y,

whenever this expression makes sense. For instance if
k(x,y)=\nu(x-y)
with
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
then under additional mild conditions
Lu(x)
can be defined for smooth functions (see Appendix
B.1
).
Besides this, a curious reader easily verifies that a similar analysis can be achieved with the spaces
W_{k}^{p}(\R^{d})
and the nonlocal operator
L
replaced with the space
W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
and the local
p
-Laplace operator
-\Delta_{p}
.

From now we assume that
\cE^{k}(u,u)<\infty
and
\cE^{k}(v,v)<\infty
. We will consider special cases of the
k(x,y)
that are of paramount interest.
First we consider the form
\cE_{\Omega}(\cdot,\cdot)
given by

\displaystyle\cE_{\Omega}(u,

\displaystyle v)=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\psi(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\,\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\min(\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x),\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y))\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

That is,
k(x,y)=\min(\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x),\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y))\nu(x-y)
. Next, the Gauss-Green formula (see Appendix
B.2
) motivates us to consider the special energy form
\cE(\cdot,\cdot)
, define by

\displaystyle\cE(u,v)

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\,\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\,\max(\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x),\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y))\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

That is, in this case we have
k(x,y)=\max(\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x),\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y))\nu(x-y)
. Taking
k(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}(\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x),\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y))\nu(x-y)
we obtain another related form
\cE_{+}(\cdot,\cdot)
, that is

\displaystyle\cE_{+}

\displaystyle(u,v)=\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\,\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))(\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)+\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y))\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

As shown in the next result, the forms
\cE(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\cE_{+}(\cdot,\cdot)
are equivalents.

Proposition 3.3
.

We have
\cE(u,u)\leq 2\cE_{+}(u,u)\leq 2\cE(u,u)
and
\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)\leq\cE(u,u)
.

Proof.

The proof is immediate since we have
\min(a,b)\leq\max(a,b)\leq a+b\leq 2\max(a,b)
for all
a,b>0
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

For brevity let us denote
\chi(x,y)=|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-2}(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))
. Recall that
\Omega_{e}=\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
and
\Omega_{\nu}=\Omega+\supp\nu
. The following identities hold

\displaystyle\cE(u,v)

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\chi(x,y)\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x=\Big(\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}+2\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega}\Big)\chi(x,y)\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\Big(\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}+2\iint\limits_{\Omega_{e}\Omega}\Big)\chi(x,y)\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x=\hskip-8.61108pt\iint\limits_{\mathcal{D}(\Omega_{\nu},\Omega_{e})}\hskip-8.61108pt\chi(x,y)\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

where
\mathcal{D}(\Omega_{\nu},\Omega_{e})=(\Omega_{\nu}\times\Omega_{\nu})\setminus(\Omega_{e}\times\Omega_{e})
. Indeed, since
y\in\Omega
and
x\in\R^{d}\setminus\Omega
necessarily
x\in\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
otherwise
\nu(x-y)=0
. Analogously we have

\displaystyle\cE_{+}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}\chi(x,y)\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega_{\nu}}\chi(x,y)\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}\chi(x,x+h)\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x=\iint\limits_{\Omega\,\supp\nu}\chi(x,x+h)\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x.

3.2.
Nonlocal Sobolev-Slobodeckij-like spaces

We define the space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
by

\displaystyle W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)=\Big\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega)\,:\,|u|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)}:=\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)<\infty\Big\},

equipped with the norm

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)}=\big(\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|u|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)}\big)^{1/p}=\big(\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)\big)^{1/p}.

For the fractional kernel
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
,
s\in(0,1)
, the space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
corresponds to the classical fractional Sobolev-Slobodeckij space
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
.
Whereas, if
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
then we find that
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)=L^{p}(\Omega)
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\big|u(x)-u(y)\big|^{p}\,\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=2^{p}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

We point out that a Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev type inequality for the nonlocal Sobolev space
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
is established in
[
51
]
. For various choices of
\nu
it appears (see Section
7
) that,
\cE(u,u)<\infty
implies
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
.
Motivated by this remark and following
[
94
,
47
,
54
]
we introduce the crucial space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})&=\Big\{u:\R^{d}\to\R\text{ meas.}\,:\,u|_{\Omega}\in L^{p}(\Omega)\,\!\text{ and }\,\!|u|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}<\infty\Big\}\,\\
&=\Big\{u:\R^{d}\to\R\text{ meas.}\,:\,u|_{\Omega}\in L^{p}(\Omega)\,\!\text{ and }\,\!\cE(u,u)<\infty\Big\},\end{split}

(3.1)

where we put
|u|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}:=\cE_{+}(u,u)
. The equality in (
3.1
) follows from Proposition
3.3
. The space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is a seminormed space equipped with the seminorm defined by

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}=\big(\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|u|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\big)^{1/p}\asymp\big(\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\cE(u,u)\big)^{1/p}.

Last, we define
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
as the space of functions that vanish on the complement of
\Omega
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})&=\{u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\penalty\ :\penalty\ u=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{a.e. on }\R^{d}\setminus\Omega\}\,\\
&=\{u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})\penalty\ :\penalty\ u=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{a.e. on }\R^{d}\setminus\Omega\}\,.\end{split}

(3.2)

Naturally,
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is endowed with the
\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
which is equivalent therein with the norm

\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})}
, viz., for
u\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})}=\big(\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\cE(u,u)\big)^{1/p}\asymp\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}.

It is straightforward to check that
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is a closed subspace of
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
and
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. Another closely related closed subspace of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is defined as

\displaystyle W_{\nu,0}^{p}(\Omega|\R^{d})=\overline{C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})},{\text{ i.e., the closure of $C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)$ in $W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})$}}.

It is worth emphasizing that if
\Omega
has a continuous boundary then
W_{\nu,0}^{p}(\Omega|\R^{d})
coincides with
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
(see
[
54
, Theorem 3.76]
and also Theorem
3.11
). However, this equality does not hold true in general. For
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp},s\in(0,1)
we denote
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})=W^{s,p}(\Omega|\R^{d})
,
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})=W^{s,p}_{\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and
W_{\nu,0}^{p}(\Omega|\R^{d})=W^{s,p}_{0}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. Furthermore, if
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
then
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})=L^{p}(\R^{d},\mu)
with
\mu(x)=\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)+\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(x)\int_{\Omega}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y
. Indeed if
u\in L^{p}(\R^{d},\mu)
then
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
and we have

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x+2\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p+1}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}+2^{p}\int_{\Omega^{c}}|u(y)|^{p}\int_{\Omega}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p+1}(1+\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})})\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d},\mu)}.

Conversely, if
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
then
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)=W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
and we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{c}}

\displaystyle|u(y)|^{p}\mu(y)\mathrm{d}y\leq 2^{p-1}\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}(|u(y)-u(x)|^{p}+|u(x)|^{p})\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=2^{p-1}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}+2^{p-1}\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}|u(y)-u(x)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

Remark 3.5
.

As a direct consequence of the Proposition
3.1
we have the following.

(1)

If
u,v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
then
|\cE(u,v)|\leq\cE(u,u)^{1/p^{\prime}}\cE(v,v)^{1/p}
and
\cE(u,\cdot)\in(W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}))^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
u,v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
then
|\cE_{\Omega}(u,v)|\leq\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)^{1/p^{\prime}}\cE_{\Omega}(v,v)^{1/p}
and
\cE_{\Omega}(u,\cdot)\in(W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega))^{\prime}
.

A simple proof of the following Theorem can be found in
[
54
, Theorem 3.46]
.

Theorem 3.6
.

The spaces

W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
and
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
are separable reflexive Banach spaces.

Remark 3.7
.

It is worthwhile noticing that
\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
is always a norm on
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
, but not in general a norm on
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
if
\nu
is not fully supported. A simple counterexample is given by
\nu(h)=\mathds{1}_{B_{1}(0)}(h)
and
\Omega=B_{1}(0)
. For the function
u(x)=\mathds{1}_{B^{c}_{2}(0)}(x)
we have
\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}=0
whereas
u\neq 0
. More generally, assume that
\Omega
is bounded and
\nu
has a compact support. Let
S=\R^{d}\setminus\big(\Omega\cup\Omega_{\nu}\big)
and consider the function
u(x)=\mathds{1}_{S}(x)
. A routine verification shows that
\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}=0
but
u\neq 0
. This means that
(W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})})
cannot be a normed space.

However
(W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})})
is a Banach space when
\nu
is of full support; cf.
[
54
,
52
]
for the proof. Let us say a few words for the general case where
\nu
is not necessarily fully supported. In view of the Remark
3.4
we introduce another version of the space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. Namely, recall
\Omega_{\nu}=\supp\nu+\Omega
and
\Omega_{\nu}^{*}=\Omega\cup\Omega_{\nu}
we consider the space

\displaystyle W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})

\displaystyle=\big\{u:\Omega_{\nu}^{*}\to\R\,\text{meas.}:u|_{\Omega}\in L^{p}(\Omega),\,|u|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}<\infty\big\},

equipped with the seminorm
\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}=\big(\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|u|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}\big)^{1/p}
where
we define

\displaystyle|u|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega_{\nu}}\hskip-4.30554pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

It is worth recalling that if
0\in\supp\nu
then
\Omega\subset\Omega_{\nu}
so that
\Omega_{\nu}^{*}=\Omega\cup\Omega_{\nu}=\Omega_{\nu}
.

Remark 3.8
.

We have
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})\equiv W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
in the sense that both spaces are isometric isomorphic.
Clearly if
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
then
u_{\nu}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
with
u_{\nu}=u|_{\Omega_{\nu}^{*}}
and we have
\|u_{\nu}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}=\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
. Conversely, if
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
then
\widetilde{u}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
where
\widetilde{u}
is the zero extension of
u
off
\Omega_{\nu}^{*}
and
\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}=\|\widetilde{u}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
.
Note that
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is the null function if and only if
u=0
a.e. on
\Omega_{\nu}^{*}
. The notation
W^{p}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
instead of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
should be also appropriate. However, we deliberately keep the notation
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
for simplicity. It is counterintuitive to think that
\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}
always defines a norm on
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
. In view of the Remark
2.3
, it is legitimate to assume that
|\supp\nu\cap\{\nu=0\}|=0
. Nevertheless, the latter seems not enough to show that
\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}
is a norm. More precisely, one may consider the following question.

Open Question:
Assume that
\nu(h)>0
for all
h\in\supp\nu
. Prove or disprove that is
\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}
defines a norm on
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
.

Next, we provide a condition under which we can show that
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
is a Banach space.

Theorem 3.9
.

The space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\equiv W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
is a separable and reflexive Banach space if we have

\displaystyle\omega(x):=\int_{\Omega}1\land\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y>0\quad\text{for almost all $x\in\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega$}.

(
S_{1}
)

This claim is true in the particular case where
\nu
is of full support.

Proof.

Observe that if
x\not\in\Omega_{\nu}
then for all
y\in\Omega
we have
x-y\not\in\supp\nu
. It follows that
\omega(x)=0
on
\R^{d}\setminus\Omega_{\nu}
. By Theorem
5.9
we have
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})\equiv W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\R^{d},\omega)\equiv L^{p}(\Omega_{\nu},\omega).
Whence, there is
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{\nu},\omega)}\leq C\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}\quad\text{for all $u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})$}.

(3.3)

Assume that
(

S
1

)
holds, if
\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}=0
then
u=0
a.e. on
\Omega
and by (
3.3
),
u\cdot\omega=0
a.e. on
\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
. There holds that
u=0
a.e.
\Omega_{\nu}
, since
\omega>0
a.e. on
\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
. Whence the seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}
is a norm on
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
.
Now, consider
(u_{n})_{n}
to be a Cauchy sequence in
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
, then taking into account the estimate (
3.3
), the sequences
(u_{n})_{n}
and
(w_{n})_{n}
with
w_{n}=u_{n}\cdot\omega
are Cauchy sequences in
L^{p}(\Omega)
and
L^{p}(\Omega_{\nu})
respectively. Thus, by passing to a subsequence if necessary, we can assume there exist
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
and
w\in L^{p}(\Omega_{\nu})
such that

(1)

(u_{n})
converges in
L^{p}(\Omega)
and pointwise a.e. on
\Omega
to some
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
,

(2)

(w_{n})
converges in
L^{p}(\Omega_{\nu})
and pointwise a.e. in
\Omega_{\nu}
to some
w\in L^{p}(\Omega_{\nu})
.

We extend
u
for
x\in\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
by
u(x)=w(x)/\omega(x)
which is well defined since
\omega>0
a.e. on
\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
. It follows that

u:\Omega^{*}_{\nu}=\Omega\cup\Omega_{\nu}\to\R
is measurable since
u_{n}\to u
a.e. on
\Omega
and for almost all
x\in\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
we have
u_{n}(x)=w_{n}(x)/\omega(x)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}w(x)/\omega(x)=u(x).

It turns out that
(u_{n})_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence in
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
converging to
u
on
L^{p}(\Omega)
and a.e. on
\Omega_{\nu}
. This combined with Fatou’s lemma implies that

\displaystyle|u_{n}-u|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}\leq\liminf_{m\to\infty}|u_{n}-u_{m}|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

We conclude that
\|u_{n}-u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
which show the completeness of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
.
Now, consider the isometry
\mathcal{I}:W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})\to L^{p}(\Omega)\times L^{p}(\Omega\times\Omega_{\nu})
with
\mathcal{I}(u)=(u(x),(u(x)-u(y))\nu^{1/p}(x-y))
. From its Banach structure, the space
\big(W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu}),\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})}\big)
, identified with
\mathcal{I}\big(W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})\big)
, is separable and reflexive as a closed subspace of the separable and reflexive space
L^{p}(\Omega)\times L^{p}(\Omega\times\Omega_{\nu})
.
∎

Example 3.10
.

A typical situation where
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
is a Banach space although
\nu>0
a.e. only on
\supp\nu
,
can be obtained as follows. Let
\Omega=B_{1}(0)
and consider
\nu(h)=\mathds{1}_{B_{1}(0)}(h)
or
\nu(h)=\mathds{1}_{B_{1}(0)}(h)|h|^{-d-sp}
,
s\in(0,1)
so that
\supp\nu=\overline{B_{1}(0)}
. Thus,
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})=W^{p}_{\nu}(B_{1}(0)|B_{2}(0))
is a Banach space with
\Omega_{\nu}=B_{1}(0)+\overline{B_{1}(0)}=B_{2}(0)
.

3.3.
Density of smooth functions

For many results, it is crucial that smooth functions with compact support are dense in the function space under consideration. Let us summarize some important results in this direction.

Theorem 3.11
.

Let
\nu
satisfies (
L
) with full support and let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open.

(1)

C^{\infty}(\Omega)\cap W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
is dense in
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
.

(2)

If the boundary
\partial\Omega
is continuous then
C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
is dense in
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
.

(3)

If the boundary
\partial\Omega
is continuous, then
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is dense in
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.

(4)

If the boundary
\partial\Omega
is Lipschitz, then
C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
is dense in
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.

(5)

C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
is dense in
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
.

The proofs of the first and second statements can be found in
[
54
]
and
[
45
]
. The first statement reminisces of the Meyer-Serrin density type result (see
[
54
, Theorem 3.67]
).
Note that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
consists of functions of the form
v|_{\overline{\Omega}}
where
v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\R^{d})
. The proof of the third statement is given in
[
49
]
for a special choice of
\nu
and in
[
54
]
,
[
13
]
for the general case. Note however that the third may fail if
\partial\Omega
is not continuous see for instance
[
49
, Remark 7]
. This goes against the claim
[
28
, Theorem 3.3.9]
.
The proof of the fourth and the fifth assertions are given in
[
56
,
54
]
.

3.4.
Connection with classical Sobolev spaces

Let us delve into the connection between nonlocal Sobolev spaces and classical Sobolev spaces by examining the embeddings and related results. Recall that
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
denotes the classical Sobolev space endowed with the norm

\|u\|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}=\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\,.

While,
W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
is the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
with respect to the
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. We emphasize that
W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
also coincides with the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
in
W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
.
Let
W^{p}_{\nu,0}(\Omega)
be the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
in
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
. Note that, the zero extension to
\R^{d}
of any function in
W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
belongs to
W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
. Recall that
\Omega
is an
W^{1,p}
-extension domain if there is an operator
E:W^{1,p}(\Omega)\to W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
and
C>0
such that
Eu|_{\Omega}=u
and
\|Eu\|_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}\leq C\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}
for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open. The following embeddings hold true.

(1)

W^{1,p}(\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu}(\R^{d}).

(2)

If
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is an
W^{1,p}
-extension domain then
W^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
.

(3)

W^{p}_{\nu}(\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)\,.

(4)

W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega).

(5)

If
\partial\Omega
continuous then
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu,0}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

Note that
(ii)
is implied by
(i)
. Whereas
(v)
follows from the fact that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is dense in
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
; see Theorem
3.11
. We only prove
(i)
, i.e.,
W^{1,p}(\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu}(\R^{d})
since the remaining ones are trivial. A routine argument yields that, for
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
and
h\in\R^{d}

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x+h)-u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\leq|h|^{p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})},

whereas
\|u(\cdot+h)-u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\leq 2^{p}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}
. Therefore we get

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x+h)-u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\leq 2^{p}(1\land|h|^{p})\|u\|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}\,.

Integrating both side over
\R^{d}
with respect to the measure
\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h
yields

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2^{p}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p}\mathrm{d}h)}\|u\|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}.

(3.4)

The desired embedding readily follows.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

The embedding
W^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
may fail if
\Omega
is not an extension domain
(see
[
55
, Counterexample 3.10]
or

[
34
, Example 9.1]
).
More importantly,
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
can be viewed as limiting space of the nonlocal space of type
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
and
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
; see for instance
[
55
,
54
]
.

4.
Characterization of the p-Lévy integrability

We will now see that the
p
-Lévy integrability condition (
L
) is optimal and can be self-generated from the associated energy form. In fact the
p
-Lévy integrability condition (
L
) renders the space
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
more consistent, in a sense that it warrants the space
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
to contain smooth functions.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\nu\geq 0
be symmetric. The following assertions are equivalent.

(1)

The
p
-Lévy condition (
L
) holds, i.e.
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
.

(2)

The embedding
W^{1,p}(\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
is continuous.

(3)

\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)<\infty
for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
.

(4)

\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)<\infty
for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
.

Moreover, this remains true when
p=1
with
BV(\R^{d})
in place of
W^{1,1}(\R^{d})
.

This characterization in Theorem
4.1
highlights the robustness of the
p
-Lévy integrability condition across different function spaces, and ensuring the finiteness of the associated energy forms.

Proof.

(i)\implies(ii)
The estimate (
3.4
) readily yields the continuity of the embedding
W^{1,p}(\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
. The implications
(ii)\implies(iii)
and
(iii)\implies(iv)
are straightforward. It remains to prove that
(iv)\implies(i)
. To this end, assume that
\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)<\infty
for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
. Let
\zeta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R)
with
\zeta\not\equiv 0
and
\supp\zeta\subset(0,1)
and
\vartheta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d-1})
such that
\vartheta=1
on
Q^{\prime}
with
Q^{\prime}=[0,1]^{d-1}
. Consider
u_{i}(x)=\zeta\otimes\vartheta(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})=\zeta(x_{i})\vartheta(x^{\prime}_{i})
where
x^{\prime}_{i}=(x_{1},\cdots x_{i-1},x_{i+1}\cdots,x_{d})
. Clearly we have
u_{i}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
and
\nabla u_{i}(x)=\zeta^{\prime}(x_{i})e_{i}
for
x\in Q=[0,1]^{d}
. By the continuity of the shift, for each
\varepsilon>0,
there is
\delta>0
such that

\|\nabla u(\cdot+h)-\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(Q)}<\varepsilon
for all
|h|\leq\delta.

Using this and the inequality
b^{p}\geq 2^{1-p}(a+b)^{p}-a^{p}
,
a,b\geq 0
we find that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cE_{\R^{d}}(u_{i},u_{i})&\geq\int_{Q}\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u_{i}(x+th)\cdot h\mathrm{d}t\Big|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\geq 2^{1-p}\int_{Q}\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}|\nabla u_{i}(x)\cdot h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x-\varepsilon\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\\
&=2^{1-p}\int_{Q}|\zeta^{\prime}(x_{i})|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}|h_{i}|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h-\varepsilon\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h.\end{split}

Note that
\int_{Q}|\zeta^{\prime}(x_{i})|^{p}\mathrm{d}x=\|\zeta^{\prime}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(0,1)}
. We have
|h_{1}|^{p}+\cdots+|h_{d}|^{p}\geq c_{d,p}|h|^{p}
for some
c_{d,p}>0
, by equivalence of Euclidean norms. Taking
\varepsilon=\frac{C}{2d}
with
C=2^{1-p}c_{d,p}\|\zeta^{\prime}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(0,1)}
we get

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\cE_{\R^{d}}(u_{i},u_{i})

\displaystyle\geq(C-d\varepsilon)\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h=\frac{C}{2}\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h.

(4.1)

Now, consider
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{\delta/2}(0)),

u\not\equiv 0
. Since
B_{\delta/2}(0)\subset B_{\delta}(x)
for all
x\in B_{\delta/2}(0)
we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)&\geq 2\int_{B_{\delta/2}(0)}|u(x)|^{p}\int_{B^{c}_{\delta/2}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\geq 2\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(B_{\delta/2}(0))}\int_{B^{c}_{\delta}(0)}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h.\end{split}

(4.2)

Combing (
4.1
) and (
4.2
) yields
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
that is
\nu
satisfies (
L
). The case
p=1
follows analogously.
∎

The next result underscores that for radial
\nu
, the condition
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})\neq\{0\}
is both necessary and sufficient for the
p
-Lévy integrability condition to hold. We first need the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\phi\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
such that
\phi\geq 0
and
\int_{\R^{d}}\phi(z)\mathrm{d}z=1
. Then for all
u\in L^{1}_{\loc}(\R^{d})
, we have

\displaystyle\cE_{\R^{d}}(u*\phi,u*\phi)\leq\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u).

Proof.

From Jensen’s inequality and simple change of variables we find that

\displaystyle\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{d}}\phi(z)\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x-z)-u(y-z)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\geq\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\Big|\int_{\R^{d}}\phi(z)(u(x-z)-u(y-z))\mathrm{d}z\Big|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u*\phi(x)-u*\phi(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\cE_{\R^{d}}(u*\phi,u*\phi).

∎

Theorem 4.3
.

If
\nu
is radial, then
\nu
satisfies (
L
) if and only if
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})\neq\{0\}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.1
we know
C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})\subset W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
if
\nu
satisfies (
L
).
Conversely, assume there is
v\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
such that
v\not\equiv 0
. Then
|v|\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
with
\cE(|v|,|v|)\leq\cE(v,v)
, since
||v(x)|-|v(y)||\leq|v(x)-v(y)|
. Let
\phi(x)=\kappa e^{-|x|^{2}}
satisfies
\|\phi\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}=1
for some
\kappa>0
. By Young’s inequality and Lemma
4.2
we get
u=|v|*\phi\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)\leq\cE(|v|,|v|)\leq\cE(v,v)<\infty.

Clearly
u\in C^{\infty}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
and
u(x)>0
for each
x\in\R^{d}
, i.e.,
\supp u=\R^{d}
. On the other hand, Young’s inequality implies
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
. In particular,
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\neq 0
.
For each
\varepsilon>0
, there is
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon)>0
such that
\|\nabla u(\cdot+h)-\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}<\varepsilon
for all
|h|\leq\delta
.
Minkowski’s inequality implies

\displaystyle\Big(\hskip-4.30554pt\iint\limits_{\R^{d}B_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|\nabla u(x)\cdot h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}\hskip-8.61108pt

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\hskip-4.30554pt\iint\limits_{\R^{d}B_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\hskip-4.30554pt\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h\mathrm{d}t\Big|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\Big(\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)^{1/p}.

By the rotation invariance of the Lebesgue measure, for
z\in\R^{d}
and
e\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

\displaystyle\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w\cdot z|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=|z|^{p}\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w\cdot e|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=K_{d,p}|z|^{p}.

(4.3)

The fundamental theorem of calculus and a passage to polar coordinates yield

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cE(u,u)^{1/p}&\geq\Big(\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\,B_{\delta}(0)}\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h\mathrm{d}t\Big|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}\\
&\geq\Big(\int_{\R^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\hskip-8.61108pt|\nabla u(x)\cdot w|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\int_{0}^{\delta}\hskip-4.30554ptr^{p+d-1}\nu(r)\mathrm{d}r\Big)^{1/p}\\
&-\varepsilon\Big(\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)^{1/p}\\
&=\Big(K^{1/p}_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}-\varepsilon\Big)\Big(\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)^{1/p}.\end{split}

Since
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})\setminus\{0\}
, we can take
\varepsilon=\frac{K^{1/p}_{d,p}}{2}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\neq 0
so that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cE(u,u)\geq\frac{K_{d,p}}{2}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h.\end{split}

Necessarily, we get
\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h<\infty
.
Next, it is not difficult to show that
u(x)\to 0
as
|x|\to\infty
. Thus for
r>0
there is
R>\delta>0
such that
0<u(x)\leq r
whenever
|x|>\frac{R}{2}
. Now consider the 1-Lipschitz function

\displaystyle\zeta_{r}(s)=\max(-r,\min(r,s))=\begin{cases}s&|s|\leq r\\
r\operatorname{sgn}(s)&|s|\geq r.\end{cases}

It is readily seen that
|\zeta_{r}(u(x))-\zeta_{r}(u(y))|\leq|u(x)-u(y)|
and
0\leq\zeta_{r}(u(x))\leq u(x)
. In particular,
\cE(u,u)\geq\cE(\zeta_{r}(u),\zeta_{r}(u))
. If we set
w_{r}(x)=u(x)-\zeta_{r}(u(x))
then
w_{r}(x)=0
whenever
|x|>R/2
.
Since
B_{R/2}(x)\subset B_{R}(0)
for all
x\in B_{R/2}(0)
, we find that

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)\geq\cE(w_{r},w_{r})

\displaystyle\geq 2\int_{B_{R/2}(0)}|w_{r}(x)|^{p}\int_{\R^{d}\setminus B_{R/2}(0)}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\|w_{r}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\int_{|h|\geq R}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h.

Since
u
is not constant we get
\|w_{r}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\neq 0
. Therefore we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{|h|\geq R}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h<\infty.

Last, note that for
\delta\leq|h|\leq R
and
x\in B_{\frac{\delta}{4}}(0)
we have
|x|<\frac{\delta}{4}<\frac{\delta}{2}\leq|x+h|\leq R+\frac{\delta}{2}
. Since
u
is smooth with full support we put

\displaystyle M=\min_{\frac{\delta}{2}\leq z\leq R+\frac{\delta}{2}}\int_{B_{\frac{\delta}{4}}(0)}|u(x)-u(z)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x>0.

From this we find that

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)

\displaystyle\geq\hskip-4.30554pt\int_{B_{R}(0)\setminus B_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-2.15277pt\int_{B_{\frac{\delta}{4}}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|u(x)-u(x+h)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\,\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\geq M\int_{B_{R}(0)\setminus B_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-17.22217pt\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h.

Hence, it follows that
\int_{B_{R}(0)\setminus B_{\delta}(0)}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h<\infty.

Finally, we find that
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
.
∎

The following result is a straightforwards consequence of Theorem
4.1
and Theorem
4.3
.

Theorem 4.4
.

Assume
\nu\geq 0
is radial. The following assertions are equivalent.

(1)

The
p
-Lévy condition (
L
) holds, i.e.
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
.

(2)

The embedding
W^{1,p}(\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
is continuous.

(3)

\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)<\infty
for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
.

(4)

\cE_{\R^{d}}(u,u)<\infty
for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
.

(5)

The space
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
is nontrivial, i.e.,
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})\neq\{0\}
.

Moreover, this remains true when
p=1
with
BV(\R^{d})
in place of
W^{1,1}(\R^{d})
.

As illustrated in the next result, the Lévy integrability (
L
) draws the borderline for which a space of type
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
is trivial or not, see
[
55
, Proposition 2.15]
or
[
54
, Proposition 3.46]
for a general setting.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\nu\geq 0
be symmetric and put
C_{\delta}=\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h
,
\delta>0
. The following hold true.

(1)

If
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
then
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})=L^{p}(\R^{d})
with equivalence in norms.

(2)

If
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p}\mathrm{d}h)
and
\Omega
is bounded, then bounded Lipschitz functions are in
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
and
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.

(3)

If
\nu
is radial,
\Omega
is connected and
C_{\delta}=\infty
for all
\delta>0
, in particular
\nu\not\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p}\mathrm{d}h)
, then
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
or
u\in C^{1}(\Omega)\cap W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
if and only if
u=c
is a constant function.

(4)

If
\nu
is radial and
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
then there is
\delta=\delta(u)>0
, such that

\displaystyle\frac{K_{d,p}}{2}C_{\delta}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\leq|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})}\leq 2^{p}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p}\mathrm{d}h)}\|u\|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}.

If
\nu
is radial, then for any
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
there is
\delta=\delta(u)>0
, such that

\displaystyle\frac{K_{d,p}}{2}C_{\delta}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\leq|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})}\leq 2^{p}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p}\mathrm{d}h)}\|u\|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}.

5.
Nonlocal Trace Theorem

5.1.
Weighted
L^{p}
-spaces

In order to set up IDEs in
L^{p}
-spaces over
\R^{d}
, we introduce Borel measures on
\R^{d}
that capture the behavior of
\nu
at infinity. We will establish the embedding of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
into weighted spaces
L^{p}(\R^{d},\omega)
for different measures
\omega
. We will need the notion of unimodality.

Definition 5.1
.

\nu
is called
unimodal
if
\nu
is radial with an almost decreasing profile, i.e., there is
c>0
such that
\nu(|x|)\geq c\,\nu(|y|)
for all whenever
|x|\leq|y|
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\nu
satisfies the
p
-Lévy integrability condition (
L
) and
B\subset\R^{d}
be an open set with positive measure, i.e.,
|B|>0
. Define the weights
\overline{\nu}_{B},\widetilde{\nu}_{B}:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty)
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{B}\left(1\wedge\nu(x-y)\right)\mathrm{d}y,

\displaystyle\overline{\nu}_{B}(x)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{ess}\inf\limits_{y\in B}\nu(x-y)\,.

If
\nu
is a unimodal, then for an arbitrary fixed number
R>1
, we define the weight
\widehat{\nu}_{R}:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty)
by

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}_{R}(x)

\displaystyle=\nu(R(1+|x|)).

We emphasize that
\nu
needs not be unimodal for the definition of
\widetilde{\nu}_{B}
and
\overline{\nu}_{B}
.

It is worthwhile noticing that
\widetilde{\nu}_{B}
,
\overline{\nu}_{B}
and
\widehat{\nu}_{R}
somehow rule out the eventual singularity of the original kernel
\nu
. This is illustrated in the next proposition on important properties of the three measures
\widetilde{\nu}_{B}
,
\overline{\nu}_{B}
and
\widehat{\nu}_{R}
.

Proposition 5.3
.

The following assertion are true.

(1)

\widehat{\nu}_{R},\,1\land\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
.

(2)

\widetilde{\nu}_{B}\in L^{1}_{\loc}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
. If
|B|<\infty
, then
\widetilde{\nu}_{B}\in L^{1}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
.

(3)

\overline{\nu}_{B}\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
. If
\nu
unimodal then
\overline{\nu}_{B}\in L^{1}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
.

(4)

\overline{\nu}_{B}(x)=\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x)=0
for all
x\not\in B_{\nu}:=B+\supp\nu
.

(5)

For
B_{1}\subset B_{2}
we have
\overline{\nu}_{B_{2}}\leq\overline{\nu}_{B_{1}}
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{B_{1}}\leq\widetilde{\nu}_{B_{2}}
.

Proof.

The proof of
(i)-(iii)
can be found in
[
54
, Chapter 3]
or adapted from
[
52
, Section 2.3]
.
To prove
(iv)
, consider
x\not\in B+\supp\nu
. Then
x-y\not\in\supp\nu
for all
y\in B
. In other words,
\nu(x-y)=0
for almost all
y\in B.
Whence,
\overline{\nu}_{B}(x)=\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x)=0
.
(v)
is blatantly obvious.
∎

The weights
\widehat{\nu}_{R}
,
\widetilde{\nu}_{B}
and
\overline{\nu}_{B}
turn out to be comparable when
\nu
is unimodal and satisfies the doubling scaling condition at infinity (
5.2
).

Definition 5.4
.

We say that a radial kernel
\nu
satisfies a doubling condition at infinity if:

\displaystyle\forall\,\,\theta\geq 1\text{ there exist }c_{1},c_{2}>0\text{ with }c_{1}\nu(r)\leq\nu(\theta r)\leq c_{2}\nu(r)\,\,\forall\,\,r\geq 1\,.

(5.1)

Not that the property (
5.1
) is indeed equivalent to say that

\displaystyle\text{ There exist }c_{1},c_{2}>0\text{ with }c_{1}\nu(r)\leq\nu(2r)\leq c_{2}\nu(r)\text{ for all }r\geq 1.

(5.2)

The doubling condition is only relevant when the support of
\nu
is large enough.

Theorem 5.5
.

If
\nu
is unimodal and
B
is bounded, e.g. say
B\subset B_{R}(0)
then

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}_{R}C^{-1}(1\land\nu(2Rx))\leq\widehat{\nu}_{R}(x)\leq C(1\land\nu(x)),

\displaystyle C^{-1}\widehat{\nu}_{R}(x)\leq\overline{\nu}_{B}(x)\leq C\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x)\leq\sigma_{R}C(1\land\nu(\frac{x}{2})),

where
\sigma_{R}=(1+\nu^{-1}(2R))
and
\sigma^{*}_{R}=1\land\nu(2R)
.
In addition, if
\nu
is of full support and satisfies the doubling condition (
5.1
) then

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x)\asymp\overline{\nu}_{B}(x)\asymp\widehat{\nu}_{R}(x)\asymp 1\land\nu(x).

Here the constant
C>0
and the constants behind the relation
\asymp
are generic and depend on
B
,
R
and
\nu
. Moreover, one notes that
\sigma^{*}_{R}=\sigma^{-1}_{R}=0
if
\nu(2R)=0
.

Proof.

Let us fix
x\in\R^{d}
. Since
R\leq R(1+|x|)
and
|x|\leq R(1+|x|)
by unimodality,

\widehat{\nu}_{R}(x)\leq C1\land\nu(x)\quad
with
\quad C=c(1+\nu(R))
.

For
|x|\leq 1
we have
R(1+|x|)\leq 2R
, the unimodality implies

c\widehat{\nu}_{R}(x)\geq\nu(2R)\geq\nu(2R)(1\land\nu(2Rx))\geq\sigma^{*}_{R}(1\land\nu(2Rx)).

For
|x|\geq 1
we have
R(1+|x|)\leq 2R|x|
the unimodality implies
c\widehat{\nu}_{R}(x)\geq\nu(2Rx)\geq\sigma^{*}_{R}(1\land\nu(2Rx)).

In any case, taking
C=c(1+\nu(R))
, we find that

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}_{R}C^{-1}(1\land\nu(2Rx))\leq\widehat{\nu}_{R}(x)\leq C(1\land\nu(x)).

Next, according to Proposition
5.3

\overline{\nu}_{B}
is bounded, thus by definition of
\overline{\nu}_{B}

\displaystyle\overline{\nu}_{B}(x)\leq(1+\|\overline{\nu}_{B}\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})})(1\land\nu(x-y))\quad\text{for almost all $\,y\in B$}

whence letting
C=|B|^{-1}(1+\|\overline{\nu}_{B}\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})})
we find that

\displaystyle\overline{\nu}_{B}(x)\leq C\int_{B}1\land\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y=C\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x).

We find that
|x-y|\leq R(1+|x|)
for
y\in B
, the unimodality implies
\widehat{\nu}_{R}(x)\leq c\nu(x-y)
. Hence we get

\displaystyle c^{-1}\widehat{\nu}_{R}(x)\leq\essinf_{y\in B}\nu(x-y)=\overline{\nu}_{B}(x).

The second claim is proved. Now, for
\frac{|x|}{2}\leq 2R
the unimodality implies
1\leq c\nu^{-1}(2R)\nu(\frac{x}{2})\leq c\sigma_{R}\nu(\frac{x}{2})
. Since by definition of
\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x)
we have
\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x)\leq\|1\land\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x)\leq\sigma_{R}(c+1)\|1\land\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}(1\land\nu(\frac{x}{2})).

If
\frac{|x|}{2}\geq 2R
then
\frac{|x|}{2}\leq|x-y|
for all
y\in B\subset B_{R}(0)
. By unimodality we get
\nu(x-y)\leq c\nu(\frac{x}{2})
which implies
1\land\nu(x-y)\leq(c+1)1\land\nu(\frac{x}{2})
for all
y\in B
. Thence we get

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x)=\int_{B}1\land\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\leq\sigma_{R}(c+1)|B|(1\land\nu(\frac{x}{2})).

In any case, taking
C=(c+1)(|B|+\|1\land\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})})
we find that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x)\leq\sigma_{R}C(1\land\nu(\frac{x}{2})).

Last, assume that
\nu
has full support and satisfies the doubling condition (
5.1
). Given the previous estimates, it is sufficient to show that
1\land\nu(\frac{x}{2})\asymp 1\land\nu(x)\asymp 1\land\nu(2Rx)
. For
|x|\leq 2
then unimodality implies

c\nu(2Rx)\geq\nu(2R)\geq\nu(2R)(1\land\nu(x))
. Likewise,
c\nu(x)\geq\nu(2)(1\land\nu(\frac{x}{2}))
. We deduce that

\displaystyle(1+c\nu^{-1}(2))^{-1}1\land\nu(\frac{x}{2})\leq 1\land\nu(x)\leq(1+c\nu^{-1}(2R))1\land\nu(2Rx).

For
|x|\geq 2
, the doubling condition (
5.1
) implies
c_{1}\nu(\frac{x}{2})\leq\nu(x)\leq c_{2}\nu(2Rx)
, so that

\displaystyle(c_{1}^{-1}+1)^{-1}1\land\nu(\frac{x}{2})\leq 1\land\nu(x)\leq(c_{2}+1)1\land\nu(2Rx).

Finally we put
C=\max(c_{1}^{-1}+1,c_{2}+1,1+c\nu^{-1}(2R),1+c\nu^{-1}(2))
so that
0<C<\infty
since
\nu
has full support and hence we get

\displaystyle C^{-1}(1\land\nu(\frac{x}{2}))\leq 1\land\nu(x)\leq C(1\land\nu(2Rx).

This achieves the proof.
∎

Example 5.6
.

Consider
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
,
s\in(0,1)
then
\widetilde{\nu}\asymp 1\land\nu
. In this case one can take
\widetilde{\nu}(h)=(1+|h|)^{-d-sp}
.
In this case, the space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
equals the classical Sobolev-Slobodeckij space
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
. For the same choice of
\nu
we define
W^{s,p}(\Omega|\R^{d})
as the space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.

It is possible to define other interesting weighted norms on
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
which are equivalent to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Assume that
B\subset\Omega
where
B
is bounded, say
B\subset B_{R}(0)
.
Let
\omega\in\{\widetilde{\nu}_{B},\overline{\nu}_{B},\widehat{\nu}_{R}\}
. If
\nu
is unimodal then the following seminorms
\|\cdot\|^{\#}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
and
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
are equivalent.

\displaystyle\|u\|^{*p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)|^{p}\omega(x)\mathrm{d}x+\hskip-4.30554pt\iint\limits\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}\hskip-4.30554pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle\|u\|^{\#p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\omega(x)\mathrm{d}x+\hskip-4.30554pt\iint\limits\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}\hskip-4.30554pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

Furthermore, the seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
and
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
are also equivalent provided that
\Omega
is bounded.

Remark 5.8
.

It is noteworthy to emphasize that the seminorms
