\|\cdot\|^{\#}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
,
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
and
\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
are norms on
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
whenever the condition (

S
1

) holds true; cf Theorem
3.9
. In particular, if
\nu
is of full support.

Proof.

In virtue of Theorem
5.5
we have

C^{-1}\widehat{\nu}_{R}(x)\leq\omega(x)\leq C\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x)
for some
C>0
.
Whence, since
B
is bounded and
\nu
is of full support, the unimodality implies that there is
c^{\prime}>0
such that
\omega(x)\geq c^{\prime}
for almost all
x\in B
. This together with the fact that

B\subset\Omega
and
\int_{\Omega^{c}}(1\land\nu(x-y))\mathrm{d}y\leq\|1\land\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}
implies

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}

\displaystyle|u(x)|^{p}\omega(x)\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq c^{\prime}\int_{B}|u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq c^{\prime}\|1\land\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}^{-1}\iint\limits_{B\Omega^{c}}|u(x)|^{p}(1\land\nu(x-y))\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\iint\limits_{B\Omega^{c}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq(1\land c^{\prime}\|1\land\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}^{-1})\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}B}\Big[|u(x)|^{p}+|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\Big]1\land\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

Using
b^{p}\geq 2^{1-p}(a+b)^{p}-a^{p}
,
a,b\geq 0
and
\omega(y)\leq C\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(y)
we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}

\displaystyle\omega(x)\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\geq C_{1}\int_{\Omega^{c}}|u(y)|^{p}\omega(y)\mathrm{d}y\,

with
C_{1}=\frac{C^{-1}}{2^{p-1}}(1\land c^{\prime}\|1\land\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}^{-1})
. This clearly implies that
\|u\|^{*}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq C\|u\|^{\#}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
for some
C>0
. Thus,
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
and
\|\cdot\|^{\#}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
are equivalent.
Now if
\Omega
is bounded, the unimodality and the boundedness of
\omega
(see Proposition
5.3
) yield
\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}\geq\omega(x)\geq c^{\prime}
,
x\in\Omega
for some
c^{\prime}>0
. The equivalence of
\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
and
\|\cdot\|^{\#}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
readily follows.
∎

The next result shows that functions in
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
possess certain weighted integrability.

Theorem 5.9
.

Assume
B\subset\Omega
and
R\geq 1
. Let
\omega\in\{\widetilde{\nu}_{B},\overline{\nu}_{B},\widehat{\nu}_{R}\}
.

(1)

For
\omega\in\{\widetilde{\nu}_{B},\overline{\nu}_{B}\}
we have the continuous embedding

W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\R^{d},\omega).

The same holds for
\omega=\widehat{\nu}_{R}
if in addition
|B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega|>0
.

(2)

For
\omega\in\{\overline{\nu}_{B},\widehat{\nu}_{R}\}
we have the continuous embeddings

\displaystyle L^{p}(\R^{d},\omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p-1}(\R^{d},\omega)\cap L^{1}(\R^{d},\omega)

(5.3)

equivalently for any
q\in[\min(1,p-1),p]
we have

\displaystyle L^{p}(\R^{d},\omega)\hookrightarrow L^{q}(\R^{d},\omega)\hookrightarrow L^{\min(1,p-1)}(\R^{d},\omega).

(5.4)

The same holds for
\omega=\widetilde{\nu}_{B}
if in addition
|B|<\infty
.

Proof.

(i)
The embedding

W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\R^{d},\widetilde{\nu}_{B})
is obtained as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)|^{p}\widetilde{\nu}_{B}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\int_{B}|u(y)|^{p}\int_{\R^{d}}1\land\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+2^{p-1}\iint\limits_{B\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}1\land\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\Omega}|u(y)|^{p}\mathrm{d}y+C\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=C\|u\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}.

Here
C=2^{p-1}(1+\|1\land\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})})
. To prove

W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\R^{d},\overline{\nu}_{B})
, let us consider
K^{\prime}\subset B\subset\Omega
be a measurable such that
0<|K^{\prime}|<\infty
. For all
y\in K^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}\hskip-4.30554pt|u(x)|^{p}\overline{\nu}_{B}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\big(\|\overline{\nu}_{B}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}|u(y)|^{p}+\hskip-4.30554pt\int_{\R^{d}}\hskip-8.61108pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}x\big).

Integrating over
K^{\prime}
we find that

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)|^{p}\overline{\nu}_{B}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{K^{\prime}}|u(y)|^{p}\mathrm{d}y+C\iint\limits_{K^{\prime}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\|u\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}.

where
C=2^{p-1}|K^{\prime}|^{-1}(\|\overline{\nu}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}+1).

Finally, if
|B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega|>0
, consider
B^{\prime}=B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega
so that
B^{\prime}\subset B_{R}(0)
. Since for the definition of
\widehat{\nu}_{R}
,
\nu
is unimodal, by Theorem
5.5

there is
C=C(B^{\prime},R)>0
such that
\widehat{\nu}_{R}(x)\leq C\overline{\nu}_{B^{\prime}}(x)
.
Thus, we deduce from the previous case that

W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\R^{d},\overline{\nu}_{B^{\prime}})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\R^{d},\widehat{\nu}_{R}).

(ii)
For
\omega\in\{\widetilde{\nu}_{B},\overline{\nu}_{B},\widehat{\nu}_{R}\}
, Proposition
5.3
implies
\omega\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
. Thus Hölder’s inequality implies
\|u\|_{L^{r}(\R^{d},\omega)}\leq\|\omega\|^{1/r-1/q}_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\|u\|_{L^{q}(\R^{d},\omega)}
whenever
q\geq r>0
. The desired embeddings immediately follow.
∎

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
\omega\in\{\widetilde{\nu}_{B},\overline{\nu}_{B},\widehat{\nu}_{R}\}
where
B\subset\Omega
. Assume
|B|<\infty
and
|B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega|>0
,
R\geq 1
.
The following embeddings are continuous

\displaystyle W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\R^{d},\omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p-1}(\R^{d},\omega)\cap L^{1}(\R^{d},\omega).

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of Theorem
5.9
.
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

Assume
\nu
is unimodal with full support and satisfies the doubling condition (
5.1
). The following embeddings are continuous

\displaystyle W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\R^{d},1\land\nu)\hookrightarrow L^{p-1}(\R^{d},1\land\nu)\cap L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land\nu).

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Corollary
5.10
and Theorem
5.5
.
∎

In the fractional setting
L^{p-1}(\R^{d},\omega)
is sometimes called the tail space.

Corollary 5.12
.

Let
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
with
s\in(0,1)
, we put
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})=W^{s,p}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. The following embeddings are continuous

\displaystyle W^{s,p}(\Omega|\R^{d})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\R^{d},(1+|h|)^{-d-sp}))

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow L^{p-1}(\R^{d},(1+|h|)^{-d-sp}))\cap L^{1}(\R^{d},(1+|h|)^{-d-sp})).

Proof.

This follows from Corollary
5.11
since
1\land\nu(h)\asymp(1+|h|)^{-d-sp}
.
∎

5.2.
Nonlocal , trace space of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})

The main goal of this part is to introduce an abstract notion trace space of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
similarly as one does for the space
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Due to its nonlocal character, the nonlocal trace space of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
assumes functions defined on
\R^{d}\setminus\Omega
, or strictly speaking on the nonlocal boundary
\Omega_{e}=\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
of
\Omega
with respect to
\nu
. The main reason is that elements of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
are essentially defined on
\R^{d}
, or strictly speaking on the nonlocal hull

\Omega_{\nu}^{*}=\Omega\cup\Omega_{\nu}
,
\Omega_{\nu}=\Omega+\supp\nu
(see Theorem
3.9
). This contrasts with the local situation, where the trace space of
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
(with
\Omega
smooth enough) are elements defined on the boundary
\partial\Omega
.

Definition 5.13

(Trace space of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
)
.

The trace space of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
denoted
T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
is the space of restrictions to
\R^{d}\setminus\Omega
of functions of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. More precisely,

\displaystyle T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})=\{v:\Omega^{c}\to\mathbb{R}\penalty\ \text{meas.}\penalty\ \hbox{ such that }\penalty\ v=u|_{\Omega^{c}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \hbox{with }\penalty\ \penalty\ u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\}.

We endow
T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
with its natural norm,

\displaystyle\|v\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}=\inf\{\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}:\penalty\ \penalty\ u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\penalty\ \penalty\ \hbox{ with }\penalty\ \penalty\ v=u|_{\Omega^{c}}\}.

If we identify
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\equiv W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
then we also identify
T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})\equiv T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega_{e})
where
\Omega_{e}=\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
and

\displaystyle T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega_{e})=\{v:\Omega_{e}\to\mathbb{R}\penalty\ \text{meas.}\penalty\ \penalty\ \hbox{ such that }\penalty\ \penalty\ v=u|_{\Omega_{e}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \hbox{with }\penalty\ \penalty\ u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})\}.

Theorem 5.14
.

If
(W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})})
is a Banach space then
(T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}),\|\cdot\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})})
is also a Banach space.

Proof.

Noting that
T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
and the quotient space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})/W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
are identical with equal norm in space and that
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is a closed subspace of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}),
one concludes that
T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
is complete.
Alternatively, for the reader’s convenience, we provide a detailed proof regarding the completeness of
T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
under the norm
\|\cdot\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
. Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a Cauchy sequence in
T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
then up to the extraction of a subsequence we may assume that

\displaystyle\|u_{n}-u_{n+1}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}<\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}\quad\hbox{for all $n\geq 1$}.

Fix
\overline{u}_{1}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
such that
u_{1}=\overline{u}_{1}|_{\Omega^{c}}
.
By definition of
\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
there exists
v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
such that
u_{1}-u_{2}=v|_{\Omega^{c}}
and

\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}<\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}+\frac{1}{4}\,.

Letting
\overline{u}_{2}=v+\overline{u}_{1}
i.e.
v=\overline{u}_{1}-\overline{u}_{2}
yields
\overline{u}_{2}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
,
u_{2}=\overline{u}_{2}|_{\Omega^{c}}
and hence

\|\overline{u}_{1}-\overline{u}_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}<\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}+\frac{1}{4}<\frac{1}{2}\,.
Repeating this process one constructs a sequence
\overline{u}_{n}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
such that

\displaystyle\|\overline{u}_{n}-\overline{u}_{n+1}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}<\frac{1}{2^{n}}\qquad\hbox{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ n\geq 1

which turns out to be a Cauchy sequence in the complete space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.
Let
\overline{u}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
be the limit of
(\overline{u}_{n})_{n}
.
Clearly, setting
u=\overline{u}\mid_{\Omega^{c}}
we have

\|u_{n}-u\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\leq\|\overline{u}_{n}-\overline{u}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Finally, the original Cauchy sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
converges up to the extraction of subsequence to
u
and hence converges itself to
u
.
∎

Let us state the nonlocal trace theorem for the trace space
T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
.

Theorem 5.15

(Nonlocal trace theorem)
.

Let
\omega\in\{\widetilde{\nu}_{\Omega},\overline{\nu}_{\Omega},\widehat{\nu}_{R}\}
where
|B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega|>0

(
see Definition
5.2
)
.
Define the trace operator
u\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}(u)=u\mid_{\Omega^{c}}
. The following assertions hold.

(1)

\ker(\operatorname{Tr})=W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and
\operatorname{Tr}(W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}))=T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
.

(2)

The mappings
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}}T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Id}}L^{p}(\Omega^{c},\omega)

are continuous.

(3)

If
\Omega
is bounded in one direction (see Section
7
), then there is a bounded and continuous mapping
\operatorname{Ext}:T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})\to W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
with
\operatorname{Ext}\circ\operatorname{Tr}=Id
.

Proof.

It is easy to check that
\operatorname{Tr}(W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}))=T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
and
\ker(\operatorname{Tr})=W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.
It immediately follows from the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
that

\|\operatorname{Tr}(u)\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\leq\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}
for all
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
whereas, by Theorem
5.9
there exists
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\operatorname{Tr}(u)\|_{L^{p}(\Omega^{c},\omega)}\leq\|u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d},\omega)}\leq C\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\qquad\text{for all }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}).

Inasmuch as the above estimate is true for all
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
, we deduce
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega^{c},\omega)}\leq C\|u\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
,

u\in T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
. Last, for
g\in T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
consider
\operatorname{Ext}g=u_{g}
where by Theorem
8.18

u_{g}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is the unique weak solution to the Dirichlet problem
Lu=0
in
\Omega
and
u=g
in
\R^{d}\setminus\Omega
. The operator
\operatorname{Ext}
is well-defined and
\operatorname{Ext}\circ\operatorname{Tr}=Id
. The
continuity and the boundedness follow from Theorem
8.18
.
∎

One may view the objects
L^{p}(\Omega^{c},\omega)
,
T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
,
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
respectively as the nonlocal counterpart of the objects
L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
,
W^{1-1/p,p}(\partial\Omega)
,
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and
W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
. Let us recall the classical trace theorem.

Theorem 5.16

(Classical trace theorem, see
[
17
, Chap III]
)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is bounded Lipschitz. There exists a linear and continuous trace operator
\gamma_{0}:W^{1,p}(\Omega)\to L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
such that

\gamma_{0}u=u|_{\partial\Omega}
for all
u\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})\cap W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Moreover,
\ker(\gamma_{0})=W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
and, by definition,
\gamma_{0}(W^{1,p}(\Omega))=W^{1,1-1/p}(\partial\Omega)
.

Remark 5.17
.

Let us emphasize that our nonlocal trace operator
\operatorname{Tr}
does not need any special construction via
functional analysis and density argument. Since
\Omega^{c}
is still a d-dimensional manifold. Then it makes sense to consider hardcore restriction of functions on
\Omega^{c}.

Moreover, no regularity on
\Omega
is required nor on
u
. Whereas in the local situation (see Theorem
5.16
), the trace of a Sobolev function
u
on the boundary
\partial\Omega
requires the smoothness of both
u
and
\partial\Omega
.

It is natural to ask the following question: Can the space
T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
be self-defined with an intrinsic norm preserving its initial Banach structure in a way that its trivial connection to
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is less seeable?
In the local situation, it is possible to define a scalar product on the space
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
when
\Omega
is a special Lipschitz domain (see
[
35
]
). This question is discussed in
[
52
,
60
]
when
p=2
, using the main result from
[
13
]
.
Another treatment of nonlocal trace operator for the special fractional kernel
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
is encapsulated in
[
44
, Theorem 3]
.

6.
Compact embeddings

In this section we prove compact embeddings of the spaces
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
,
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and
W_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
into
L^{p}(\Omega)
. Our result on global compactness Theorem
6.6
requires some extra regularity assumptions on
\Omega
compatible with
\nu
. We exploit some recent ideas from
[
69
,
40
]
. However, extended details and discussions for the global compactness can be found in
[
54
,
52
]
.

6.1.
Local and global compactness results

Given a Banach space
X
, we say that an operator
T:X\to L^{p}_{\loc}(\Omega)
is compact if for any compact set
K\subset\Omega
, the operator

R_{K}T:X\to L^{p}_{\loc}(K)
,
R_{K}Tu=Tu|_{K}
is compact.

Lemma 6.1

(
[
23
, Corollary 4.28]
)
.

Let
w\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
. The convolution operator
T_{w}:L^{p}(\R^{d})\to L^{p}(\R^{d})
,
T_{w}u=w*u
is continuous with
\|T_{w}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\R^{d}),L^{p}(\R^{d}))}\leq\|w\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}
. Moreover,
R_{K}T_{w}:L^{p}(\R^{d})\to L^{p}(K)
is compact for any measurable set
K\subset\R^{d}
with
|K|<\infty
.

We now present the characterization of the local compactness result that will be used in the sequel.

Theorem 6.2
.

If
\nu
satisfies (
L
), then the following assertions are equivalent.

(1)

\nu
is not integrable, i.e.,
\nu\not\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
.

(2)

The embedding
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(K)
is compact for any measurable set with
|K|<\infty
.

(3)

The embedding
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}_{\loc}(\R^{d})
is compact.

(4)

The embedding
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(B(0,1))
is compact.

Proof.

We only prove
(iv)\implies(i)
and
(i)\implies(ii)
as the other implications are straightforward. Assume
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
and put
B=B(0,1)
then
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})=L^{2}(\R^{d})
and hence the mappings
L^{p}(B)\xrightarrow{E}W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})\xrightarrow{R_{B}}L^{p}(B)
, where
Eu=\overline{u}
is the zero extension of
u
and
R_{B}u=u|_{B}
, are continuous. Since is the identity map
I=R_{B}\circ E:L^{p}(B)\to L^{p}(B)
is not compact, necessarily,
R
is not compact.
Now assume
\nu\not\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
and
\delta>0
sufficiently small such that
0<\|\nu_{\delta}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. Consider
w_{\delta}(h)=\nu_{\delta}(h)\|\nu_{\delta}\|^{-1}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
so that
\|w_{\delta}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=1
. Define
T_{w_{\delta}}=w_{\delta}*u
,
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By the symmetry of
\nu
we have,

\displaystyle T_{w_{\delta}}u(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}w_{\delta}(y)u(x-y)\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}w_{\delta}(y)u(x+y)\mathrm{d}y\,.

Meanwhile, Jensen’s inequality implies

\displaystyle\|u-

\displaystyle T_{w_{\delta}}u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[u(x)-u(x+h)]w_{\delta}(h)\mathrm{d}\,h\Big|^{p}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\|\nu_{\delta}\|^{-1}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u(x)-u(x+h)|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}\,h\mathrm{d}x\leq\|\nu_{\delta}\|^{-1}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})}\,.

Thus, since
\nu\not\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
, for a subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|K|<\infty
we find that

\displaystyle\|R_{K}-R_{K}T_{w_{\delta}}\|_{\mathcal{L}\big(W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d}),\,L^{p}(K)\big)}\leq\|\nu_{\delta}\|^{-1/p}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{\delta\to 0}0\,.

It follows that the operator
R_{K}:W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})\to L^{p}(K)
with
R_{K}u=u|_{K}
is compact since each operator
R_{K}T_{w_{\delta}}
is compact (by Lemma
6.1
) and the set of compact operator is closed.
∎

Another version of Theorem
6.2
is proved in
[
69
, Theorem 1.1]
for the case
p=2
and also in
[
29
, Theorem 2.1]
under restrictive assumptions on the kernel
\nu
, using the Pego criterion for compact compactness in
L^{2}(\R^{d})
. It is worth mentioning that earlier analogous results are provided in
[
83
, Proposition 6]
and
[
21
, Proposition 1]
for periodic functions on the torus. This technique of killing the singularity is also used in
[
8
, Lemma 3.1]
.

Corollary 6.3
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open and
\nu\not\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
. The embedding
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}_{\loc}(\Omega)
is compact.
Moreover, if
|\Omega|<\infty
then the embedding
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
, is compact.

Proof.

For fixed
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
the operator
J_{\varphi}:W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)\to W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
,
J_{\varphi}u=u\varphi
is continuous. By Theorem
6.2

the embedding
W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(K)
,
K=\supp\varphi\subset\Omega
, is compact.
It follows that the embedding
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(K)
is compact. Hence the first claim is proved.
The embeddings
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
are continuous and, by Theorem
6.2
, the last one is compact when
|\Omega|<\infty
. Whence the second claim follows.
∎

Another consequence of Theorem
6.2
is the following local compactness.

Corollary 6.4
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open and
\nu\not\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
.
The following assertions hold.

(1)

Any bounded sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
in
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
admits a subsequence
(u_{n_{j}})_{j}
that converges in
L^{p}_{\loc}(\Omega)
to some
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
. Moreover,

\displaystyle|u|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}|u_{n}|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)}.

(2)

Assume
|\Omega|<\infty
then for a bounded sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
in
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
there exists
u\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and subsequence
(u_{n_{j}})_{j}
converging to
u
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
. Moreover,

\displaystyle|u|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}|u_{n}|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})}.

6.2.
Global compactness.

The global compactness requires some compatibility between the singularity of the kernel
\nu
and the regularity of the
boundary
\partial\Omega
. Let us introduce conditions on
\Omega
and
\nu
yielding the global compactness result.

Definition 6.5
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open bounded,
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
and
\nu\not\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
.
We say that
(\nu,\Omega)
is in the class
\mathscr{A}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, if in addition

(
\mathbf{\mathscr{A}_{1}}
)

\ldots
there exists an
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
-extension operator
E:W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)\to W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})
, i.e., there is
C(\nu,\Omega,d)>0
such that for every
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
,
\|u\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\R^{d})}\leq C\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)}
and
Eu|_{\Omega}=u
.

(
\mathbf{\mathscr{A}_{2}}
)

\ldots

\Omega
has Lipschitz boundary,
\nu
is radial and
q(\delta)\xrightarrow{\delta\to 0}\infty
where

\displaystyle q(\delta):=\frac{1}{\delta^{p}}\int\limits_{B_{\delta}(0)}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\,.

(6.1)

(
\mathbf{\mathscr{A}_{3}}
)

\ldots
the following condition holds true:
\widetilde{q}(\delta)\xrightarrow{\delta\to 0}\infty
where

\displaystyle\widetilde{q}(\delta):=\inf_{a\in\partial\Omega}\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}\nu(h-a)\mathrm{d}h

(6.2)

with
\Omega_{\delta}=\{x\in\Omega:\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)>\delta\}
.

Note that the assumption
\nu\not\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
here is redundant. Indeed, having
q(\delta)\xrightarrow{\delta\to 0}\infty

or
\widetilde{q}(\delta)\xrightarrow{\delta\to 0}\infty
implies
\nu\not\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
. The converse is not true, e.g., for
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d}\mathds{1}_{B_{1}(0)}
we have
\nu\not\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
but
q(\delta)=c_{d}.
For the same example and
\Omega=B_{1}(0)
we have also
\widetilde{q}(\delta)=c_{d}.

Here is our global compactness result; see
[
52
]
for the case
p=2
or
[
54
, Theorem 3.89]
.

Theorem 6.6
.

If the couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the class
\mathscr{A}_{i},\penalty\ i=1,2,3
then the embedding
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact. In particular, the embedding
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact.

Remark 6.7
.

The Rellich-Kondrachov’s compact embeddings
W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
and
W^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
when
\Omega
is Lipschitz, respectively can be derived from Theorem
6.2
combined with the embedding
W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and from Theorem
6.6
combined with the embedding
W^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
when
\Omega
is Lipschitz.

7.
Nonlocal Poincaré type inequalities

7.1.
Nonlocal Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality

In this section
1\leq p<\infty
,
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open and that
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty)
is symmetric satisfying
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d}\setminus B(0,r))
for every
r>0
. In particular, the latter holds true if
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{\gamma}\mathrm{d}h)
for any
\gamma\geq 0
.
Let us define the space
L^{p}_{\Omega}(\R^{d})
by

\displaystyle L^{p}_{\Omega}(\R^{d})=\{u\in L^{p}(\R^{d}):\,\text{$u=0$ a.e. on $\Omega^{c}$}\}.

Next, we use a more refined argument to prove the above Poincaré-Friedrichs type inequalities with strictly positive constants, in a general setting only by assuming that
\nu
is nontrivial that is,
\nu\not\equiv 0
. We follow the strategy from
[
69
]
using the
2^{m}
-folded convolutions which is also used in
[
47
, proof of Lemma 2.7]
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
q\in L^{1}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
with
q\geq 0
be symmetric, i.e.,
q(h)=q(-h)
and nontrivial, i.e.,
\big|\{q>0\}\big|>0
. Define
2^{m}
-fold convolution of
q
as follows
q_{0}=q
,
q_{m}=q_{m-1}*q_{m-1}=\underbrace{q*\cdots*q}_{2^{m}-\text{times}}
. For every
m\geq 1
, the following assertions are true.

(1)

Each
q_{m}
belongs to
L^{1}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
and is uniformly continuous.

(2)

There exists
\delta>0
, such that
\inf_{B_{\delta_{m}}(0)}q_{m}>0
with

\delta_{m}=(\frac{7}{4})^{m}\delta
so that
\delta_{m}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}\infty
.

(3)

For
u:\R^{d}\to\R
measurable, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{m}(u)\leq B_{m}\mathcal{Q}(u),\qquad B_{m}=2^{pm}\|q\|^{2^{m}-1}_{L^{1}(\R^{d})},

(7.1)

where we set
\mathcal{Q}(u)=\mathcal{Q}_{0}(u)
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{m}(u):=\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}q_{m}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.

(i) Since
q_{m}=q_{m-1}*q_{m-1}
, by the Young’s inequalities
q_{m}\in L^{1}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
and

\displaystyle\|q_{m}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\leq\|q_{m-1}\|^{2}_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\leq\|q\|^{2^{m}}_{L^{1}(\R^{d})},

(7.2)

\displaystyle\|q_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}\leq\|q_{m-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}\|q_{m-1}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\leq\|q\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}\|q\|^{2^{m}-1}_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}.

(7.3)

The uniform continuity
q_{m}
follows from the continuity of the shift in
L^{1}(\R^{d})
.

(ii) Since
q
is not identically vanishing, we have

\displaystyle q_{1}(0)=q*q(0)=\int_{\R^{d}}|q(h)|^{2}\mathrm{d}h>0

wherefrom, the continuity of
q_{1}
at
0
implies that
\theta=\inf_{B_{\delta}(0)}q_{1}>0
for some
\delta>0
. We claim that
q_{2}(x)=q_{1}*q_{1}(x)>0
whenever
|x|\leq\frac{7\delta}{4}
. Indeed, note that
B_{\frac{\delta}{16}}(\frac{x}{2})\subset B_{\delta}(0)\cap B_{\delta}(x)
since for
z\in B_{\frac{\delta}{16}}(\frac{x}{2})
we have

\displaystyle\big|z-\frac{x}{2}\pm\frac{x}{2}\big|\leq\big|z-\frac{x}{2}\big|+\big|\frac{x}{2}\big|\leq\frac{15\delta}{16}<\delta.

Given that
q_{1}(h)q_{1}(x-h)>\theta^{2}
for all
h\in B_{\delta}(0)\cap B_{\delta}(x)
we finally get

\displaystyle q_{2}(x)=q_{1}*q_{1}(x)\geq\theta^{2}|B_{\frac{\delta}{16}}(0)|>0\quad\text{for all $|x|\leq\frac{7\delta}{4}$}.

Repeating this process, one finds that for
\theta_{m-1}=\inf_{B_{\delta_{m-1}}(0)}q_{m-1}(x)>0

\displaystyle q_{m}(x)\geq\theta^{2}_{m-1}|B_{\frac{\delta_{m-1}}{16}}(0)|>0\quad\text{for all $|x|\leq\delta_{m}.$}

(iii) The inequality
|a+b|^{p}\leq 2^{p-1}(|a|^{p}+|b|^{p})
and Fubini’s theorem yield

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{m}(u)

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(x+h)|^{p}\int_{\R^{d}}q_{m-1}(z)q_{m-1}(h-z)\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(x+z)|^{p}\int_{\R^{d}}q_{m-1}(z)q_{m-1}(h-z)\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+2^{p-1}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x+z)-u(x+h)|^{p}\int_{\R^{d}}q_{m-1}(z)q_{m-1}(h-z)\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=2^{p-1}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(x+z)|^{p}\Big(\int_{\R^{d}}q_{m-1}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\Big)q_{m-1}(z)\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\overset{\xi=h-z}{+}2^{p-1}\int_{\R^{d}}q_{m-1}(z)\mathrm{d}z\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x+z)-u(x+z+\xi)|^{p}q_{m-1}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=2^{p}\|q_{m-1}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\mathcal{Q}_{m-1}(u).

In short, this combined with inequality (
7.2
) implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{m}(u)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\|q_{m-1}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\mathcal{Q}_{m-1}(u)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\prod_{k=1}^{m}2^{p}\|q\|^{2^{k-1}}_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\Big)\mathcal{Q}(u)=2^{pm}\|q\|^{2^{m}-1}_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\mathcal{Q}(u).

∎

In the sequel, it is decisive to keep in mind that for
u\in L^{p}_{\Omega}(\R^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,u)

\displaystyle=\hskip-12.91663pt\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\hskip-8.61108pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x=\hskip-4.30554pt\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\hskip-4.30554pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

Theorem 7.2

(Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality I)
.

Assume that
\nu\not\equiv 0
, i.e.,
\big|\{\nu>0\}\big|>0

and that
\Omega
is bounded one direction, i.e., there exist
R>0
and
e\in\R^{d}
,
|e|=1
such that
\Omega\subset H_{R}
with
H_{R}=\{z\in\R^{d}:|z\cdot e|\leq R\}
. Then for
m\in\mathbb{N}
large, there is
0<C_{R,m}:=C(d,p,R,m,\nu)<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C_{R,m}\mathcal{E}(u,u)\,\qquad\text{for all $u\in L^{p}_{\Omega}(\R^{d})$}.

(7.4)

Moreover, letting
q_{m}=q_{m-1}*q_{m-1}
,
q_{0}=q
,
q:=1\land\nu
we can choose

\displaystyle C_{R,m}=2^{pm}\|q\|^{2^{m}-1}_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\Big(\int_{H^{c}_{2R}}q_{m}(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)^{-1}.

Proof.

We have
H_{R}\subset H_{2R}(x)=\{z\in\R^{d}:|(z-x)\cdot e|\leq 2R\}
for
x\in H_{R}
.
Accordingly, for
u\in L^{p}_{\Omega}(\R^{d})
there holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{m}(u)

\displaystyle\geq 2\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\int_{H_{R}^{c}}q_{m}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\int_{H_{2R}^{c}(x)}q_{m}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y=\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\int_{H_{2R}^{c}}q_{m}(h)\mathrm{d}h.

The fact that
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d}\setminus B(0,r))
implies
q=1\land\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
. In addition,
\nu\equiv 0
if and only if
q\equiv 0
. Thus,
q=1\land\nu\leq\nu
, the estimate above and Lemma
7.1
(iii) yield

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)\geq\mathcal{Q}(u)\geq B^{-1}_{m}\mathcal{Q}_{m}(u)\geq C^{-1}_{R,m}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

According to Lemma
7.1
(i),
q_{m}\in L^{1}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
and by Lemma
7.1
(ii) for
m
large we have
\inf_{B_{\delta_{m}}(0)}q_{m}>0
and
|B_{\delta_{m}}(0)\setminus H^{c}_{2R}|>0
where we recall
\delta_{m}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}\infty
. Hence

\displaystyle 0<|B_{\delta_{m}}(0)\setminus H^{c}_{2R}|\big(\inf_{B_{\delta_{m}}(0)}q_{m}\big)\leq\int_{H^{c}_{2R}}q_{m}(h)\mathrm{d}h\leq\|q_{m}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}<\infty,

wherefrom we deduce that
0<C_{R,m}<\infty
.
∎

Next, we want to consider the case where
|\Omega|<\infty
. We need the following Lemma from
[
51
]
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
E\subset\R^{d}
be measurable with
|E|<\infty
. Then we have

\displaystyle\essinf_{x\in\R^{d}}\int_{E^{c}}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\geq\nu^{\#}(|E|),\quad\text{with}\quad\nu^{\#}(|E|)=\int_{\{\nu<\nu^{*}(r_{E})\}}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h,

(7.5)

where
r_{E}=\big(\frac{|E|}{|B_{1}(0)|}\big)^{1/d}
and
\nu^{*}
is the symmetric rearrangement of
\nu

\displaystyle\nu^{*}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{\{|\nu|>s\}^{*}}(x)\mathrm{d}s=\inf\{s>0:\penalty\ |\{|\nu|>s\}|\leq c_{d}|x|^{d}\}.

(7.6)

It is worth noting that when
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp},

h\neq 0
we have
\nu^{*}=\nu
and

\displaystyle\nu^{\#}(|E|)=\int_{\{\nu<\nu^{*}(r_{E})\}}\hskip-12.91663pt\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h=\int_{|h|>r_{E}}\hskip-12.91663pt|h|^{-d-sp}\mathrm{d}h=\frac{1}{sp}\Big(\frac{|E|}{|B_{1}(0)|}\Big)^{-sp}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|.

Accordingly, Lemma
7.3
implies the following; see also
[
34
, Lemma 6.1]
,

\displaystyle\essinf_{x\in\R^{d}}\int_{E^{c}}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\geq\frac{1}{sp}\Big(\frac{|E|}{|B_{1}(0)|}\Big)^{-sp}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|.

Theorem 7.4

(Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality II)
.

Assume
\nu\not\equiv 0
, i.e.,
\big|\{\nu>0\}\big|>0
and
|\Omega|<\infty
. Then for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
the following inequality holds

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C^{\#}_{m}\mathcal{E}(u,u)\,\qquad\text{for all $u\in L^{p}_{\Omega}(\R^{d})$},

(7.7)

where, for sufficiently large
m
, we have
0<C^{\#}_{m}=C(d,p,|\Omega|,m,\nu)<\infty
with

\displaystyle C^{\#}_{m}=2^{pm}\|q\|^{2^{m}-1}_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\Big(q^{\#}_{m}(|\Omega|)\Big)^{-1}.

Here we recall
q_{m}=q_{m-1}*q_{m-1}
,
q_{0}=q
with
q:=1\land\nu=\min(1,\nu)
and
q_{m}^{\#}
is defined as in (
7.5
).

Proof.

Accordingly, for
u\in L^{p}_{\Omega}(\R^{d})
there holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{m}(u)

\displaystyle\geq 2\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\Omega^{c}}q_{m}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\geq\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}q^{\#}_{m}(|\Omega|).

The assumptions on
\nu
imply that
q=1\land\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
and
\nu\equiv 0
if and only if
q\equiv 0
. Moreover,
q=1\land\nu\leq\nu
, the above estimate and Lemma
7.1
(iii) yield

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)\geq\mathcal{Q}(u)\geq B^{-1}_{m}\mathcal{Q}_{m}(u)\geq(C^{\#}_{m})^{-1}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

By Lemma
7.1
(i),
q_{m}\in L^{1}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
and hence
q^{\#}_{m}(|\Omega|)<\infty
. By Lemma
7.1
(ii) we have
\delta_{m}>|\Omega|
for
m
large so that
q^{\#}_{m}(|\Omega|)\geq\inf_{B_{\delta_{m}}(0)}q_{m}>0
. Therefore,
0<C^{\#}_{m}<\infty
.
∎

7.2.
Nonlocal Poincaré inequality

In this section
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open and bounded,
1\leq p<\infty
and
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty)
is symmetric. Our goal in this section is to find some conditions on
\Omega
and
\nu
under which the following Poincaré inequality holds true, i.e., we can find a constant
C=C(d,p,\Omega,\nu)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)\,\quad\text{for all $u\in L^{p}(\Omega)$}.

(
P
)

Here and in what follows the notation
\mbox{$\fint_{E}$}u=\frac{1}{|E|}\int_{E}u(x)\mathrm{d}x
. So that since
\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)\leq\cE(u,u)
, (

P

) would imply

\displaystyle\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\cE(u,u)\,\quad\text{for all $u\in L^{p}(\Omega)$}.

(7.8)

We were unable to find a suitable reference of the following Lemma
7.5
, which is probably known in the literature. We however give a simple proof for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma 7.5

(Finite chain covering)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is connected. For every
r>0
there is a finite family of balls
(B_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
,
B_{i}=B(x_{i},r)
covering
\Omega
with
x_{i}\in\Omega
such that
B_{i-1}\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
,
i=2,3,\cdots,n
.

Proof.

It is readily seen that the balls
B(x,r)
,
x\in\Omega
covers the compact set
\overline{\Omega}
(the closure of
\Omega
). Thus, there is a finite sub-cover
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{i}\,:\,i\in\{1,2,\cdots,n\}\}
of
\Omega
with
B_{i}=B(x_{i},r),x_{i}\in\Omega
. Next, let us write
B_{i-1}\sim B_{i}
to indicate that
B_{i-1}\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
. Since
\Omega
is connected, there are
B,B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
such that
B\cap B^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
. Up to a relabeling, denote the chain
C_{2}=B_{1}\sim B_{2}
with
B_{1}=B,B_{2}=B^{\prime}
. Assume, up to relabeling the indices, there is a chain

\displaystyle C_{i}=B_{1}\sim B_{2}\sim\cdots\sim B_{i}.

Given that
\Omega
is connected, there is
B\in\mathcal{B}\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}B_{j}
such that
B\cap\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}B_{j}\neq\emptyset
. Thus, we can consider
j_{0}=\max\{j\in\{1,2,\cdots,i\}\,:B_{j}\sim B\}
and define a chain
C_{i+1}
as follows

\displaystyle C_{i+1}

\displaystyle=B_{1}\sim\cdots\sim B_{j_{0}-1}\sim B\sim B_{j_{0}}\sim\cdots\sim B_{i}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if $j_{0}\neq 1$},

\displaystyle C_{i+1}

\displaystyle=B\sim B_{1}\sim B_{2}\sim\cdots\cdots\sim B_{i}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if $j_{0}=1$}.

In this manner, up to relabeling the indices, one gets a chain
C_{n}
containing the whole
\mathcal{B}
.
∎

We also need to consider the following generalization of connected sets.

Definition 7.6
.

We say that

\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is
\rho-
connected,
\rho\geq 0
, if
\Omega=\Omega_{1}\cup\Omega_{2}\cup\cdots\cup\Omega_{m}
,
m\geq 1,
where each
\Omega_{i}
is open and connected such that
\dist(\Omega_{i},\Omega_{i+1})<\rho
,
i=1,2,\cdots,n-1
.

It is worthwhile to observe that every connected set is
0
-connected and the converse is not true. Consider
B_{\pm}=B_{1}(0)\cap\{x=(x^{\prime},x_{d}):\pm x_{d}>0\}
which are connected. Therefore, since
\dist(B_{-},B_{+})=0
we see that
\Omega=B_{-}\cup B_{+}
is
0
-connected
but not connected. Another observation is that if
\dist(\Omega_{i},\Omega_{i+1})<\rho
one finds
a_{i}\in\Omega_{i},b_{i}\in\Omega_{i+1}
such that
|a_{i}-b_{i}|<\rho
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Assume
\nu>0
a.e. on
B_{r}(0)
for some
r\in(0,\infty]
. Assume one of the following conditions holds.

(1)

r\geq\diam(\Omega)
.

(2)

\Omega
is connected.

(3)

\Omega
is
\rho
-connected with
0\leq\rho<r
.

There holds
\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)=0
if and only if
u=\lambda
a.e. in
\Omega
,
\lambda\in\R
.

Proof.

Clearly, if
u=\lambda
a.e. in
\Omega
then
\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)=0
. Conversely, if
\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)=0
, there is a null set
A\subset\Omega
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y=0\qquad\text{for all $x\in\Omega\setminus A$}.

For each
x\in\Omega\setminus A
there is a null set
A_{x}\subset\Omega
such that

\displaystyle|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)=0\qquad\text{for all $y\in\Omega\setminus A_{x}$}.

Since
\nu>0
a.e. in
B_{r}(0)
, we can assume up to renaming the null set
A_{x}
that

\displaystyle u(y)=u(x)\qquad\text{for $y\in B_{r}(x)\cap(\Omega\setminus A_{x}),\,\,x\in\Omega\setminus A$}.

(7.9)

(i)
If
r\geq\diam(\Omega)
then for fixed
x_{0}\in\Omega\setminus A
we have
\Omega\subset B_{r}(x_{0})
so that
u(x)=u(x_{0})
for all
x\in\Omega\setminus A_{x_{0}}
. Hence,
u=u(x_{0})
almost everywhere in
\Omega
.

(ii)
Assume
\Omega
is connected. By Lemma
7.5
, we can cover
\Omega
with a family of balls
(B_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
,
B_{i}=B(x_{i},r/2)
such that
B_{i-1}\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset,\,\,i=2,3,\cdots,n.
Thus, there is
z_{i}\in(B_{i-1}\cap B_{i})\setminus A
. Consider the null set
A^{\prime}=A_{z_{2}}\cup A_{z_{3}}\cup\cdots\cup A_{z_{n}}
.
Note that
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}\subset B_{r}(z_{i})
and according to (
7.9
) we get
u(x)=u(z_{i})
for all
x\in(B_{r}(z_{i})\cap\Omega)\setminus A_{z_{i}}
. In particular, we have
u(x)=u(z_{i})
for all
x\in(B_{i-1}\cup B_{i})\cap(\Omega\setminus A^{\prime})
. By the same token,
u(x)=u(z_{i+1})
for all
x\in(B_{i}\cup B_{i+1})\cap(\Omega\setminus A^{\prime})
. It turns out that
u(x)=u(z_{i})=u(z_{i+1})
for all
x\in B_{i}\cap(\Omega\setminus A^{\prime})
. Finally, we deduce that
u(z_{2})=u(z_{3})=\cdots u(z_{n})=\lambda
. Since
\Omega=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}\cap\Omega
, we can conclude that
u(x)=\lambda
for all
x\in\Omega\setminus A^{\prime}
where we recall that
A^{\prime}
is a null set.

(iii)
Now assume
\Omega
is
\rho
-connected with
0\leq\rho<r
, i.e.
\Omega=\Omega_{1}\cup\Omega_{2}\cdots\Omega_{m}
where each
\Omega_{i}
is open bounded, connected and
\dist(\Omega_{i},\Omega_{i+1})<\rho
. The previous step implies that
u=\lambda_{i}
a.e. on
\Omega_{i}
,
i=1,2,\cdots,m
. On the other hand, considering
0<\varepsilon<\frac{r-\rho}{2}
so that
0\leq\rho<\rho+2\varepsilon<r
, we can find
a_{i}\in\Omega_{i}
and
b_{i}\in\Omega_{i+1}
such that

\dist(\Omega_{i},\Omega_{i+1})\leq|a_{i}-b_{i}|<\rho+\varepsilon<r.

It follows that
B(a_{i},\varepsilon)\cap\Omega_{i}\neq\emptyset
and
B(a_{i},\rho+\varepsilon)\cap\Omega_{i+1}\neq\emptyset
. Given that
A
is a null set, we can find
\xi_{i}\in(\Omega_{i+1}\cap B(a_{i},\rho+\varepsilon))\setminus A
. Since
u=\lambda_{i+1}
a.e. in
\Omega_{i+1}
, by (
7.9
) we get

u(x)=u(\xi_{i})=\lambda_{i+1}
for all
x\in B(\xi_{i},r)\cap(\Omega\setminus A_{\xi_{i}}).

Note that for
z\in B(a_{i},\varepsilon)
, since
\xi_{i}\in B(a_{i},\rho+\varepsilon)
, we have

|z-\xi_{i}|\leq|z-a_{i}|+|a_{i}-\xi_{i}|<\rho+2\varepsilon<r.

This implies
B(a_{i},\varepsilon)\subset B(\xi_{i},r)
. Since
u=\lambda_{i}
a.e. in
\Omega_{i}
, in particular we have

\displaystyle\text{$\lambda_{i}=u(x)=u(\xi_{i})=\lambda_{i+1}$\,\, for \,\, $x\in B_{\varepsilon}(a_{i})\cap(\Omega_{i}\setminus A_{\xi_{i}})\subset B_{r}(\xi_{i})\cap(\Omega\setminus A_{\xi_{i}})$}.

This shows that
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\cdots=\lambda_{n},
and hence we find
u=\lambda_{1}
a.e. in
\Omega
.
∎

The following result is some general nonlocal Poincaré inequality.

Theorem 7.8
.

Assume
\Omega
is bounded and there is
r>0
such that

\displaystyle\kappa:=\essinf_{h\in B_{r}(0)}\nu(h)>0.

(7.10)

If
r\geq\diam(\Omega)
or else
\Omega
is
\rho
-connected with
r>\rho\geq 0
then there is
C=C(d,p,\Omega,\nu)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)\,\qquad\text{for all $u\in L^{p}(\Omega)$}.

(7.11)

Proof.

If
r\geq\diam(\Omega)
then
\nu(x-y)\geq\kappa
for all
x,y\in\Omega
. Hence Jensen’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)\geq\kappa\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\geq\kappa|\Omega|\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u$}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

Now assume that
0<r<\diam(\Omega)
and
\Omega
is
\rho
-connected with
0<\rho\leq r
. Assume such
C
does not exist. We can find a sequence
(u_{n})_{n}\subset L^{p}(\Omega)
such that
\fint_{\Omega}u_{n}=0
,
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
and
\cE_{\Omega}(u_{n},u_{n})\leq\frac{1}{2^{n}}
and hence

\displaystyle\kappa\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\mathds{1}_{B_{r}(0)}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\cE_{\Omega}(u_{n},u_{n})\leq\frac{1}{2^{n}}\,.

Since
(u_{n})_{n}
is bounded in
L^{p}(\Omega)
, passing through a subsequence if necessary we can assume that
(u_{n})_{n}
weakly converges in
L^{p}(\Omega)
to some
u
, where
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)

p>1
and
u
is a signed Radon measure on
\Omega
for
p=1
. In particular,
\fint_{E}u_{n}(x)\mathrm{d}x\to\fint_{E}u(x)\mathrm{d}x
for every Borel set
E\subset\Omega
. In addition, we can assume there is a null set
A\subset\Omega
such that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(x)\cap\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\mathrm{d}y\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0\quad\text{for all $x\in\Omega\setminus A$}.

(7.12)

Moreover, for each
x\in\Omega\setminus A
there is another null set
A_{x}\subset\Omega
such that

\displaystyle u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0\quad\text{for all $y\in(B_{r}(x)\cap\Omega)\setminus A_{x}$}.

(7.13)

Consider
(B_{r/2}(x_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq n}
a finite cover of
\Omega
with
x_{i}\in\Omega
.
Choose
\xi_{i}\in(B_{r/2}(x_{i})\cap\Omega)\setminus A
. Hence
(E_{\xi_{i}})_{1\leq i\leq n}
with
E_{\xi_{i}}=B_{r}(\xi_{i})\cap\Omega
is also a finite cover of
\Omega
. Note that

\displaystyle\Big|u_{n}(\xi_{i})-\fint_{E_{\xi_{i}}}u(y)\mathrm{d}y\Big|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\fint_{E_{\xi_{i}}}\big|u_{n}(\xi_{i})-u_{n}(y)\big|^{p}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+2^{p-1}\Big|\fint_{E_{\xi_{i}}}u_{n}(y)\mathrm{d}y-\fint_{E_{\xi_{i}}}u(y)\mathrm{d}y\Big|^{p}.

This together with the weak convergence and the convergence in (
7.12
) imply

\displaystyle u_{n}(\xi_{i})\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\lambda_{\xi_{i}}=\fint_{E_{\xi_{i}}}u(y)\mathrm{d}y.

Whence by (
7.13
) we get that

\displaystyle u_{n}(y)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\lambda_{\xi_{i}}\quad\text{for all $y\in E_{\xi_{i}}\setminus A_{\xi_{i}}$},\quad E_{\xi_{i}}=B_{r}(\xi_{i})\cap\Omega.

In other words,
u_{n}\to v
a.e. in
\Omega
where
v(x)=\lambda_{\xi_{i}}
for
x\in E_{\xi_{i}}
. The Fatou’s Lemma yields

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\hskip-4.30554pt|v(x)-v(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\hskip-4.30554pt\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\hskip-4.30554pt|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

It follows from Lemma
7.7
that
v=\lambda
in
\Omega
, that is,
\lambda=\lambda_{\xi_{i}}=\cdots=\lambda_{\xi_{n}}
. On the other hand, since
\Omega=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}E_{\xi_{i}}
, this combined with (
7.12
) gives

\displaystyle\|u_{n}-v\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(\int_{E_{\xi_{i}}}|u_{n}(\xi_{i})-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+|E_{\xi_{i}}|^{1/p}|u_{n}(\xi_{i})-\lambda_{\xi_{i}}|\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

That is,
(u_{n})_{n}
strongly converges to
v=\lambda
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
. Taking into account the weak convergence we deduce that
u=v=\lambda
. Since
\fint_{\Omega}u_{n}=0
and
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
, it follows that
u=\fint_{\Omega}u=0
and
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
which is a contradiction.
∎

Theorem 7.9
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is connected or
0
-connected. Assume
\nu
is unimodal and
|\{\nu>0\}|>0
, i.e.,
\nu\not\equiv 0
. Then there is
C=C(d,p,\Omega,\nu)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)\,\qquad\text{for all $u\in L^{p}(\Omega)$}.

(7.14)

Proof.

Since
\nu\not\equiv 0
there is
\xi\in\R^{d}
,
\xi\neq 0
such that
\nu(\xi)>0
. Let
r=|\xi|>0
, since
\nu
is unimodal we have
\nu(h)\geq c\nu(\xi)
for all
h\in B_{r}(0)
. Therefore, we have
\kappa=\essinf_{h\in B_{r}(0)}\nu(h)>0
where
\kappa=c\nu(\xi)>0
. The desired result follows from Theorem
7.8
.
∎

Corollary 7.10

(Fractional Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
r\in(0,\infty]
and
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be bounded. Assume either
r\geq\diam(\Omega)
or
\Omega
is
\rho
-connected with
0\leq\rho<r
. Then there is
C=C(d,p,\Omega,s,r)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathds{1}_{B_{r}(0)}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\,\,\text{for all $u\in L^{p}(\Omega)$}.

Proof.

The claim is a direct consequence of Theorem
7.8
applied to
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}\mathds{1}_{B_{r}(0)}(h)
.
∎

Remark 7.11
.

One readily recovers the usual fractional Poincaré inequality holding true for any arbitrary bounded
\Omega
. Indeed, taking
r\geq\diam(\Omega)
implies
\mathds{1}_{B_{r}(0)}(x-y)=1
for all
x,y\in\Omega
so that

\displaystyle\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\,\qquad\text{for all $u\in L^{p}(\Omega)$}.

If the assumption (
7.10
) fails i.e.,
\kappa=0
for any
r
, we can balance this deficiency with the compactness.

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
r>\rho\geq 0
and assume
\Omega
is bounded. Assume that

•

\nu>0
a.e. on
B_{r}(0)
,

•

r\geq\diam(\Omega)
or that
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is
\rho
-connected,

•

the embedding
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact.

Then there is
C=C(d,p,\Omega,\nu)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\,\qquad\text{for all $u\in L^{p}(\Omega)$}.

Proof.

Assume
C
does not exist, then we can find a sequence
(u_{n})_{n}\subset L^{p}(\Omega)
such that
\fint_{\Omega}u_{n}=0
,
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
and
\cE_{\Omega}(u_{n},u_{n})\leq\frac{1}{2^{n}}.
The sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
is thus bounded in
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
. Since the embedding
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact, passing through a subsequence if necessary we can assume that
(u_{n})_{n}
converges in
L^{p}(\Omega)
and a.e. in
\Omega
to some
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
. It clearly follows that
\fint_{\Omega}u=0
and
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
. Moreover, by Fatou’s lemma, we have

\displaystyle\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\cE_{\Omega}(u_{n},u_{n})=0.

It follows from Lemma
7.7
that
u
is constant and hence
u=\fint_{\Omega}u=0
a.e. in
\Omega
. This goes against the fact that
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
and hence our initial assumption is wrong.
∎

The Poincaré inequality fails if
\Omega
is
\rho
-connected with
\rho
too large.

Counterexample 7.13
.

Assume
d\geq 2
and consider
\Omega=\Omega_{1}\cup\Omega_{2}
with
\Omega_{1}=B_{1}(0)
and
\Omega_{2}=B_{4}(0)\setminus B_{3}(0)
and
\nu(h)=\mathds{1}_{B_{1}(0)}(h)
.
The sets
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
are connected thus
\Omega
is
\rho
-connected with
\rho>2
. The Poincaré inequality fails for the couple
(\Omega,\nu)
. Indeed, for
u(x)=\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(x)
.
As
u
is not constant on
\Omega
, it is readily seen that
\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u$}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\neq 0
. On the other hand,
u=1
on
\Omega_{1}
and
u=-1
on
\Omega_{2}
, moreover
\mathds{1}_{B_{1}(0)}(x-y)=0
if
x\in\Omega_{1}
and
y\in\Omega_{2}
. Therefore, one easily verifies that
\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)=0.

In general, the Poincaré inequality fails if
\Omega=\Omega_{1}\cup\Omega_{2}
,
\dist(\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2})>r
and
\supp\nu\subset\overline{B_{r}(0)}
. Indeed as above, for
u(x)=\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(x)
we have
\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u$}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\neq 0
and
\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)=0.

8.
Existence of weak solutions

In this section, we deal with the well-posedness of nonlocal problems (

P

ν
,
τ

). It is worth mentioning that our setting easily extends to the general class of symmetric operators
L
and
\cN
, of the form

\displaystyle Lu(x)

\displaystyle=2\pv\int_{\R^{d}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))k(x,y)\mathrm{d}y,

\displaystyle\cN u(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{d}}\psi(u(y)-u(x))k(x,y)\mathrm{d}x

where, for some
\Lambda\geq 1
,
k:\R^{d}\times\R^{d}\setminus\diag\to(0,\infty)
is a symmetric kernel satisfying the elliptic condition

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\nu(x-y)\leq k(x,y)\leq\Lambda\nu(x-y)\qquad\text{for all $(x,y)\in\R^{d}\times\R^{d}\setminus\diag$}.

We only consider case
k(x,y)=\nu(x-y)
and leave this generalization to the good hand of the readers.

8.1.
Basics on direct method

Our strategy for the existence results relies on the direct method of calculus of variations. Let us evoke some fundamental results of calculus of variation mainly collected from
[
7
,
46
,
88
]
.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
(X,\tau)
be a topological space and
\mathcal{J}:X\to\R\cup\{\infty\}
be a functional.

•

\mathcal{J}
is called sequentially
\tau
-coercive (or simply coercive) if lower level sets of
\mathcal{J}
are
\tau
-sequentially precompact, i.e., every sequence
(u_{n})_{n}\subset\{u\in X:\mathcal{J}(u)\leq a\}
,
a\in\R
, has a
\tau
-converging subsequence in
X
.

•

\mathcal{J}
is called sequentially
\tau
-lower semicontinuous (or simply lower semicontinuous) if for any sequence
(u_{n})_{n}

\tau
-converging to
u
in
X
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{J}(u_{n}).

The direct method for the minimization problem
is encapsulated in the following result.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
(X,\tau)
be a topological space. If the functional
\mathcal{J}:X\to\R\cup\{\infty\}
is both coercive and lower semicontinuous, then there is
u_{*}\in X
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(u_{*})=\min_{u\in X}\mathcal{J}(u).

Next we see characterizations of the weak lower semicontinuity and the weak coercivity for the particular situation where
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
is a normed space. Recall on
X
there is the strong topology induced by
\|\cdot\|_{X}
and the weak topology induced by the weak convergence. Let us recall Mazur’s Lemma beforehand.

Theorem 8.3

(Mazur’s Lemma,
[
46
, page 6]
)
.

If a sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
is weakly converge in
X
to some
u\in X
, then for each
n
there is
(\theta_{k}^{n})_{k=n,\cdots,N_{n}}
,
N_{n}\geq n
, such that
\|v_{n}-u\|_{X}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
where for each
n,

\displaystyle v_{n}=\sum_{k=n}^{N_{n}}\theta_{k}^{n}u_{k}\quad\text{with}\quad\sum_{k=n}^{N_{n}}\theta_{k}^{n}=1,\quad\theta_{k}^{n}\geq 0.

In Mazur’s Lemma, the coefficients
(\theta_{k}^{j})^{\prime}s
are not explicitly given. However, if
X
is a
L^{p}
-Lebesgue space with
1\leq p<\infty
, then the Banach-Saks theorem as stated in
[
50
]
infers that for a suitable subsequence
(u_{n_{k}})_{k}
, one can explicitly choose
v_{j}=\frac{1}{j}\sum_{k=1}^{j}u_{n_{k}}
, that is we have
\theta_{k}^{j}\in\{0,\frac{1}{j}\}
. The Mazur’s Lemma implies the following characterization of the weak lower semicontinuity.

Theorem 8.4
.

A weakly lower semicontinuous functional
\mathcal{J}:X\to\R\cup\{\infty\}
is lower semicontinuous. The converse is true if in addition
\mathcal{J}
is convex.

Theorem 8.5
.

If
\mathcal{J}:X\to\R\cup\{\infty\}
is weakly coercive then
\mathcal{J}(u)\to\infty
as
\|u\|_{X}\to\infty.
The converse holds if in addition
X
is a reflexive Banach space.

8.2.
Neumann problem

The Neumann problem for the operator
L
associated with the data
f:\Omega\to\R
and
g:\Omega^{c}\to\mathbb{R}
, is to find
u:\R^{d}\to\R
such that

\displaystyle Lu=f\quad\text{in}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Omega\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\mathcal{N}u=g\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ on }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \R^{d}\setminus\Omega.

(
N
)

It is worth to emphasize that problem (

N

) makes sense only if have
g=0
on
{\R^{d}\setminus\Omega_{\nu}}=0
. Recall that
\Omega_{\nu}=\Omega+\supp\nu
and
\Omega_{\nu}^{*}=\Omega\cup\Omega_{\nu}
is the nonlocal hull of
\Omega
. Keep in mind that
\Omega\subset\Omega_{\nu}=\Omega_{\nu}^{*}
if
0\in\supp\nu
.
This is obviously due to the fact that by nonlocality we have

\displaystyle\cN u(x)=\int_{\Omega}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y=0\qquad\text{for all $x\in\R^{d}\setminus\Omega_{\nu}$},

we recall
\psi(t)=|t|^{p-2}t
. By nonlocality, cf. Section
2
, it turns out that
\Omega_{\nu}
is the smallest set such that

\displaystyle L_{\Omega_{\nu}}u(x):=\int_{\Omega_{\nu}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y=Lu(x)\quad\text{for all $x\in\Omega$}.

(8.1)

Therefore, it is sufficient to prescribe the complement data on
\Omega_{e}=\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
. It is rather counterintuitive to see that prescribing the nonlocal boundary data on
\Omega_{e}
is not all restrictive. Indeed, if we put
g_{e}=g|_{\Omega_{e}}
, the restriction of
g
on
\Omega_{e}
, the problem (

N

) is the same as finding
u:\Omega_{\nu}^{*}=\Omega\cup\Omega_{\nu}\to\R
such that

\displaystyle L_{\Omega_{\nu}^{*}}u=f\quad\text{in}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Omega\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\mathcal{N}u=g_{e}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ on }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Omega_{e}=\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega,

(
N_{\nu}
)

where we recall
\Omega_{e}=\Omega_{\nu}^{*}\setminus\Omega=\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
is nonlocal boundary of
\Omega
with respect to
\nu
. Actually, both problems (

N

) and (

N
ν

) are equivalent.
Indeed, if
u
solves (

N

) then clearly
u_{\nu}=u|_{\Omega_{\nu}^{*}}
solves (

N
ν

). Conversely, if
u_{\nu}
solves (

N
ν

), one verifies that
u=\widetilde{u}_{\nu}
solves (

N

) where
\widetilde{u}_{\nu}
is the zero the extension of
u_{\nu}
off
\Omega_{\nu}^{*}
, i.e.,
\widetilde{u}_{\nu}=u_{\nu}
on
\Omega_{\nu}^{*}
and
\widetilde{u}_{\nu}=0
on
\R^{d}\setminus\Omega_{\nu}^{*}
. From now on, the problem (

N

) is understood in the sense of (

N
ν

). Motivated by the Gauss-Green formula (
B.6
), in Appendix
B.2
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}Lu(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{E}(u,v)-\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}u(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y,

we define weak solutions of the Neumann problem as follows.

Definition 8.6
.

Let
f\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}
and
g\in T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}
. We call
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
a weak solution or a variational solution of Neumann problem (

N

) if

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\langle f,v\rangle+\langle g,v\rangle\quad\mbox{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\,.

(
V^{\prime}
)

Note that if
\Omega
is bounded, the existence of a solution implies the compatibility condition
\langle f,1\rangle+\langle g,1\rangle=0.

It is worth emphasizing that, the choice of
f
and
g
legitimately follows from the natural embeddings
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega^{c},\omega)
. In particular, we have the following.

Definition 8.7
.

Let
\omega\in\{\widetilde{\nu}_{B},\overline{\nu}_{B},\widehat{\nu}_{R}\}
,
B\subset\Omega
(see Definition
5.2
). Let
f\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
and
g\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}})
. We say that
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is a weak solution to the Neumann problem (

N

) if

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad\mbox{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\,.

(
V
)

In this case, taking
v=1,
the compatibility condition reads

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)\mathrm{d}y=0\,.

(
C
)

Some comments and remarks about the Neumann problem may be helpful at this stage.

Remark 8.8
.

Note that
[
36
, Def. 3.6]
and subsequent definitions like
[
79
, Definition 2.7]
look very similar to (

V

) at first glance. However, the test space defined in
[
36
, Eq. (3.1)]
,
[
79
, Section 2]
depends on the Neumann data
g
, which is not natural. Our test space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
in the weak formulation (

V

) does not depend on the Neumann data
g
.

Remark 8.9
.

The compatibility condition
\langle f,1\rangle+\langle g,1\rangle=0
or (

C

) is an implicit requirement that the data
f
and
g
must fulfill before any attempt of solving the problems (

N

),
(

V

) or (

V
′

). This is essentially due to the fact that the operators
L
and
\mathcal{N}
annihilate additive constants. An immediate effect is that, if
f
and
g
are compatible, as long as
u
is a solution to the problem (

N

), (

V

) or (

V
′

) so is any function
u+c
with
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Accordingly, problems (

N

), (

V

) or (

V
′

) are ill-posed in the sense of Hadamard. One of the finest strategies to overcome this issue is to introduce the reduced problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\langle f,v\rangle+\langle g,v\rangle,\quad\mbox{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}\,

(
V^{\prime\perp}
)

where the space
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
is given by

\displaystyle W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}:=\big\{u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}):\int_{\Omega}u(x)\mathrm{d}x=0\big\}.

Note that
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
only discards non-zero constants from
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and is a closed subspace of
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.
It is worth to emphasize that, if
f
and
g
are compatible and
u
is a solution to reduced problem (

V

′
⁣
⟂

) then each
u+c,\,c\in\R
is also solution to (

V
′

) and vice versa.

Remark 8.10
.

The nonlocal formulation of the Neumann problem should be contrasted with the local one, where the operators
L
and
\mathcal{N}
respectively replaced by the operators
-\Delta_{p}
and
\partial_{n,p}u(x)=|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\nabla u(x)\cdot n(x)
. Recall that in passing that
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
is a weak to the Neumann problem

\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}u=f\quad\text{in}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Omega\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\partial_{n,p}u=g\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ on }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \partial\Omega.

(8.2)

if
u
satisfies the variational problem

\displaystyle\cE^{0}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}\sigma(y),\quad\mbox{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)\,,

(8.3)

here we define

\displaystyle\cE^{0}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla v(x)\mathrm{d}x.

The local counterpart of the compatibility condition (

C

) is given by

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}g(y)\mathrm{d}\sigma(y)=0.

(8.4)

Define
W^{1,p}(\Omega)^{\perp}:=\big\{u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega):\int_{\Omega}u(x)\mathrm{d}x=0\big\}.
The local counterpart of (

V

′
⁣
⟂

) reads

\displaystyle\cE^{0}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}\sigma(y),\quad\mbox{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)^{\perp}\,.

(8.5)

For
\Omega
and
u
sufficiently regular,
p\geq 2
, it can be shown that (see for instance
[
52
,
54
]
),
u
solves the problem (

N

) if and only if it solves the problem (

V

). This is in particular possible under the condition that the Gauss-Green formula (
B.6
) holds. To put it simply, under mild conditions on
\Omega
and
\nu
both problems (

N

) and (

V

) are equivalent when the solutions are sufficiently regular enough. Actually, solutions to the variational problem (

V
′

) are critical points of the functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{E}(v,v)-\langle f,v\rangle-\langle g,v\rangle.

It is important to keep in mind that the Fréchet derivative of
\mathcal{J}
on
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is given by

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{J}^{\prime}(u),v\rangle=\cE(u,v)-\langle f,v\rangle-\langle g,v\rangle.

Proposition 8.11
.

The variational problem (

V

′
⁣
⟂

) is equivalent to the minimization problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(u)=\min_{v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}}\mathcal{J}(v).

(
M^{\perp}
)

Analogously, the problem (

V
′

) is equivalent to the minimization problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(u)=\min_{v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\mathcal{J}(v).

(
M
)

Proof.

Let
u,v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
. Assume (

V

′
⁣
⟂

) holds. Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities imply

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)\leq\mathcal{E}(u,u)^{1/p^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}(v,v)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}(u,u)+\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{E}(v,v)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}(u,u)-\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{E}(u,u)+\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{E}(v,v).

By virtue of (

V

′
⁣
⟂

) which holds for
u
and
v
we get
\mathcal{J}(u)\leq\mathcal{J}(v)
and thus
u
solves (

M
⟂

).
Conversely let
u
satisfies (

M
⟂

), i.e.,
\mathcal{J}(u)\leq\mathcal{J}(v)
for
v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
. In particular,
\mathcal{J}(u)\leq\mathcal{J}(u+tv)
for all
t\in\R
. Hence, the mapping
t\mapsto\mathcal{J}(u+tv)
is differentiable and has a critical point at
t=0
. It follows that
u
satisfies (

V

′
⁣
⟂

) since

\displaystyle\cE(u,v)-\langle f,v\rangle-\langle g,v\rangle=\langle\mathcal{J}^{\prime}(u),v\rangle=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\mathcal{J}(u+tv)-\mathcal{J}(u)}{t}=0.

The equivalence between (

V

) and (

M

) can be proved analogously.
∎

We are now in position to state the well-posedness of the problems (

V

′
⁣
⟂

), (

V
′

) and (

V

). Given a weight
\omega
, we opt for the convention that
\omega^{-1}(x)=0
whenever
\omega(x)=0
.

Theorem 8.12
.

Let
\omega\in\{\widetilde{\nu}_{\Omega},\overline{\nu}_{\Omega},\widehat{\nu}_{R}\}
where
|B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega|>0

(
see Definition
5.2
)
.
Assume that (

S
1

) holds so that
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\equiv W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\Omega_{\nu})
is a reflexive Banach space and that Poincaré inequality (

P

) holds (see page

P

). Assume
f\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}

(
or
f\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega))
and
g\in T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}

(
or
g\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}}))
. The following assertions hold.

(1)

Existence
.
There is a unique
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
satisfying (

V

′
⁣
⟂

).
The problem (

V
′

) has a solution
w\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
if and only if the compatibility
\langle f,1\rangle+\langle g,1\rangle=0
holds and
w=u+c
for some
c\in\R.

(2)

Boundedness
.
There is
C=C(d,p,\Omega,\nu)>0
such that any solution
w
to the problem (

V
′

) satisfies

\displaystyle\|w-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq C\Big(\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\Big)^{1/(p-1)}.

(8.6)

(3)

Continuity
.
Moreover, if
u_{i}=w_{i}-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w_{i}$}
,
i=1,2
where
w_{i}
satisfies (

V
′

) with
f=f_{i}
then we have

\displaystyle\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq\begin{cases}C\big(\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\big)^{1/(p-1)}&\hskip-8.61108pt\text{$p\geq 2$},\\
CM\big(\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\big)&\hskip-8.61108pt\text{$p<2$},\end{cases}

(8.7)

where

M=M(f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},g_{2})=\big(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g_{i}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\big)^{\frac{2-p}{p-1}}.

(4)

Problem (

V

).
In the case
f\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
and
g\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}})
identified with the forms
v\mapsto\langle f,v\rangle=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x
and
v\mapsto\langle g,v\rangle=\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y
respectively. Then
f\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}
,
g\in T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}
and there is
C_{1}=C_{1}(d,p,\Omega,\nu)
such that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\leq\|f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}\quad\text{and}\quad\|g\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\leq C_{1}\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}})}.

In other words, both problems (

V

) and (

V
′

) are identical.

Proof.

We emphasize that throughout the proof,
C>0
denotes a generic constant only depending on the constant from the Poincaré inequality and
p
.

(i)
Since
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
we have
g\in T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}
, i.e.,
g\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}
. Thus, the functional

v\mapsto\mathcal{J}(v)=\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{E}(v,v)-\langle f,v\rangle-\langle g,v\rangle

is clearly continuous (hence lower semicontinuous) and convex on
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
a fortiori on
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
. According to Theorem
8.4
,
\mathcal{J}
is weakly lower semicontinuous. On the other hand, in virtue of the Poincaré inequality (

P

) one readily finds a constant
C>0

\displaystyle C\|v-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}v$}\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq\cE(v,v)\quad\text{for all $v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})$}.

(8.8)

Therefore if
v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(v)\geq C\|v\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}-\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}-\|g\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}.

Since
p>1
it follows that
\mathcal{J}(v)\to\infty
as
\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\to\infty
. In fact, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{J}(v)}{\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}}\xrightarrow{}\infty,\quad\text{as $\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\to\infty$}.

Since
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is a Banach space, one deduces in view of Theorem
8.5
that
\mathcal{J}
is weakly coercive on
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
.
Hence, by Theorem
8.2

\mathcal{J}
possesses a minimizer

u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
, i.e.,
u
solves (

M
⟂

). By Proposition
8.11
,
u
is also a solution to (

V

′
⁣
⟂

). The uniqueness follows from the strict convexity of
\mathcal{J}
or merely from
(iii)
; see the estimate (
8.7
).

Now if
f
and
g
are compatible then we easily observe that
\mathcal{J}(v+c)=\mathcal{J}(v)
for every
v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and every
c\in\R
. For
v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
we have
v-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}v$}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
. Hence

\mathcal{J}(u+c)=\mathcal{J}(u)\leq\mathcal{J}(v-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}v$})=\mathcal{J}(v)
.
Thus,
u+c
also solves (

M

) which is equivalent to (

V
′

). Conversely if
w
solves (

V
′

) then one verifies that
w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
solves (

V

′
⁣
⟂

). By uniqueness we get
u=w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}
that is
w=u+c
with
c=\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}
. Moreover, by taking
v=1
in (

V
′

) yields the compatibility condition.

(ii)
If
w\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is a solution to (

V
′

) we have

\displaystyle\cE(w,w)

\displaystyle=\cE(w,w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$})=\langle f,w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\rangle+\langle g,w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}(\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}).

Combining this with the estimate (
8.8
) we find that

\displaystyle\|w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq C(\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}})^{1/(p-1)}.

(iii)

Case
p\geq 2
.
The estimate
(|b|^{p-2}b-|a|^{p-2}a|)(b-a)\geq A^{\prime}_{p}|b-a|^{p}
from (
A.3
) implies

\displaystyle|\cE(v,v-v^{\prime})-\cE(v^{\prime},v-v^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\geq A^{\prime}_{p}\cE(v-v^{\prime},v-v^{\prime}).

(8.9)

Put
w=w_{1}-w_{2}
so that
u_{1}-u_{2}=w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. By (
8.9
) we have

\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{p}\cE(w,w)

\displaystyle\leq|\cE(w_{1},w_{1}-w_{2})-\cE(w_{2},w_{1}-w_{2})|

\displaystyle=|\cE(w_{1},w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$})-\cE(w_{2},w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$})|

\displaystyle=|\langle f_{1}-f_{2},w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\rangle+\langle g_{1}-g_{2},w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\|w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}(\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}).

Inserting this into (
8.8
) implies

\displaystyle\|w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq C(\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}})^{1/(p-1)}.

Case
1<p<2
.
Let rewrite the inequality

(|b|^{p-2}b-|a|^{p-2}a)(b-a)\geq A^{\prime}_{p}|b-a|^{2}(|a|^{p}+|b|^{p})^{\frac{p-2}{p}}
(see (
A.4
)) as

\displaystyle c_{p}|b-a|^{p}\leq\big((|b|^{p-2}b-|a|^{p-2}a)(b-a)\big)^{1/q}(|a|^{p}+|b|^{p})^{1/q^{\prime}},

where
q=\frac{2}{p}
,
q^{\prime}=\frac{2}{2-p}
and
c_{p}=2^{\frac{2-p}{2}}{A^{\prime}_{p}}^{\frac{p}{2}}
.
This, together with Hölder inequality yields, with
v_{1}=v-v^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\big(\cE(v,v_{1})-\cE(v^{\prime},v_{1})\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\big(\cE(v,v)+\cE(v^{\prime},v^{\prime})\big)^{\frac{2-p}{2}}\geq c_{p}\cE(v_{1},v_{1}).\vskip-17.22217pt

(8.10)

The same reasoning as above yields

\displaystyle c_{p}\cE(w,w)\leq\|w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}^{\frac{p}{2}}

\displaystyle\big(\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\big(\cE(w_{1},w_{1})+\cE(w_{2},w_{2})\big)^{\frac{2-p}{2}}.

On the other hand, by the estimate (
8.6
) we have

\displaystyle\big(\cE(w_{1},w_{1})+\cE(w_{2},w_{2})\big)^{\frac{2-p}{2}}\hskip-4.30554pt\leq\hskip-2.15277ptC\big(\hskip-0.86108pt\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g_{i}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\hskip-1.29167pt\big)^{\frac{2-p}{p-1}}\hskip-4.30554pt=\hskip-2.15277ptCM.

Altogether with the estimate (
8.8
) implies

\displaystyle\|w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq CM\big(\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\big).

(iv)
Clearly, Hölder inequality implies

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\Big|\leq\|f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}\|v\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq\|f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\,.

By Hölder inequality and the continuity of the embedding
\operatorname{Tr}:T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega^{c},\omega)
(see Theorem
5.15
) we get

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}})}\|v\|_{L^{p}(\Omega^{c},\omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}})}\|v\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}.

The remaining follows since in this case problems (

V

) and (

V
′

) are identical.
∎

Remark 8.13
.

A modification of the Neumann data up to a multiplicative weight; such as the substitution
g(y)=g_{*}(y)\omega^{\beta}(y)
,
\beta\in\R
results in another variant of the Neumann problem.
Naturally, one retrieves the following configuration:

(1)

The Neumann problem (

N

) becomes

\displaystyle Lu=f\quad\text{in}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Omega\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\mathcal{N}u=g_{*}\,\omega^{\beta}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ on }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \R^{d}\setminus\Omega.

(
N_{*}
)

(2)

The weak formulation (

V

) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{*}(y)v(y)\omega^{\beta}(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad\mbox{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\,,

(
V_{*}
)

whereas the compatibility condition (

C

) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{*}(y)\omega^{\beta}(y)\mathrm{d}y=0.

(
C_{*}
)

(3)

Last, if
g_{*}\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{\gamma})
with
\beta=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{\gamma}{p^{\prime}}
, the map
v\mapsto\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{*}(y)\omega^{\beta}(y)\mathrm{d}y
is linear and belongs to
T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}
. Some special couples are given by
(\gamma,\beta)\in\{(1-p^{\prime},0),(1,1),(0,\frac{1}{p})\}.

The next result concerns the non-existence of weak solutions when the Neumann data
g
is not in the weighted nonlocal trace space
L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}})
. In other words,
L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}})
is a sufficiently large function space as the data space for the Neumann problem.

Theorem 8.14

(
Non-existence of weak solution
)
.

Let
\Omega=B_{1}(0)
and
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
,
s\in(0,1)
so that
\widetilde{\nu}(h)\asymp(1+|h|)^{-d-sp}
.
There exists
g\in L^{1}(\Omega^{c})\setminus L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\widetilde{\nu}^{1-p^{\prime}})
compatible with
f=0
i.e.
\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)\mathrm{d}y=0
, such that the Neumann problem
Lu=0
on
\Omega
and
\mathcal{N}u=g
on
\R^{d}\setminus\Omega
does not have a weak solution in
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.

Proof.

Let us define
g(x)=g_{\gamma}(x)\widetilde{\nu}(x)
with
g_{\gamma}(x)=\frac{x_{1}}{|x|}(|x|-1)^{\gamma}\mathds{1}_{B^{c}_{1}(0)}(x)
,
\gamma\in\R
.
For
x\in B^{c}_{1}(0)
we have
\dist(x,\partial\Omega)=\dist(x,\mathbb{S}^{d-1})=(|x|-1)
and

\displaystyle\int_{B_{1}(0)}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\asymp(|x|-1)^{-sp}\land(|x|-1)^{-d-sp}.

Using polar coordinates yields that

\displaystyle\|g_{\gamma}\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}

\displaystyle=2\int_{B^{c}_{1}(0)}\frac{|x_{1}|^{p}}{|x|^{p}}(|x|-1)^{p\gamma}\int_{B_{1}(0)}|x-y|^{-d-sp}\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\asymp 2\int_{B^{c}_{1}(0)}\frac{|x_{1}|^{p}}{|x|^{p}}(|x|-1)^{p\gamma-sp}(1\land(|x|-1)^{-d})\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=2|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|K_{d,p}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}r^{p\gamma-sp}\mathrm{d}r+\int_{1}^{\infty}r^{p\gamma-sp-1}\mathrm{d}r\Big).

Therefore we deduce that
g_{\gamma}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
if and only if
\gamma\in(\frac{sp-1}{p},s)
. Analogously,
g_{\gamma}g_{\beta}\in L^{1}(\Omega^{c},\widetilde{\nu})
if and only if
\gamma+\beta\in(-1,sp)
. Since
g_{p^{\prime}\gamma+0}=g^{p^{\prime}}_{\gamma}
, it follows that
g_{\gamma}\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\widetilde{\nu})
if and only if
\gamma\in(-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}},\frac{sp}{p^{\prime}})
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\|g_{\gamma}g_{\beta}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega^{c},\widetilde{\nu})}

\displaystyle=\int_{B^{c}_{1}(0)}\frac{|x_{1}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}(|x|-1)^{\gamma+\beta}(1+|x|)^{-d-sp}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\asymp\Big(\int_{0}^{1}r^{\gamma+\beta}\mathrm{d}r+\int_{1}^{\infty}r^{\gamma+\beta-sp-1}\mathrm{d}r\Big).

By duality
g=g_{\gamma}\widetilde{\nu}\in L^{1}(\Omega^{c})\setminus L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\widetilde{\nu}^{1-p^{\prime}})
if and only if
g_{\gamma}\in L^{1}(\Omega^{c},\widetilde{\nu})\setminus L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\widetilde{\nu})
, i.e., if and only if
\gamma\in(-1,-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}]\cup[\frac{sp}{p^{\prime}},sp)
.
Moreover by symmetry of
g_{\gamma}
, we have that
g=g_{\gamma}\widetilde{\nu}
satisfies the compatibility condition

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\gamma}(y)\widetilde{\nu}(y)\mathrm{d}y=0.

Assume the Neumann problem has a weak solution
u
. That is

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\gamma}(y)v(y)\widetilde{\nu}(y)\mathrm{d}y\quad\text{for all}\quad v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}).

Consider
C=1+\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}>0
, we find that

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\gamma}(y)v(y)\widetilde{\nu}(y)\mathrm{d}y\Big|\leq C\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\quad\text{ for all}\quad v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}).

In particular taking
v=g_{\beta}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
amounts the above estimate to

\displaystyle\|g_{\gamma}g_{\beta}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega^{c},\widetilde{\nu})}=\Big|\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\gamma}(y)g_{\beta}(y)\widetilde{\nu}(y)\mathrm{d}y\Big|\leq C\|g_{\beta}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})},

for all
\beta\in(\tfrac{sp-1}{p},s)
. Now if
sp\geq 1
, consider
\gamma\in(\frac{sp}{p^{\prime}},\frac{1}{p}+\frac{sp}{p^{\prime}})\subset[\frac{sp}{p^{\prime}},sp)
and take
\beta=sp-\gamma
.
If
sp\leq 1
consider
\gamma\in(-1,-(s+\tfrac{1}{p^{\prime}}))\subset(-1,-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}]
and take
\beta=-\gamma-1
.
In both cases,
\gamma\in(-1,-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}]\cup[\frac{sp}{p^{\prime}},sp)
and

\beta\in(\tfrac{sp-1}{p},s)
and
\gamma+\beta\in\{-1,sp\}
.
In other words,
g_{\gamma}\in L^{1}(\Omega^{c},\widetilde{\nu})\setminus L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\widetilde{\nu})
and
g_{\beta}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. Whence
\|g_{\gamma}g_{\beta}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega^{c},\widetilde{\nu})}=\infty
and
\|g_{\beta}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}<\infty
, which contradicts the above inequality.
∎

The well-posedness of the Neumann problem for the regional operator
L_{\Omega}
can be derived analogously.

\displaystyle L_{\Omega}u(x):=2\pv\int_{\Omega}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y.

Theorem 8.15
.

Assume that the Poincaré inequality (

P

) holds (see page

P

). Let
f\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)^{\prime}
. The following assertions are true.

(1)

Existence
. There is a unique
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)^{\perp}
satisfying
\cE_{\Omega}(u,v)=\langle f,v\rangle
for all
v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)^{\perp}
. Furthermore a function
w\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)
is weak solution to
L_{\Omega}u=f
in
\Omega
, i.e., satisfies

\displaystyle\cE_{\Omega}(u,v)=\langle f,v\rangle\qquad\text{for all $v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)$},

(8.11)

if and only if
w=u+c
form some
c\in\R
and
\langle f,1\rangle=0
.

(2)

Boundedness
.
Any solution
w
to (
8.11
) satisfies

\displaystyle\|w-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|^{1/(p-1)}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)^{\prime}}.

(3)

Continuity
.
If
u_{i}=w_{i}-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w_{i}$}
,
i=1,2
where
w_{i}
satisfies (
8.11
) with
f=f_{i}
then

\displaystyle\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)}\leq\begin{cases}C\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|^{1/(p-1)}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)^{\prime}}&\hskip-8.61108pt\text{$p\geq 2$},\\
C\big(\|f_{1}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)^{\prime}}+\|f_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)^{\prime}}\big)^{\frac{2-p}{p-1}}\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega)^{\prime}}&\hskip-8.61108pt\text{$p<2$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of Theorem
8.12
.
∎

8.3.
Dirichlet problem

The Dirichlet problem associated with the data
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:\Omega^{c}\to\R
is to find
u:\R^{d}\to\R
such that

\displaystyle Lu=f\quad\text{in}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Omega\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad u=g\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ on }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \R^{d}\setminus\Omega.

(
D
)

In contrast to the Neumann condition (
\cN u=g
on
\R^{d}\setminus\Omega
), the Dirichlet condition
(
u=g
on
\R^{d}\setminus\Omega
) does not impose any constraint on
g
. Note however that the evaluation of
g
on
\R^{d}\setminus\Omega_{\nu}
with
\Omega_{\nu}=\Omega+\supp\nu
, does not influence the values of
u
in
\Omega
since
Lu(x)=L_{\Omega_{\nu}}u(x)
for all
x\in\Omega
. This is merely due to the fact that
Lu(x)=L_{\Omega_{\nu}}u(x)
for all
x\in\Omega
; see (
8.1
).
It is therefore enough to prescribe the Dirichlet data only the exterior domain (the nonlocal boundary)
\Omega_{e}=\Omega_{\nu}^{*}\setminus\Omega=\Omega_{\nu}\setminus\Omega
where
\Omega_{\nu}=\Omega+\supp\nu
and
\Omega_{\nu}^{*}=\Omega\cup\Omega_{\nu}
is the nonlocal hull of
\Omega
. Accordingly, the problem (

D

) is the same as finding
u:\Omega_{\nu}^{*}=\Omega\cup\Omega_{\nu}\to\R
such that
