\displaystyle Lu=f\quad\text{in}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Omega\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad u=g_{e}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ on }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Omega_{e}.

(
D_{\nu}
)

where
g_{e}=g|_{\Omega_{e}}
is the restriction of
g
on
\Omega_{e}
.
Actually, both problems (

D

) and (

D
ν

) are equivalent.
Indeed, if
u
solves (

D

) then clearly
u_{\nu}=u|_{\Omega_{\nu}^{*}}
solves (

D
ν

). Conversely, if
u_{\nu}
solves (

D
ν

) then the function defined
u(x)=\widetilde{u}_{\nu}(x)
for
x\in\Omega_{\nu}^{*}
and
u(x)=g(x)
for
x\in\R^{d}\setminus\Omega_{\nu}^{*}
solves (

D

).
From now on, the problem (

D

) is understood in the sense of (

D
ν

). Motivated by the Gauss-Green formula (
B.6
), in Appendix
B.2
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}Lu(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{E}(u,v)-\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}u(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y,

we define weak solutions of the Dirichlet problem as follows.

Definition 8.16
.

Let
f\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}
and
g\in T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
. We say that
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is a weak solution or the variational solution of the Dirichlet problem (

D

) if

\displaystyle u-g\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\langle f,v\rangle\quad\mbox{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\,.

(
V_{0}
)

Actually, for any extension
\overline{g}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
of
g
, i.e.,
g=\overline{g}
a.e. on
\Omega^{c}
, solution to the variational problem (

V
0

) are critical points of the functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{E}(v,v)-\langle f,v-\overline{g}\rangle,\qquad\text{$v\in g+W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})$}.

(8.12)

It is decisive to keep in mind that the Fréchet derivative of
\mathcal{J}_{0}
is given as

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{0}(u),v\rangle=\cE(u,v)-\langle f,v\rangle\qquad\text{for all $u,v\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})$}.

Proposition 8.17
.

Let
\overline{g}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
be an extension of
g
, i.e.,
g=\overline{g}
a.e. on
\Omega^{c}
. The variational problem (

V
0

) is equivalent to the minimization problem

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{J}_{0}(u)=\min_{v-\overline{g}\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\mathcal{J}_{0}(v).\end{split}

(
M_{0}
)

Moreover, if
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is bounded in one direction or
|\Omega|<\infty
and
\nu\not\equiv 0
then any solution to(

V
0

) or (

M
0

) is independent on the choice of
\overline{g}
.

Proof.

Let
u,v\in\overline{g}+W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
then
v-u\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. Assume
u
solves (

V
0

) then using Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities we get

\displaystyle\langle f,v-u\rangle=\mathcal{E}(u,v-u)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}(u,u)^{1/p^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}(v,v)^{1/p}-\mathcal{E}(u,u)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{E}(v,v)-\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{E}(u,u).

Since
\langle f,v-u\rangle=\langle f,v-\overline{g}\rangle-\langle f,u-\overline{g}\rangle
, it follows that
\mathcal{J}_{0}(u)\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}(v)
hence
u
solves (

M
0

).

Conversely let
u
satisfies (

M
0

), i.e.,
u-g\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and
\mathcal{J}_{0}(u)\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}(v)
for
v\in g+W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.
In particular, since
u+tv\in g+W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
for all
v\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and
t\in\R
it follows that
\mathcal{J}_{0}(u)\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}(u+tv)
. Thus, the mapping
t\mapsto\mathcal{J}_{0}(u+tv)
is differentiable and has a critical point at
t=0
. It follows that
u
satisfies (

V
0

) since

\displaystyle\cE(u,v)-\langle f,v\rangle=\langle\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{0}(u),v\rangle=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}(u+tv)-\mathcal{J}_{0}(u)}{t}=0.

Now, assume
g_{i}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
,
i=1,2
are different extensions of
g
, i.e.,
g_{1}=g_{2}=g
a.e. on
\Omega^{c}
. Let
u_{i}
be the associated solution to (

V
0

) (or minimizer of
\mapsto\mathcal{J}_{0}(v+g_{i})
). In particular
u_{1}=u_{2}=g
a.e. on
\Omega^{c}
and
u_{i}-g_{i}\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. Hence testing with
u_{1}-u_{2}\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
, by definition of
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
we have
\cE(u_{1},u_{1}-u_{2})=\cE(u_{1},u_{1}-u_{2})\langle f,u_{1}-u_{2}\rangle
. In virtue of the estimates (
8.9
) and (
8.10
) we deduce that
\cE(u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1}-u_{2})=0
. According to Theorem
7.2
or Theorem
7.4
the Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality (
7.4
) or (
7.7
) holds, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\cE(u_{1}-u_{2},u_{1}-u_{2})=0.

Thus,
u_{1}=u_{2}
a.e. on
\Omega
and
u_{1}=u_{2}=g
a.e. on
\Omega^{c}
, that is we get
u_{1}=u_{2}
a.e. on
\R^{d}
.
∎

We are now in position to state the well-posedness of the problem (

V
0

).

Theorem 8.18
.

Assume
\nu\not\equiv 0
, i.e.,
|\{\nu>0\}|>0
and
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is bounded in one direction or
|\Omega|<\infty
. Let
f\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}
and
g\in T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
. The following assertions hold.

(1)

Existence
. The variational problem (

V
0

) has a unique solution
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.

(2)

Boundedness
. Moreover, there is
C=C(d,p,\Omega,\nu)>0
such that for any
\overline{g}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
with
\overline{g}|_{\Omega^{c}}=g
,

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)

\displaystyle\leq C(\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}+\cE(\overline{g},\overline{g})),

(8.13)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq C\big(\|f\|^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g\|^{p}_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\big)^{1/p}.

(8.14)

(3)

Continuity
.
Let
u_{i}
be the solution associated with
f=f_{i}
and
g=g_{i}
,
i=1,2
. Let us put
R=(D^{\frac{1}{p-1}}+D)^{1/2}
with

D=D(f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},g_{2})=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\big(\|f_{i}\|^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g_{i}\|^{p}_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\big)^{1/p^{\prime}}.

The following estimates hold true. If
p\geq 2
we have

\displaystyle\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|^{1/(p-1)}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}

\displaystyle+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}+D^{1/p}\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|^{1/p}_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}).

If
1<p<2
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}&\leq C(D^{\frac{2-p}{p-1}}\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\\
&+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}+R\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}^{1/2}).\end{split}

Remark 8.19
.

By definition of the trace space, for
g\in T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
there is
\overline{g}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
such that
g=\overline{g}
a.e. on
\Omega^{c}
. Recall that by Proposition
8.17
, any solution
u
to the Dirichlet problem (

V
0

) does not depend on the choice of
\overline{g}
. Furthermore we recall that

\displaystyle\|g\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}=\inf\{\|\overline{g}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\,:\,\overline{g}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}),\,\overline{g}=g\,\text{ a.e. on $\Omega^{c}$}\}.

Without loss of generality, it is sufficient to assume that all Dirichlet data
g\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.

Proof.

We emphasize that throughout the proof,
C>0
denotes a generic constant only depending on the constant from the Poincaré inequality and
p
.

(i)
The functional
v\mapsto\mathcal{J}_{0}(v+g)=\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{E}(v+g,v+g)-\langle f,v\rangle
is clearly convex and continuous (hence lower semicontinuous) on
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. According to Theorem
8.4

\mathcal{J}_{0}
is weakly lower semicontinuous.
On the other hand, in virtue of Theorem
7.2
or Theorem
7.4
the Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality (
7.4
) or (
7.7
) holds. In any case, one readily finds a constant
C>0

\displaystyle C\|v\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq\cE(v,v)\quad\text{for all $v\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})$}.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle C\|v-g\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq C\cE(v-g,v-g)\quad\text{for all $v\in g+W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})$}.

(8.15)

Therefore, since
\cE(v,v)\leq 2^{p}\cE(v+g,v+g)+2^{p}\cE(g,g)
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0}(v+g)\geq 2^{-p}C\|v\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}-\cE(g,g)-\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}.

Since
p>1
we have
\mathcal{J}(v+g)\to\infty
as
\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\to\infty
. In fact, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}(v+g)}{\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}}\xrightarrow{}\infty,\quad\text{as $\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\to\infty$, $v\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})$}.

Since
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is always a reflexive Banach space as
p>1
, Theorem
8.5
implies that
v\mapsto\mathcal{J}_{0}(v+g)
is weakly coercive on
W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. Hence, by Theorem
8.2

\mathcal{J}_{0}(\cdot+g)
possesses a minimizer
u_{0}\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
, that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0}(u_{0}+g)=\min_{w\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\mathcal{J}_{0}(w+g)=\min_{v\in g+W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\mathcal{J}_{0}(v).

In other words
u=u_{0}+g
solves (

M
0

) and, by Proposition
8.17
,
u
is also a solution to (

V
0

).
It is worth emphasizing that
u
is independent of the choice of the extension
g
; see Proposition
8.17
. The uniqueness follows from the strict convexity of
\mathcal{J}_{0}(\cdot+g)
or merely from the estimates in
(iii)
.

(ii)

Since
u
is a solution to (

V
0

) we have

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)

\displaystyle=\cE(u,u-g)+\cE(u,g)=\langle f,u-g\rangle+\cE(u,g)

\displaystyle\leq\|u-g\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\cE(u,u)^{1/p^{\prime}}\cE(g,g)^{1/p}.

Since
u-g\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
the coercivity estimate (
8.15
) yields

\displaystyle\|u-g\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq C\cE(u-g,u-g)^{1/p}\leq C\cE(u,u)^{1/p}+C\cE(g,g)^{1/p}.

Next, for
a,b\in\R

\delta>0
, applying the Young’s inequality on
a\delta
and
\frac{b}{\delta}
implies

\displaystyle|ab|\leq\frac{\delta^{p}a^{p}}{p}+\frac{b^{p^{\prime}}}{p^{\prime}\delta^{p^{\prime}}}.

(8.16)

Accordingly, by exploiting the Young inequality (
8.16
) we get

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)^{1/p^{\prime}}\cE(g,g)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta^{p}\cE(u,u)}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{\cE(g,g)}{p\delta^{\frac{p^{2}}{p^{\prime}}}},

\displaystyle C\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\cE(u,u)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta^{p}\cE(u,u)}{p}+\frac{C^{p^{\prime}}\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}}{p^{\prime}\delta^{p^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle C\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\cE(g,g)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta^{p}\cE(g,g)}{p}+\frac{C^{p^{\prime}}\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}}{p^{\prime}\delta^{p^{\prime}}}.

Inserting altogether in the previous estimate we obtain

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\cE(u,u)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+C\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\cE(g,g)^{1/p}+\cE(u,u)^{1/p^{\prime}}\cE(g,g)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{p}\cE(u,u)+(\frac{1}{p\delta^{\frac{p^{2}}{p^{\prime}}}}+\frac{\delta^{p}}{p})\cE(g,g)+\frac{2C^{p^{\prime}}}{p^{\prime}\delta^{p^{\prime}}}\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}.

Taking in particular
\delta^{p}=\frac{1}{2}
yields the desired estimate (
8.13
)

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)

\displaystyle\leq C(\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}+\cE(g,g)).

The coercivity estimate (
8.15
) implies

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}&\leq\|g\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+C\cE(u-g,u-g)^{1/p}\\
&\leq C\|g\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}+C\cE(u,u)^{1/p}.\end{split}

(8.17)

This together with the penultimate estimate yields the inequality (
8.14
)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|f\|^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}+\|g\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})})^{1/p}.

(iii)
Put
u=u_{1}-u_{2}
,
f=f_{1}-f_{2}
and
g=g_{1}-g_{2}
. We clearly have
u-g\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.

Case
p\geq 2
.

By exploiting the estimate (
8.15
) and Young’s inequality (
8.16
) we find that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|u-&g\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\leq C\cE(u-g,u-g)^{1/p}\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\\
&\leq C\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}(\cE(u,u)^{1/p}+\cE(g,g)^{1/p})\\
&\leq\frac{\delta^{p}\cE(u,u)}{p}+\frac{C}{p^{\prime}\delta^{p^{\prime}}}\|f\|^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+CD\cE(g,g)^{1/p},\end{split}

(8.18)

where we used
\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\leq D
. Analogously as for (
8.13
), we find that

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\cE(u_{1},g)-\cE(u_{2},g)|&\leq(\cE(u_{1},u_{1})^{1/p^{\prime}}+\cE(u_{2},u_{2})^{1/p^{\prime}})\cE(g,g)^{1/p}\\
&\leq CD\cE(g,g)^{1/p}.\end{split}

(8.19)

Using the definition of
u_{i}
and the estimates (
8.9
), (
8.18
) and (
8.19
) we obtain

\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{p}\cE(u,u)

\displaystyle\leq|\cE(u_{1},u-g)-\cE(u_{2},u-g)+\cE(u_{1},g)-\cE(u_{2},g)|

\displaystyle=|\langle f,u-g\rangle+\cE(u_{1},g)-\cE(u_{2},g)|

\displaystyle\leq\|u-g\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+|\cE(u_{1},g)-\cE(u_{2},g)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta^{p}\cE(u,u)}{p}+\frac{C}{p^{\prime}\delta^{p^{\prime}}}\|f\|^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+CD\cE(g,g)^{1/p}.

Accordingly, taking
\delta^{p}=A^{\prime}_{p}
yields

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)\leq C\|f\|^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+CD\cE(g,g)^{1/p}.

Since
u-g\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
, combining this with the estimate (
8.17
) gives

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|f\|^{p^{\prime}}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}+\|g\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}+D\|g\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})})^{1/p}.

\displaystyle\leq C(\|f\|^{1/(p-1)}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}+\|g\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}+D^{1/p}\|g\|^{1/p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}).

Case
1<p<2
.
Using the definition of
u_{i}
and the estimates (
8.10
) yields

\displaystyle c_{p}\cE(u,u)

\displaystyle\leq\big(\cE(u_{1},u-g)-\cE(u_{2},u-g)+\cE(u_{1},g)-\cE(u_{2},g)\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}H

\displaystyle=\big(\langle f,u-g\rangle+\cE(u_{1},g)-\cE(u_{2},g)\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}H

\displaystyle\leq\big(\|u-g\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+|\cE(u_{1},g)-\cE(u_{2},g)|\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}H,

with
H=\big(\cE(u_{1},u_{1})+\cE(u_{2},u_{2})\big)^{\frac{2-p}{2}}.
By exploiting once more, the estimates in (
8.18
) and (
8.19
) one readily arrives at the following

\displaystyle c_{p}\cE(u,u)

\displaystyle\leq\hskip-2.15277ptCD^{\frac{p^{\prime}(2-p)}{2}}(\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}(\cE(u,u)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\cE(g,g)^{1/p}))^{\frac{p}{2}}\hskip-1.72218pt+\hskip-2.15277ptD^{p/2}\,\cE(g,g)^{1/2})

\displaystyle\leq CD^{\frac{p(2-p)}{2(p-1)}}(\|f\|^{\frac{p}{2}}_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}(\cE(u,u)^{1/2}+\cE(g,g)^{1/2}))+D^{p/2}\,\cE(g,g)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta^{2}\cE(u,u)}{2}+\frac{C}{2\delta^{2}}\|f\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}D^{{}^{\frac{p(2-p)}{p-1}}}\hskip-2.15277pt+C(D^{p^{\prime}/2}+D^{p/2})\cE(g,g)^{1/2},

we used
\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\leq D
and the inequality (
8.16
). For
\delta^{2}=c_{p}
we get

\displaystyle\cE(u,u)\leq CD^{{}^{\frac{p(2-p)}{p-1}}}\|f\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+C(D^{p/2(p-1)}+D^{p/2})\cE(g,g)^{1/2}.

Since
u-g\in W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})
, combining this with the estimate (
8.17
) implies

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C(D^{\frac{2-p}{p-1}}\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu,\Omega}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}

\displaystyle+(D^{\frac{1}{p-1}}+D)^{1/2}\|g\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}^{1/2}).

∎

Theorem 8.20

(Weak comparison principle)
.

Assume that
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is bounded in one direction or that
|\Omega|<\infty
and
\nu\not\equiv 0
. Let
u,v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. Assume that
v\leq u
a.e. on
\Omega^{c}
and
Lv\leq Lu
in
\Omega
in the weak sense, i.e.,

\displaystyle\cE(v,w)\leq\cE(u,w)\qquad\text{for all $w\in W_{\nu,0}^{p}(\Omega|\R^{d})$, $w\geq 0$}.

Then we have
v\leq u
a.e. in
\R^{d}
.

Proof.

Recall that for
t\in\R
we put
\psi(t)=|t|^{p-2}t
and
t_{+}=\max(t,0)
and
t_{-}=\max(-t,0)
so that
t=t_{+}-t_{-}
. Note that by Corollary
A.7
we have

\displaystyle(\psi(b)-\psi(a))

\displaystyle((b_{1}-a_{1})_{+}-(b_{2}-a_{2})_{+})

\displaystyle\geq\begin{cases}A^{\prime}_{p}|(b_{1}-a_{1})_{+}-(b_{2}-a_{2})_{+}|^{p}&\hskip-4.30554ptp\geq 2,\\
A^{\prime}_{p}|(b_{1}-a_{1})_{+}-(b_{2}-a_{2})_{+}|^{2}(|b|+|a|)^{p-2}&\hskip-4.30554pt1<p<2.\end{cases}

Consider
w=(v-u)_{+}
so that
w=0
on
\Omega^{c}
since
v-u\leq 0
on
\Omega^{c}
. Hence
w\in W_{\nu,0}^{p}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and
w\geq 0
.
Taking
b_{1}=v(x),

b_{2}=v(y),

a_{1}=u(x),\,a_{2}=u(y)
and proceeding as for the estimates (
8.9
) and (
8.10
) we get

\displaystyle 0\geq\cE(v,w)-\cE(u,w)

\displaystyle\geq A^{\prime}_{p}\cE(w,w)\qquad\text{$p\geq 2$},

\displaystyle 0\geq\big(\cE(v,w)-\cE(u,w)\big)\big(\cE(v,v)+\cE(u,u)\big)^{\frac{2-p}{p}}

\displaystyle\geq c_{p}\cE(w,w)^{\frac{2}{p}}\qquad\text{$1<p<2$}.

In any case we deduce that
\cE(w,w)=0
. In view of the Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality (see Theorem
7.2
and Theorem
7.4
) we also have
\|w\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=0
and hence
\|w\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}=0
. It follows that
w=(v-u)_{+}=0
a.e. on
\R^{d}
equivalently
v\leq u
a.e. on
\R^{d}
.
∎

Theorem 8.21

(Weak and strong maximum principle)
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
8.20
be in force. Assume that
u=g\geq 0
a.e. on
\Omega^{c}
and
Lu=f\geq 0
in
\Omega
in the weak sense.
Then we have
u\geq 0
a.e. in
\R^{d}
. If in addition
u
is continuous and
\nu>0
then either
u\equiv 0
in
\R^{d}
or
u>0
in
\Omega
.

Proof.

The fact that
u\geq 0
a.e. in
\R^{d}
follows from Theorem
8.20
. Assume
u
is continuous and
\nu>0
. If
u(x_{0})\leq 0
for some
x_{0}\in\Omega
then since
\psi
is increasing we have

\displaystyle 0\leq Lu(x_{0})

\displaystyle=2\pv\int_{\R^{d}}\psi(u(x_{0})-u(y))\nu(x_{0}-y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq-2\pv\int_{\R^{d}}(u(y))^{p-1}\nu(x_{0}-y)\mathrm{d}y\leq 0,

which is only possible if
u(y)=0
for all
y\in\R^{d}
since
u\geq 0
and
\nu>0
.
∎

8.4.
Robin problem

In the classical setting for the
p
-Laplace operator, the Robin boundary problem
2
2

2

According to the over 20 years of survey work
[
61
]
, there is no historical evidence why the problem (
8.20
) is termed after Robin’s name in the setting
p=2
. The survey
[
61
, p.69]
also points out that the first mathematical appearance of the problem (
8.20
) goes back at least to the works on cooling law by Fourier(1822) and/or Newton (1701, but a mathematical contribution by Newton is uncertain).

– also known as Fourier boundary problem or third boundary problem –combines the Dirichlet and Neumann boundary problem as follows

\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}u=f\,\,\text{ in }\Omega\quad\text{ and }\quad\partial_{n,p}u+\beta|u|^{p-2}u=g\,\,\text{ on }\partial\Omega.

(8.20)

Here
f\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
and
\beta,g:\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
are given. Analogously, in the nonlocal set-up, the Robin problem for
L
with data
\beta,g:\Omega^{c}\to\mathbb{R}
and
f\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
is to find
u:\R^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle Lu=f\text{ in }\Omega\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{N}u+\beta|u|^{p-2}u=g\text{ on }\Omega^{c}.

(8.21)

Note that, for
\beta=0
one recovers the inhomogeneous Neumann problem. For
\beta\to\infty
it leads to the homogeneous Dirichlet problem. Let define the form

\displaystyle Q_{\beta}(u,v)=\mathcal{E}(u,v)+\int_{\Omega^{c}}|u(y)|^{p-2}u(y)v(y)\beta(y)\mathrm{d}y.

As for the Neumann problem, we define a weak solution of (
8.21
) as follows.

Definition 8.22
.

We say that
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is a weak solution (or variational solution) of the Robin problem (
8.21
) if

\displaystyle Q_{\beta}(u,v)=\langle f,v\rangle+\langle g,v\rangle\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{for all }\penalty\ v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}).

(
V_{\beta}
)

In fact, the problem (

V
β

) is equivalent to the minimization problem

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{J}_{\beta}(u)&=\min_{v\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\mathcal{J}_{\beta}(v),\\
\mathcal{J}_{\beta}(v)&=\frac{1}{p}Q_{\beta}(v,v)-\langle f,v\rangle-\langle g,v\rangle.\end{split}

(
M_{\beta}
)

Theorem 8.23
.

Let
\omega\in\{\widetilde{\nu}_{\Omega},\overline{\nu}_{\Omega},\widehat{\nu}_{R}\}
where
|B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega|>0

(
see Definition
5.2
)
.
Assume that
\nu
have full support,
\beta\omega^{-1}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{c})
,
\beta
is nontrivial, i.e.,
|\Omega^{c}\cap\{\beta>0\}|>0
and the embedding
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact. Assume
f\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}

(
or
f\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega))
and
g\in T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}

(
or
g\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega^{c},\omega^{1-p^{\prime}}))
.

(1)

Existence
. There exists a unique
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
satisfying (

V
β

).

(2)

Boundedness
. Moreover, there exists
C=C(d,p,\Omega,\nu,\beta)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq C\Big(\|f\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\Big)^{1/(p-1)}.

(8.22)

(3)

Continuity
.
If
u_{i}
,
i=1,2
satisfies (

V
β

) with
f=f_{i}
and
g=g_{i}
then

\displaystyle\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq\begin{cases}C\big(\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\hskip-4.30554pt+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\big)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}&\hskip-4.30554pt\text{$p\geq 2$},\\
CM\big(\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}\hskip-4.30554pt+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\big)&\hskip-4.30554pt\text{$p<2$},\end{cases}

where

M=M(f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},g_{2})=\big(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\prime}}+\|g_{i}\|_{T^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{\prime}}\big)^{\frac{2-p}{p-1}}.

Proof.

First of all, we claim that the form
Q_{\beta}(\cdot,\cdot)
is coercive on
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. To prove this, it is sufficient to prove that there exists a constant
C=C(d,p,\Omega,\nu,\beta)>0
such that

\displaystyle Q_{\beta}(u,u)\geq C\|u\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\quad\text{for all }\penalty\ u\in W_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(8.23)

Assume
C
does not exist, then one finds a sequence
u_{n}\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
preferably
\|u_{n}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}=1
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u_{n},u_{n})+\int_{\Omega^{c}}|u_{n}(y)|^{p}\beta(y)\mathrm{d}y=Q_{\beta}(u_{n},u_{n})<\frac{1}{2^{n}}\,.

In virtue of the compactness of the embedding

W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
, the sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
converges up to a subsequence in
L^{p}(\Omega)
to some
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
. It turns out that
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
, since

\mathcal{E}(u_{n},u_{n})\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and for all
n\geq 1
,
\|u_{n}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}=1
. Moreover, Fatou’s lemma implies

\displaystyle\cE_{\Omega}(u,u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\cE(u_{n},u_{n})=0.

Given that
\nu
is of full support and
\mathcal{E}_{\Omega}(u,u)=0
it follows that
u
is constant almost everywhere on
\Omega
. Since
\mathcal{E}(u_{n},u_{n})\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
, we can extend
u
as a function that is constant almost everywhere on
\R^{d}
so that
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. That
\mathcal{E}(u_{n},u_{n})\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and
\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
imply that
\|u_{n}-u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
as we have
\mathcal{E}(u,u)=0
. On the other hand, since
\beta\omega^{-1}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{c})
and we have the continuous embedding
W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega^{c},\omega)
, by the nonlocal trace Theorem
5.15
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{c}}|u(y)|^{p}\beta(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\int_{\Omega^{c}}|u_{n}(y)|^{p}\beta(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+2^{p-1}\|\beta\omega^{-1}\|_{\infty}\int_{\Omega^{c}}|u_{n}(y)-u(y)|^{p}\omega(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}Q_{\beta}(u_{n},u_{n})+C\|u_{n}-u\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0\,.

It follows that
u=0
, since
u
is constant a.e and
|\Omega^{c}\cap\{\beta>0\}|>0
. This negates the fact that
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
and hence our initial assumption was wrong.
The other details follow analogously as for the Neumann problem (

V
′

), by replacing the form
\cE(\cdot,\cdot)
with
Q_{\beta}(\cdot,\cdot)
.
∎

9.
Transition from nonlocal to local

In this section we introduce and characterize what we name as
p
-Lévy approximation family; which serves as the main tool for the convergence of nonlocal objects to local ones. For instance, we show the convergence of nonlocal energy forms to local ones, as well as the pointwise convergence of nonlocal
p
-Lévy operators to the
p
-Laplacian.

9.1.
Basics on
p
-Lévy approximation family

A family of radial
p
-Lévy integrable kernels
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
,
\nu_{\varepsilon}:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty)
, is said to be a
p
-Lévy approximation family if it satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}\hskip-4.30554pt(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=1\,\,\text{and for all $\delta>0,$}\,\,\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|>\delta}\hskip-8.61108pt(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=0.

(9.1)

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
\beta\in\R
and
R>0
. Assume
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfies (
9.1
) then

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|\leq R}(1\land|h|^{p+\beta})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=\begin{cases}0\quad\text{ if}\quad\beta>0\\
1\quad\text{ if}\quad\beta=0\\
\infty\quad\text{if}\quad\beta<0.\end{cases}

Proof.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1\land R)
sufficiently small. By (
9.1
) we get

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{\delta<|h|\leq R}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|>\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|<\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=1-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|\geq\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=1.

The case
\beta=0
follows immediately.
If
\beta>0
then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\hskip-4.30554pt\int\limits_{|h|\leq R}\hskip-8.61108pt(1\land|h|^{p+\beta})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Big(R^{\beta}\hskip-12.91663pt\int\limits_{\delta<|h|\leq R}\hskip-12.91663pt\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h+\delta^{\beta}\hskip-8.61108pt\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta}\hskip-8.61108pt(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)\hskip-2.15277pt=\delta^{\beta}\hskip-4.30554pt.

Analogously, since
\delta<1
, if
\beta<0
then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\hskip-4.30554pt\int\limits_{|h|\leq R}\hskip-8.61108pt(1\land|h|^{p+\beta})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Big(\delta^{p}R^{\beta}\hskip-12.91663pt\int\limits_{\delta<|h|\leq R}\hskip-12.91663pt\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h+\delta^{\beta}\hskip-8.61108pt\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta}\hskip-8.61108pt(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)\hskip-2.15277pt=\hskip-2.15277pt\delta^{\beta}\hskip-4.30554pt.

In either case, letting
\delta\to 0
provides the claim.
∎

Remark 9.2
.

Assume the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfies (
9.1
). Note that the relation

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|>\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=0,

(9.2)

is often known as the concentration property and is merely equivalent to

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|>\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=0\quad\text{for all}\quad\delta>0.

Indeed, for all
\delta>0
we have

\displaystyle\int_{|h|>\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\leq\int_{|h|>\delta}\hskip-8.61108pt\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\leq(1\land\delta^{p})^{-1}\,\int_{|h|>\delta}\hskip-8.61108pt(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h.

Consequently, for all
\delta>0
we also have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|\leq\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|\leq\delta}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=1.\quad

(9.3)

Let us mention some prototypical examples of sequences
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfying (
9.1
) of interest here. For more examples we refer the reader to
[
55
,
54
,
56
]
.

Example 9.3
.

Assume
\nu
is radial and
p
-Lévy normalized, i.e.,

\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\,\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h=1.

Consider the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
defined as the rescaled version of
\nu
with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\begin{cases}\varepsilon^{-d-p}\nu\big(h/\varepsilon\big)&\text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ |h|\leq\varepsilon,\\
\varepsilon^{-d}|h|^{-p}\nu\big(h/\varepsilon\big)&\text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ \varepsilon<|h|\leq 1,\\
\varepsilon^{-d}\nu\big(h/\varepsilon\big)&\text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ |h|>1.\end{cases}\end{split}

Example 9.4
.

Consider the sequence
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
of fractional kernels defined by

\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=a_{\varepsilon,d,p}|h|^{-d-(1-\varepsilon)p}\quad\text{with }\quad a_{\varepsilon,d,p}=\frac{p\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}.

Indeed, passing through polar coordinates yields

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{(1\land|h|^{p})}{|h|^{-d-(1-\varepsilon)p}}\mathrm{d}h=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\hskip-4.30554ptr^{\varepsilon p-1}\,\mathrm{d}r+\hskip-4.30554pt\int_{1}^{\infty}\hskip-4.30554ptr^{-1-(1-\varepsilon)p}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)\hskip-2.15277pt=a^{-1}_{\varepsilon,d,p}.

For
\delta>0,
a similar computation gives

\displaystyle a_{\varepsilon,d,p}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})|h|^{-d-(1-\varepsilon)p}\,\mathrm{d}h\leq\varepsilon\delta^{-(1-\varepsilon)p}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0.

The choice of
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=a_{\varepsilon,d,p}|h|^{-d-(1-\varepsilon)p}
gives rise to a multiple of fractional
p
-Laplace operator, namely, we have
L_{\varepsilon}u=\frac{2a_{\varepsilon,d,p}}{C_{d,1-\varepsilon,p}}(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u
,
s=1-\varepsilon
. We emphasize that
C_{d,p,s}
(cf. Section
9.4
) is the normalizing constant of
(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}
and that
\frac{2a_{\varepsilon,d,p}}{C_{d,1-\varepsilon,p}}\to K_{d,p}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Example 9.5
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
and
\beta>-d
. Set

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{d+\beta}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\varepsilon^{d+\beta}}|h|^{\beta-p}\mathds{1}_{B_{\varepsilon}}(h).

Some special cases are obtained with
\beta=0
,
\beta=p
and
\beta=(1-s)p-d
for
s\in(0,1)
. For the limiting case
\beta=-d
consider
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}<1
and put

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\log(\varepsilon_{0}/\varepsilon)}|h|^{-d-p}\mathds{1}_{B_{\varepsilon_{0}}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}}(h).

9.2.
Characterization of
p
-Lévy approximation family

Now we characterize the class
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
such that for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
, the following formula holds

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x=K_{d,p}\int_{\R^{d}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x.

(9.4)

In fact, this is equivalent to saying that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\R^{d}}\hskip-8.61108pt(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=1\,\,\text{and for all $\delta>0,$}\,\,\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|>\delta}\hskip-8.61108pt\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=0.

(9.5)

To be more accurate, we have the following.

Theorem 9.6
.

Let
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
be a family of radial functions.
The following are equivalent.

(1)

The family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfies (
9.5
).

(2)

The relation (
9.4
) holds for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
.

(3)

The relation (
9.4
) holds for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
.

(4)

There exists
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})\setminus\{0\}
such that the relation (
9.4
) holds for each
u_{\tau}(x)=\tau^{d}u(\tau x)
,
\tau>0
.

Remark 9.7
.

It is worthwhile noticing that properties in (
9.1
) and (
9.5
) are equivalent in the sense that, using the normalizing factor
c_{\varepsilon}^{-1}=\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h
one readily recovers (
9.1
) from (
9.5
) vice-versa. Whence Theorem
9.6
also characterizes families satisfying (
9.1
).

Proof.

Up to replacing
\nu_{\varepsilon}
with
c_{\varepsilon}\nu_{\varepsilon}
with
c_{\varepsilon}^{-1}=\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h
, the implication
(i)\implies(ii)
follows from
[
55
]
or
[
54
, Theorem 5.23]
. We only prove that
(iv)\implies(i)
, as the remaining implications are trivial.
Note in passing that, since
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})\setminus\{0\}
we have
\|u_{\tau}\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}=\|u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\neq 0
and
\|\nabla u_{\tau}\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}=\tau^{1/p}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\neq 0
. By continuity of the shift operator, for every
\eta\in(0,1)
there is
0<\delta_{\eta}<\eta
such that
\|\nabla u(\cdot+h)-\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}<\eta
for all
|h|\leq\delta_{\eta}
. Thus for
\tau>0
we find that

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\tau}(\cdot+h)-\nabla u_{\tau}\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}<\tau^{1/p}\eta\quad\text{for all $|h|\leq\delta_{\eta}/\tau$.}

Minkowski’s inequality implies

\displaystyle\Big(\int_{\R^{d}}\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}

\displaystyle|\nabla u_{\tau}(x)\cdot h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}\leq\tau^{1/p}\eta\Big(\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\Big(\int_{\R^{d}}\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-0.86108pt\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u_{\tau}(x+th)\cdot h\mathrm{d}t\Big|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}.

Observe that, since
\nu_{\varepsilon}
is radial, using polar coordinates yields,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt|\nabla u(x)\cdot h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h&=K_{d,p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h.\end{split}

(9.6)

Accordingly, using the fundamental theorem of calculus, the formula (
4.3
) and the foregoing yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\,B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt|u_{\tau}(x)-u_{\tau}(x+h)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\int_{\R^{d}}\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u_{\tau}(x+th)\cdot h\mathrm{d}t\Big|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\geq\Big[\Big(\int_{\R^{d}}\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|\nabla u_{\tau}(x)\cdot h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\hskip-4.30554pt-\eta\Big(\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-0.43057pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)^{1/p}\Big]^{p}\\
&=\tau\Big(K^{1/p}_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}-\eta\Big)^{p}\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h.\end{split}

For the sake of brevity, let us put

\displaystyle\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\tau},u_{\tau})

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\,\R^{d}}|u_{\tau}(x)-u_{\tau}(x+h)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle R_{\tau}(\eta,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\,B^{c}_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt|u_{\tau}(x)-u_{\tau}(x+h)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x.

Therefore from the above, we find that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\tau},u_{\tau})&\geq\tau\Big(K^{1/p}_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}-\eta\Big)^{p}\hskip-4.30554pt\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-12.91663pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h+R_{\tau}(\eta,\varepsilon).\end{split}

(9.7)

Using once again the fundamental theorem of calculus and (
9.6
) we easily get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\tau},u_{\tau})&\leq\tau K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h+R_{\tau}(\eta,\varepsilon).\end{split}

(9.8)

Now we consider the following quantities

\displaystyle\rho_{\tau}^{+}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{\eta\to 0}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-17.22217pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h,\,\,\rho_{\tau}^{-}=\liminf_{\eta\to 0}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-17.22217pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h,

\displaystyle R_{\tau}^{+}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{\eta\to 0}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}R_{\tau}(\eta,\varepsilon)\quad\text{and}\quad R_{\tau}^{-}=\liminf_{\eta\to 0}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}R_{\tau}(\eta,\varepsilon).

Passing to the
\lim\hskip-2.15277pt\sup
and
\lim\hskip-2.15277pt\inf
in (
9.7
) and (
9.8
) respectively, we get the following

\displaystyle\tau K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}

\displaystyle\geq\tau\rho_{\tau}^{+}K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}+R_{\tau}^{+}

\displaystyle\tau K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\tau\rho_{\tau}^{-}K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}+R_{\tau}^{-}.

It follows that
\rho_{\tau}^{+}\leq 1
. To show that
\rho_{\tau}^{-}\geq 1
we observe that analogously to (
9.8
), for all
\delta>0
and
\theta>0
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\theta},u_{\theta})&\leq\theta K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\hskip-4.30554pt\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h+2^{p}\|u_{\theta}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\hskip-4.30554pt\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\hskip-8.61108pt\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h.\end{split}

Then passing to the
\lim\hskip-2.15277pt\inf
like previously also yields that

\displaystyle 1\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h+\frac{1}{\theta}\frac{2^{p}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}}{K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\hskip-4.30554pt\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h.

Letting
\theta\to\infty
implies that

\displaystyle 1\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\quad\text{for all $\delta>0$}.

In particular taking
\delta=\delta_{\eta}/\tau
we obtain

\displaystyle\rho_{\tau}^{-}=\liminf_{\eta\to 0}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\geq 1.

From the foregoing we get
\rho_{\tau}^{+}\leq 1\leq\rho_{\tau}^{-}
, that is, we have

\displaystyle\rho_{\tau}^{+}=\rho_{\tau}^{-}=\lim_{\eta\to 0}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{B_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=1.

(9.9)

Therefore, we also deduce that

\displaystyle R_{\tau}^{+}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{\eta\to 0}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\,B^{c}_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt|u_{\tau}(x)-u_{\tau}(x+h)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

For fixed
\delta>0
and
\eta<\delta\tau
we have

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\,B^{c}_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|u_{\tau}(x)-u_{\tau}(x+h)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\hskip-12.91663pt\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\,B^{c}_{\delta_{\eta}/\tau}(0)}\hskip-17.22217pt|u_{\tau}(x)-u_{\tau}(x+h)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x.

This implies that, for all
\delta>0
we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\,B^{c}_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt|u_{\tau}(x)-u_{\tau}(x+h)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

For fixed
\delta>0
and
\tau<\frac{\delta}{2}
so that
\supp u_{\tau}\subset B_{\delta/2}(0)
, if
x\in B_{\delta/2}(0)
and
h\in B^{c}_{\delta}(0)
we have
x+h\in B^{c}_{\delta/2}(0)
and hence
u_{\tau}(x+h)=0
. Therefore, it follows that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\,B^{c}_{\delta}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt|u_{\tau}(x)-u_{\tau}(x+h)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|\geq\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h.

Therefore we have,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|\geq\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=0\quad\text{for all $\delta>0$}.

Finally, combining this and (
9.9
), with
\tau=1
, it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=\lim_{\eta\to 0}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|<\delta_{\eta}}\hskip-5.16663pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=1.

The proof is now complete.
∎

9.3.
Pointwise asymptotic

In this section, we wish to establish the asymptotics of the nonlinear nonlocal operators
L_{\varepsilon}u
and
\cN_{\varepsilon}u
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, with

\displaystyle L_{\varepsilon}u(x)

\displaystyle=2\pv\int_{\R^{d}}\!\!\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y,\qquad\hbox{$(x\in\R^{d})$}

\displaystyle\cN_{\varepsilon}u(x)

\displaystyle=2\int_{\Omega}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\qquad\hbox{$(x\in\Omega^{c})$}.

Typically we show that
L_{\varepsilon}u
converges to
K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u
pointwise and in the weak sense, while
\cN_{\varepsilon}u
converges to
K_{d,p}\partial_{n,p}u=K_{d,p}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot n
in a (sort of) weak sense, where
n
is normal vector on
\partial\Omega
. In fact, here we extend the linear results
[
55
, Proposition 2.5]
and
[
52
, Lemma 5.3]
which only deals with the particular case
p=2
; see also
[
54
, Lemma 5.75 & Proposition 2.38]
wherein the asymptotic for general nonlocal elliptic operators is treated. To begin with, let us establish the following spherical mean representation for the
p
-Laplacian.

Lemma 9.8

(Spherical mean representation of
\Delta_{p}
)
.

Assume
u\in C^{2}(B_{1}(x))
and
\nabla u(x)\neq 0
when
1<p<2
then for
d\geq 2
we have

\displaystyle\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla u(x)\cdot w|^{p-2}D^{2}u(x)w\cdot w\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\frac{K_{d,p}}{p-1}\Delta_{p}u(x).

Proof.

If
p\geq 2
and
\nabla u(x)=0
, there is nothing to prove. Let us put
e(x)=\frac{\nabla u(x)}{|\nabla u(x)|}
and consider
O:\R^{d}\to\R^{d}
be a rotation, i.e.,
O^{t}O=I
such that
e(x)=Oe_{d}
with
e_{d}=(0,0,\cdots,1)
. Note that
Oz\cdot Oy=z\cdot y
. By rotational invariance of the Lebesgue measure, the change of variables
w=O\xi
yields
\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(\xi)
and

\displaystyle Iu(x)

\displaystyle:=\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla u(x)\cdot w|^{p-2}D^{2}u(x)w\cdot w\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle=|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|e(x)\cdot w|^{p-2}D^{2}u(x)w\cdot w\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle=|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\xi_{d}|^{p-2}[O^{t}D^{2}u(x)O]\xi\cdot\xi\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(\xi)

\displaystyle=|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}q_{ij}(x)\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w_{d}|^{p-2}w_{i}w_{j}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w).

Here
Q(x)=(q_{ij}(x))_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
is the
d\times d
-matrix
Q(x)=O^{t}D^{2}u(x)O
. By symmetry, we get

\displaystyle\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w_{d}|^{p-2}w_{i}w_{j}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=0\quad\text{if $i\neq j$}.

(9.10)

It is known from
[
55
]
that for
i=j=d
we have the following formula,

\displaystyle K_{d,p}

\displaystyle=\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w_{d}|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{p+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+p}{2}\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}\big)}.

Furthermore, the quantity
\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w_{d}|^{p-2}w^{2}_{j}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)
is oblivious to the choice of
j=1,2,\cdots,d-1
. Indeed, let
O^{\prime}:\R^{d-1}\to\R^{d-1}
be a rotation such that
O^{\prime}e_{j}^{\prime}=e_{1}^{\prime}
where we put
e_{i}=(e_{i}^{\prime},0)
and
x=(x^{\prime},x_{d})
. Then
x\mapsto Ox=(O^{\prime}x^{\prime},x_{d})
is a
x_{d}
-invariant rotation such that
Oe_{j}=e_{1}
. Enforcing the change of variables
\xi=Ow
,
\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(\xi)
we obtain

\displaystyle\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w_{d}|^{p-2}w_{j}^{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\xi_{d}|^{p-2}\xi_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(\xi).

Since
|w^{\prime}|^{2}=w_{1}^{2}+w_{2}^{2}+\cdots+w_{d-1}^{2}=1-|w_{d}|^{2}
for
w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},
this implies that

\displaystyle\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w_{d}|^{p-2}w_{j}^{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d-1}\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w_{d}|^{p-2}(1-|w_{d}|^{2})\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d-1}\big(K_{d,p-2}-K_{d,p}\big).

Applying the formula
\Gamma(x+1)=x\Gamma(x)
one readily obtains

\displaystyle K_{d,p-2}=\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{p-1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+p-2}{2}\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}\big)}=\frac{d+p-2}{p-1}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{p+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+p}{2}\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}\big)}=\frac{d+p-2}{p-1}K_{d,p}.

Inserting this into the previous expression yields

\displaystyle\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w_{d}|^{p-2}w_{j}^{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\begin{cases}\frac{K_{d,p}}{p-1}&\qquad j\neq d,\\
K_{d,p}&\qquad j=d.\end{cases}

(9.11)

Beside this, using
Tr(AB)=Tr(BA)
we observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}q_{jj}(x)

\displaystyle=Tr(Q(x))=Tr(O^{t}D^{2}u(x)O)=Tr(D^{2}u(x))=\Delta u(x).

(9.12)

In addition, since
Q(x)e_{d}\cdot e_{d}=D^{2}u(x)Oe_{d}\cdot Oe_{d}
and
\frac{\nabla u(x)}{|\nabla u(x)|}=e(x)=Oe_{d}
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}q_{dd}(x)=Q(x)e_{d}\cdot e_{d}&=|\nabla u(x)|^{-2}D^{2}u(x)\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla u(x)\\
&=|\nabla u(x)|^{-2}\Delta_{\infty}u(x).\end{split}

(9.13)

Combing (
9.10
), (
9.11
), (
9.12
) and (
9.13
) we find that

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}q_{ij}(x)\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\hskip-8.61108pt|w_{d}|^{p-2}w_{i}w_{j}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}q_{jj}(x)\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\hskip-8.61108pt|w_{d}|^{p-2}w^{2}_{j}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{K_{d,p}}{p-1}\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}q_{jj}(x)+q_{dd}(x)K_{d,p}=\frac{K_{d,p}}{p-1}\sum_{j=1}^{d}q_{jj}(x)+\frac{p-2}{p-1}q_{dd}(x)K_{d,p}

\displaystyle=\frac{K_{d,p}}{p-1}\big(Tr(Q(x))+(p-2)q_{dd}(x)\big)=\frac{K_{d,p}}{p-1}\big(\Delta u(x)+(p-2)\frac{\Delta_{\infty}u(x)}{|\nabla u(x)|^{2}}\big)

Finally, inserting this in the foregoing expression yields

\displaystyle Iu(x)

\displaystyle=|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}q_{ij}(x)\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w_{d}|^{p-2}w_{i}w_{j}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{K_{d,p}}{p-1}|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\big(\Delta u(x)+(p-2)|\nabla u(x)|^{-2}\Delta_{\infty}u(x)\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{K_{d,p}}{p-1}\operatorname{div}(|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\nabla u(x))=\frac{K_{d,p}}{p-1}\Delta_{p}u(x).

∎

It is worth mentioning that the computations yielding the identities (
9.12
) and (
9.13
) are essentially adapted from the computations in
[
67
, Section 7]
.

Theorem 9.9
.

Let
u\in L^{\infty}(\R^{d})\cap C^{2}(B_{\tau}(x))
for some
\tau>0
with
\nabla u(x)\neq 0
when
1<p<2
. Then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}L_{\varepsilon}u(x)=-K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u(x).

Proof.

First of all, for every
\delta>0
by boundedness of
u
we have

\displaystyle 2\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\hskip-8.61108pt\big|\psi(u(x+h)-u(x))\big|\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\leq 2^{p}\|u\|^{p-1}_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\hskip-8.61108pt\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0.

(9.14)

Since
\pv\int_{B_{1}(0)}\psi(\nabla u(x)\cdot h)\big]\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=0
, the claim reduces to the following

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}L_{\varepsilon}u(x)&=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}2\int_{|h|<\delta}\psi(u(x+h)-u(x))\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\\
&=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}2\int_{|h|<\delta}\big[\psi(\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h)-\psi(\nabla u(x)\cdot h)\big]\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\\
&=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}2\int_{|h|<\delta}\psi^{\prime}(a)(b-a)+R(a,b)\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h,\end{split}

where, using the fundamental theorem
u(x+h)-u(x)=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h\mathrm{d}t
, we put

\displaystyle a=\nabla u(x)\cdot h\qquad\text{and}\qquad b=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h\,\mathrm{d}t.

Furthermore,
\psi^{\prime}(t)=(p-1)|t|^{p-2}
and the remainder
R(a,b)
is given by

\displaystyle R(a,b)=\psi(b)-\psi(a)-\psi^{\prime}(a)(b-a)=(b-a)\hskip-2.15277pt\int_{0}^{1}\hskip-4.30554pt\psi^{\prime}(a+t(b-a))-\psi^{\prime}(a)\mathrm{d}t.

Without loss of generality, assume
\tau=1
, i.e.,
u\in C^{2}(B_{1}(x))
. For fixed
0<\eta<1
there is
0<\delta<1
such that

\displaystyle|D^{2}u(x+h)-D^{2}u(x)|<\eta\qquad\text{whenever $|h|<\delta$}.

(9.15)

The fundamental theorem of calculus yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}b-a&=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h-\nabla u(x)\cdot h\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{2}\big[D^{2}u(x)\cdot h\big]\cdot h\\
&+\int_{0}^{1}t\int_{0}^{1}\big[D^{2}u(x+sth)\cdot h-D^{2}u(x)\cdot h\big]\cdot h\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

(9.16)

In particular, the above implies

\displaystyle|b-a|\leq C_{0}|h|^{2},\qquad C_{0}=\sup_{z\in B_{1}(x)}|D^{2}u(z)|.

Next we estimate the remainder
R(a,b)
, by distinguishing 3 cases:
1<p<2
,
2<p<3
and
p\geq 3
. Observe in passing that
R(a,b)=0
when
p=2
. To this end, let
h\in B_{\delta}(0)
.

Case:
1<p<2
.
In this case,
\nabla u(x)\neq 0
and
\psi(t)=|t|^{p-2}t
is
C^{1}
on
\R\setminus\{0\}
so that

\displaystyle\lim_{b\to a}\frac{\psi(b)-\psi(a)-\psi^{\prime}(a)(b-a)}{b-a}=0.

Set
\sigma=2-p>0
. Since
b\to a
as
h\to 0
, for
\nabla u(x)\cdot h\neq 0
and
0<\delta<1
small, we get

\displaystyle|R(a,b)|\leq|b-a|\leq C_{0}|h|^{2}=C_{0}|h|^{p+\sigma}.

Case:
2<p<3
.
Since
1<p-1<2
, the estimate (
A.2
), from Appendix
A
infers that

\big||b|^{p-2}-|a|^{p-2}\big|\leq 2^{4-p}|b-a|^{p-2}.

Set
\sigma=p-2>0
. Recall that
\psi^{\prime}(t)=(p-1)|t|^{p-2}
, for all
|h|<\delta
, we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}|R(a,b)|&\leq|b-a|\int_{0}^{1}|\psi^{\prime}(a+t(b-a))-\psi^{\prime}(a)|\mathrm{d}t\leq(p-1)2^{4-p}|b-a|^{p-1}\\
&\leq C_{1}|h|^{2(p-1)}=C_{1}|h|^{p+\sigma},\quad\text{$C_{1}=(p-1)2^{4-p}C^{p-1}_{0}$}.\end{split}

Case:
p\geq 3
.
Since
p-1\geq 2
, the estimate (
A.1
) (see Appendix
A
) implies

\displaystyle||b|^{p-2}-|a|^{p-2}|\leq(p-2)|b-a|(|b|^{p-3}+|a|^{p-3})\leq C_{3}|b-a||h|^{p-3}.

with
C_{3}=2(p-2)\sup_{z\in B_{1}(x)}|\nabla u(z)|^{p-3}.
It follows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}|R(a,b)|&\leq|b-a|\int_{0}^{1}|\psi^{\prime}(a+t(b-a))-\psi^{\prime}(a)|\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq C_{3}(p-1)|b-a|^{2}|h|^{p-3}\leq C_{4}|h|^{p+1},\quad C_{4}=C^{2}_{0}C_{3}(p-1).\end{split}

Altogether for
\sigma=|p-2|
if
1<p<3
and
\sigma=1
if
p\geq 3
and for
0<\delta<1
small, we have shown that

\displaystyle|R(a,b)|\leq\begin{cases}C|h|^{p+\sigma}&\quad 0<|p-2|<1,\\
0&\quad p=2,\\
C|h|^{p+1}&\quad p\geq 3.\end{cases}

The case
1<p<2
is understood in almost everywhere sense, since
\nabla u(x)\neq 0
so that
|B_{\delta}(0)\cap\{\nabla u(x)\cdot z=0\}|=0
. Applying Proposition
9.1
then in any case, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|<\delta}R(a,b)\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\leq C\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|<\delta}|h|^{p+\sigma}\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=0.

(9.17)

From the foregoing, combining (
9.16
) and (
9.17
) we find that

\displaystyle-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}

\displaystyle L_{\varepsilon}u(x)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}2\int_{|h|<\delta}\psi^{\prime}(a)(b-a)\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}2\int_{|h|<\delta}\hskip-8.61108pt\Big(\psi^{\prime}(\nabla u(x)\cdot h)\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h-\nabla u(x)\cdot h\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|<\delta}\big(\psi^{\prime}(\nabla u(x)\cdot h)D^{2}u(x)h\cdot h

\displaystyle+\psi^{\prime}(\nabla u(x)\cdot h)R_{1}(x,h)h\cdot h\big)\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h.

Here the remainder
R_{1}(x,h)
is the matrix defined by

\displaystyle R_{1}(x,h)h\cdot h=2\int_{0}^{1}t\int_{0}^{1}\big[D^{2}u(x+sth)\cdot h-D^{2}u(x)\cdot h\big]\cdot h\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t.

It clearly occurs that
|\nabla u(x)\cdot h|^{p-2}h|\leq|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}|h|^{p-1}
with
\nabla u(x)\neq 0
for
1<p<2
. Hence for
|h|<\delta
, (
9.15
) yields

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}2\int_{|h|<\delta}\psi^{\prime}(\nabla u(x)\cdot h)R_{1}(x,h)h\cdot h\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle\leq\eta(p-1)|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|<\delta}\hskip-8.61108pt|h|^{p}\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\leq\eta(p-1)|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\xrightarrow{\eta\to 0}0.

Since
\eta>0
is arbitrarily chosen, we deduce that

\displaystyle-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}L_{\varepsilon}u(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|<\delta}\psi^{\prime}(\nabla u(x)\cdot h)D^{2}u(x)h\cdot h\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

(9.18)

The case
d=1
follows immediately by exploiting (
9.18
). Now assume
d\geq 2
. Since
\nu_{\varepsilon}
is radial, using the polar coordinates in (
9.18
) and Proposition
9.1
yields

\displaystyle-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}L_{\varepsilon}u(x)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}(p-1)\int_{|h|<\delta}|\nabla u(x)\cdot h|^{p-2}D^{2}u(x)h\cdot h\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}(p-1)\int_{0}^{\delta}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla u(x)\cdot w|^{p-2}D^{2}u(x)w\cdot w\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\,r^{d+p-1}\nu_{\varepsilon}(r)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{(p-1)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla u(x)\cdot w|^{p-2}D^{2}u(x)w\cdot w\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|h|<\delta}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\frac{(p-1)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla u(x)\cdot w|^{p-2}D^{2}u(x)w\cdot w\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w).

The desired result follows from the spherical representation of
\Delta_{p}
(see Lemma
9.8
), viz.,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}L_{\varepsilon}u(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(p-1)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla u(x)\cdot w|^{p-2}D^{2}u(x)w\cdot w\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle=-K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u(x).

∎

Using (
9.14
) and (
9.16
), one obtains the following straightforward variants of Theorem
9.9
.

Theorem 9.10
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open and
\delta>0
. If
u\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)\cap C^{2}(B_{\tau}(x))
,
x\in\Omega
,
0<\tau<\dist(x,\partial\Omega)
, with
\nabla u(x)\neq 0
when
1<p<2
, then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}L_{\Omega,\varepsilon}u(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}L_{\Omega,\delta,\varepsilon}u(x)=-K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u(x)

where
L_{\Omega,\varepsilon}
is the regional operator and
L_{\Omega,\delta,\varepsilon}
is the constrained operator on
\Omega
, respectively defined by

\displaystyle L_{\Omega,\varepsilon}u(x)

\displaystyle=2\pv\int_{\Omega}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y,

\displaystyle L_{\Omega,\delta,\varepsilon}u(x)

\displaystyle=2\pv\int_{\Omega\cap B(x,\delta)}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y.

Note that if
\delta=\delta_{x}:=\dist(x,\partial\Omega)
, the constrained operator merely becomes

\displaystyle L_{\Omega,\delta_{x},\varepsilon}u(x)

\displaystyle=2\pv\int_{B(x,\delta_{x})}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y.

Note that by definition,
\cN_{\varepsilon}u(x)=L_{\Omega,\varepsilon}u(x)
for all
x\in\R^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
, hence (
9.14
) readily implies the following.

Theorem 9.11
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open. If
u:\R^{d}\to\R
is measurable and
u|_{\Omega}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cN_{\varepsilon}u(x)=0\qquad\text{for $x\in\R^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega}$}.

Let us point out some particular cases of Theorem
9.9
already appeared in the literature, viz.,
[
24
, Theorem 2.8]
,
[
32
, Corollary 6.2]
and the variant in
[
67
, Section 7]
.

Corollary 9.12

(
[
67
, Section 7]
,
[
24
, Theorem 2.8]
)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
9.9
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1}2s(1-s)\pv\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{\psi(u(x)-u(y))}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y=-\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{p}K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u(x).

Proof.

Apply Theorem
9.9
with
\varepsilon=1-s
and
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{p\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}|h|^{-d-(1-\varepsilon)p}
.
∎

Corollary 9.13

(
[
32
, Corollary 6.3]
)
.

If
u\in C^{2}(B_{\tau}(x))
,
\tau>0
then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{d+p}}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(x)}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-2}(u(x)-u(y))\mathrm{d}y=-\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{d+p}K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u(x).

Proof.

Apply Theorem
9.9
with
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{(d+p)\varepsilon^{-d-p}}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\mathds{1}_{B_{\varepsilon}(0)}(h)
.
∎

9.4.
A normalization constant for the fractional
p
-Laplacian

In light of Corollary
9.12
we define a suitable normalizing constant
C_{d,p,s}
for the fractional
p
-Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}
such that
C_{d,2,s}
is the normalizing constant of the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{s}
and that for
u\in L^{\infty}(\R^{d})\cap C^{2}(B_{1}(x))
we have
(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u(x)\xrightarrow{s\to 1}-\Delta_{p}u(x)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u(x):=C_{d,p,s}\pv\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{\psi(u(x)-u(y))}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y\xrightarrow{s\to 1}-\Delta_{p}u(x).

(9.19)

In view of Corollary
9.12
we find that

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1}2s(1-s)B_{p}\pv\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{\psi(u(x)-u(y))}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y=-\Delta_{p}u(x).

where, since
|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma(d/2)}
and
\Gamma(1/2)=\pi^{1/2},
the constant
B_{p}
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B_{p}}:=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{p}K_{d,p}=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{p\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{p+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+p}{2}\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}\big)}=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\Gamma\big(\frac{p+1}{2}\big)}{p\Gamma\big(\frac{d+p}{2}\big)}.

On the other hand, by
[
54
, Proposition 2.21]
the normalizing constant of the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{s}
is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{d,2,s}}=\frac{\pi^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\Gamma(\frac{2s+1}{2})\Gamma(2(1-s))}{s(1-2s)\Gamma(\frac{d+2s}{2})}\cos(s\pi).

Alternatively, be aware that a common representation formula of
C_{d,2,s}
is as follows

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{d,2,s}}=\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}\Gamma(1-s)}{s2^{2s}\Gamma(\frac{d+2s}{2})}=\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}|\Gamma(-s)|}{2^{2s}\Gamma(\frac{d+2s}{2})}.

Our normalizing constant, mimics the first expression of
\frac{1}{C_{d,2,s}}
and
\frac{1}{B_{p}}
. To wit, we define

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{d,p,s}}=\frac{\pi^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\Gamma(\frac{sp+1}{2})\Gamma(p(1-s))}{s(1-2s)\Gamma(\frac{d+sp}{2})}\cos(s\pi).

Namely, taking also into account the two expressions of
C_{d,2,s}
yielding a formula for
\cos(s\pi)
we have

\displaystyle C_{d,p,s}\hskip-2.15277pt=\hskip-2.15277pt\frac{sp(1-s)(1-2s)\Gamma(\frac{d+sp}{2})}{\pi^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\Gamma(\frac{sp+1}{2})\Gamma(p(1-s)+1)\hskip-2.15277pt\cos(s\pi)}\hskip-2.15277pt=\hskip-4.30554pt\frac{2^{2s}\,\Gamma(\frac{d+sp}{2})\Gamma(\frac{2s+1}{2})\Gamma(2(1-s))}{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}|\Gamma(-s)|\,\Gamma(\frac{sp+1}{2})\Gamma(p(1-s))}.

Clearly the relation (
9.19
) holds true since the asymptotic
s\to 1
can be rewritten with help of

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1}\frac{C_{d,p,s}}{2s(1-s)B_{p}}=1.

We immediately get the following asymptotic behaviors

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{C_{d,p,s}}{s(1-s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\Gamma(p)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{s\to 1}\frac{C_{d,p,s}}{s(1-s)}=\frac{2p}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|K_{d,p}}.

(9.20)

The above asymptotic perfectly aligns with the case
p=2
, as shown in
[
34
]
. We emphasize that the other normalizing constants for the fractional
p
-Laplacian are proposed in
[
31
,
97
]
and the constant
K_{d,p}
plays a crucial role in our asymptotic analysis. For the sake of completeness, it is natural to study the asymptotic near
s=0
. To this end, one possibility is to modify
C_{d,p,s}
near
s=0
replacing it with
C_{d,2,\frac{sp}{2}}
. The constant
C_{d,2,\frac{sp}{2}}
behaves nicely near
s=0
and might diverge near
s=1
3
3

3

Using the Euler’s reflection formula
\Gamma(\varepsilon-m)\Gamma(m+1-\varepsilon)=(-1)^{m}\Gamma(\varepsilon)\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, one derives that

(1-s)|\Gamma(-\frac{sp}{2})|\to\frac{2}{p\Gamma(\frac{p+2}{2})}
if
\frac{p}{2}\in\mathbb{N}
; otherwise,
(1-s)|\Gamma(-\frac{sp}{2})|\to 0
.
and we have

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{C_{d,2,\frac{sp}{2}}}{s(1-s)}=\frac{p}{2|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\lim_{s\to 1}\frac{C_{d,2,\frac{sp}{2}}}{s(1-s)}=\begin{cases}\infty&\frac{p}{2}\notin\mathbb{N}\\
\frac{2^{p-1}p\Gamma(\frac{p+2}{2})\Gamma(\frac{d+p}{2})}{\pi^{d/2}}&\frac{p}{2}\in\mathbb{N}.\end{cases}

It is straightforward to verify that, granted with the constant
\widetilde{C}_{d,p,s}

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{d,p,s}=\begin{cases}C_{d,p,s}&\text{if $sp\geq 1$}\\
C_{d,2,\frac{sp}{2}}&\text{if $sp<1$},\end{cases}

hence a fortiori
C_{d,p,s}
and
C_{d,2,\frac{sp}{2}}
, the following properties are guaranteed.

(1)

For
p=2
, we find that
\widetilde{C}_{d,p,s}=C_{d,p,s}=C_{d,2,\frac{sp}{2}}=C_{d,2,s}
is the normalizing constant of the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{s}
.

(2)

For any
u\in L^{\infty}(\R^{d})\cap C^{2}(B_{1}(x))

we have
(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u(x)\xrightarrow{s\to 1}-\Delta_{p}u(x)
, in fact

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1}\frac{\widetilde{C}_{d,p,s}}{2}\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{\psi(u(x)-u(y))}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=-\Delta_{p}u(x).

(3)

For any
u\in L^{\infty}(\R^{d})\cap C^{\varepsilon}(B_{1}(x))
,
\varepsilon>0
we have
(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u(x)\xrightarrow{s\to 0}|u(x)|^{p-2}u(x)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{\widetilde{C}_{d,p,s}}{2}\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{\psi(u(x)-u(y))}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=|u(x)|^{p-2}u(x).

(4)

For any
u\in L^{p}(\R^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1}\frac{\widetilde{C}_{d,p,s}}{2}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\R^{d}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x.

(5)

For any
u\in\bigcup_{s\in(0,1)}W^{s,p}_{0}(\R^{d})
, see
[
76
]
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{\widetilde{C}_{d,p,s}}{2}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x.

(6)

Moreover, we have the following asymptotic behaviors

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{\widetilde{C}_{d,p,s}}{s(1-s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{2|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{s\to 1}\frac{\widetilde{C}_{d,p,s}}{s(1-s)}=\frac{2p}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|K_{d,p}}.

(9.21)

The asymptotic
s\to 1
, highlighting the factor
K_{d,p}
is already anticipated in
[
54
, Eq: 2.38]
in the case
p=2
. Despite the amusing fact of this asymptotic, it is important for the reader must keep in mind that
C_{d,p,s}
is purely artificial and that only the case
p=2
naturally appears as the unique normalizing constant for which
\widehat{(-\Delta)^{s}u}(\xi)=|\xi|^{2s}\widehat{u}(\xi)
for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
.

9.5.
Convergence of forms

We are interested in the asymptotic of the energy forms

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(y)-u(x)|^{p-2}(u(y)-u(x))(v(y)-v(x))\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\hskip-12.91663pt\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}|u(y)-u(x)|^{p-2}(u(y)-u(x))(v(y)-v(x))\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}_{+}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}|u(y)-u(x)|^{p-2}(u(y)-u(x))(v(x)-v(y))\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}_{cr}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}|u(y)-u(x)|^{p-2}(u(y)-u(x))(v(x)-v(y))\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{0}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla v(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Note in passing that
\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(u,v)=\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}_{+}(u,v)=\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(u,v)
when
\Omega=\R^{d}
. Moreover, by
[
54
, Theorem 5.23]
,
[
55
, Theorem 1.3]
see also the variant in
[
22
,
15
]
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}_{\R^{d}}(u,u)=K_{d,p}\,\int_{\R^{d}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x.

(9.22)

Note that since
\frac{C_{d,p,s}}{2s(1-s)}\xrightarrow{s\to 1}\frac{p}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}
, see the asymptotic in (
9.20
), for the standard fractional case

\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{C_{d,p,s}}{2}|h|^{-d-sp}
,
s=1-\varepsilon
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1}\frac{C_{d,p,s}}{2}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\R^{d}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x.

In short, up to a multiplicative factor, we have
|u|_{W^{s,p}(\R^{d})}\xrightarrow{s\to 1}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}
. Next, we need the following result involving the collapse across the boundary.

Lemma 9.14
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
satisfies
|\partial\Omega|=0
. For any
u,v\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

Proof.

Consider
U_{\delta}=\{x\in\R^{d}:\dist(x,\Omega)>\delta\}
,
\delta>0
, so that
U_{\delta}\subset\Omega^{c}
. Since
\Omega
and
U_{\delta}
are open, by
[
55
, Theorem 3.3]
or
[
54
, Theorem 5.16]
we find that

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(u,u),

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\int_{U_{\delta}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE^{\varepsilon}_{U_{\delta}}(u,u)\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega^{c}}(u,u).

By the convergence dominated theorem we obtain

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega^{c}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega^{c}}(u,u).

Accordingly, since
|\partial\Omega|=0
, together with (
9.22
), we get the claim as follows

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\big(\cE^{\varepsilon}_{\R^{d}}(u,u)-\cE^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(u,u)-\cE^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega^{c}}(u,u)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE^{\varepsilon}_{\R^{d}}(u,u)-\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(u,u)-\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega^{c}}(u,u)

\displaystyle\leq K_{d,p}\Big(\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}-\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}-\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega^{c})}\Big)=0.

The case
u\neq v
follows this by applying the Hölder inequality.
∎

Next we combine the ideas of
[
55
, Theorem 1.5]
and
[
48
, Lemma 2.8]
.

Theorem 9.15
.

Assume that
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open satisfying
(i)
or
(ii)
,

(1)

\Omega
is an
W^{1,p}
-extension domain,

(2)

\partial\Omega=\partial\overline{\Omega}
and
\R^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
is an
W^{1,p}
-extension domain.

Then for
u,v\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(u,v)=K_{d,p}\,\mathcal{E}^{0}(u,v),

(9.23)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(u,v)=K_{d,p}\,\mathcal{E}^{0}(u,v),

(9.24)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}_{+}(u,v)=K_{d,p}\,\mathcal{E}^{0}(u,v).

(9.25)

Proof.

The elementary inequality
|b|^{p}-|a|^{p}-p|a|^{p-2}a(b-a)\geq 0
(see Corollary
A.5
) yields, for
t>0
and
\sigma\in\R
,

\displaystyle\cE_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(u+t\sigma v,u+t\sigma v)-\cE_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(u,u)-pt\cE_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(u,\sigma v)\geq 0.

If
\Omega
is a
W^{1,p}
-extension domain then
\cE^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(w,w)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}K_{d,p}\cE^{0}(w,w)
for
w\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
; see
[
54
, Theorem 5.23]
,
[
55
, Theorem 1.3]
or the variant in
[
15
]
. Accordingly, passing to the
\lim\hskip-2.15277pt\sup
yields

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\frac{\|\nabla(u+t\sigma v)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}-\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}}{t}\geq p\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(u,\sigma v).

Letting
t\to 0
, with
\sigma=\pm 1
yields
\sigma pK_{d,p}\cE^{0}(u,v)\geq p\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sigma\cE_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(u,v)
and hence

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(u,v)=K_{d,p}\cE^{0}(u,v).

This remains true with
\Omega
replaced by
\R^{d}
or by
\R^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
when
\R^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
is a
W^{1,p}
-extension domain. Note that in either case, we have
|\partial\Omega|=|\partial\overline{\Omega}|=0
since the boundary of an extension domain is a null set. Thus, all claims follow by combing Lemma
9.14
and the situation where
\R^{d}
,
\Omega
and/or
\R^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
is a
W^{1,p}
-extension domain. Indeed,
(i)
follows since
(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}=\Omega\times\Omega\cup\Omega\times\Omega^{c}\cup\Omega^{c}\times\Omega
and
\Omega\times\R^{d}=\Omega\times\Omega\cup\Omega\times\Omega^{c}
. The case
(ii)
follows analogously since
\Omega\times\Omega=(\R^{d}\times\R^{d})\setminus[\Omega^{c}\times\Omega\cup\Omega\times\Omega^{c}\cup\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c}]
and
(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}=(\R^{d}\times\R^{d})\setminus(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})
.
∎

As illustrated in the next result,
\partial\Omega
need not be regular if
u
or
v
vanishes on
\partial\Omega
.

Theorem 9.16
.

Let
u,v\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
and
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be any open set. If
u\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
or
v\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
then the convergences (
9.23
), (
9.24
) and (
9.25
) hold.

Proof.

By density it is sufficient to assume
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
or
v\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. In any case
\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla v(x)=0
a.e.
on
\Omega^{c}
and
(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))=0,
a.e. on

\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c}
. The result follows by combining Lemma
9.14
and Theorem
9.15
for
\Omega=\R^{d}
, i.e.,
\cE^{\varepsilon}_{\R^{d}}(u,v)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}K_{d,p}\cE^{0}(u,v).

∎

Theorem 9.17

(
[
54
, Theorem 3.37]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open. Let
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\subset L^{p}(\Omega)
and
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\Big(\|u_{\varepsilon}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{\varepsilon}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)<\infty.

If
\|u_{\varepsilon}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0
then
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and we have

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{\varepsilon}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

A refinement of
[
54
, Theorem 5.35 & 5.40]
and also
[
84
]
or
[
1
, Section 4]
yields the following result.

Theorem 9.18

(Asymptotic compactness)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open. Let
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\subset L^{p}(\Omega)
be such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\Big(\|u_{\varepsilon}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{\varepsilon}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)<\infty.

There exist
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and a subsequence
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n}
with
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0^{+}
as
n\to\infty
such that
(u_{\varepsilon_{n}})_{n}
converges to
u
in
L^{p}_{\loc}(\Omega)
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{\varepsilon}(x)-u_{\varepsilon}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

In addition, we have the following strong convergences.

(1)

If
\Omega
bounded and Lipschitz then we have
\|u_{\varepsilon_{n}}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
,

(2)

If
\Omega=\R^{d}
then we have
\|u_{\varepsilon_{n}}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega^{\prime})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
whenever
|\Omega^{\prime}|<\infty
.

10.
Robust Poincaré and Poincaré-Friedrichs inequalities

In this section, we establish
robust Poincaré type inequalities
. The robustness should be understood in the sense that within such inequalities, one can recover the corresponding classical local Poincaré inequalities. In this section
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is as in Section
9
, i.e., satisfies (
9.1
).

10.1.
Robust Poincaré inequality

Note that a set
G
is called homogeneous, if
\lambda u\in G
for
u\in G
and
\lambda\in\R
.

Theorem 10.1

(Robust Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be bounded Lipschitz and connected. Let
G\subset L^{p}(\Omega)
be a nonempty and homogeneous satisfying:

G
is closed in
L^{p}(\Omega)
,
1\not\in G
and
\lambda u\in G
for
\lambda\in\R,u\in G
.

(10.1)

There exist
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(d,p,\Omega,G)>0
and
B=B(d,p,\Omega,G)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq B\cE_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(u,u)\quad\text{for all $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$ and $u\in G$.}

(10.2)

For
0<\gamma\leq|\Omega|
and
E\subset\Omega
measurable such that
|E|>0
, obvious examples of sets
G
satisfying (
10.1
) include:

•

G_{1}=\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega)\,:\,|\{u=0\}|\geq\gamma\}
,

•

G_{2}=\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega)\,:\,\mbox{$\fint_{E}$}u=0\}
,

•

G_{3}=\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega)\,:\,u=0\,\mbox{ a.e. on }\,E\}
.

Proof.

Assume no such
\varepsilon_{0}
and
B
exist. For each
n\geq 1
taking
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}
and
B=2^{n}
there exist
\varepsilon_{n}\in(0,\frac{1}{2^{n}})
and
u_{n}\in G
for which (
10.2
) fails, i.e.,
\|u_{n}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}>2^{n}\cE_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})
.
By the homogeneity condition (
10.1
), we can assume without loss of generality that
u_{n}\in G
,
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
so that
\cE_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})\leq\frac{1}{2^{n}}
.
According to Theorem
9.18
there is
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and a subsequence still denoted
(u_{n})_{n}
converging to
u
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\cE_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})=0.

This implies that
\nabla u=0
almost everywhere on
\Omega
, which is a connected set. Necessarily,
u=c
is a constant function. We find that
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
, hence
u=c\neq 0
and
u\in G
since
G
is closed in
L^{p}(\Omega)
and
\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
. By homogeneity of G, we have
c^{-1}u=1\in G
. But by assumption, we know that
1\not\in G.
We have reached a contradiction.
∎

Here is a direct consequence of Theorem
10.1
; see also
[
84
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Corollary 10.2
.

There exist
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(d,p,\Omega)>0
and
B=B(d,p,\Omega)>0
such that, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq B\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.

It suffices to take
G=\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega)\;:\,\int_{\Omega}u=0\}
in Theorem
10.1
.
∎

Noting that the constant
B=B(d,p,\Omega,G)
is independent of
\varepsilon>0
, a noteworthy consequence of Theorem
10.1
is obtained letting
\varepsilon\to 0
; using Theorem
9.15
,
[
55
, Theorem 1.3]
or
[
54
, Theorem 5.23]
, we recover the classical Poincaré type inequality.

Corollary 10.3
.

Under the conditions and notations of Theorem
10.1
we have

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq BK_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for every $u\in G$.}

(10.3)

Corollary 10.4
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open, bounded, Lipschitz and connected. There exists
C=C(p,d,\Omega)>0
such that,
for all
s\in(0,1)
,
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\big\|u-\hbox{ $\fint_{\Omega}$}u\big\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C(1-s)\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,dy\,dx.

(10.4)

When
\Omega=Q
is a cube, the robust inequality (
10.4
) is also proved in
[
16
]
and improved in
[
76
,
63
]
. The approaches therein use techniques from harmonic analysis.

Proof.

Take,
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{p\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}|h|^{-d-(1-\varepsilon)p}
where we put
\varepsilon=1-s
. By Theorem
10.1
, there exist
s_{0}\in(0,1)
and
C=B>0
such that the inequality (
10.4
) holds for all
s\in(s_{0},1)
and
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
. Now, let
R-1=\operatorname{diam}(\Omega)
be the diameter of
\Omega
so that
R\geq 1
. For
s\in(0,s_{0})
we have
1-s_{0}<1-s
and
|x-y|^{-d-sp}\geq R^{-d-s_{0}p}
for
all
x,y\in\Omega
. This, together with Jensen’s inequality yield

\displaystyle(1-s)\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq(1-s_{0})R^{-d-s_{0}p}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\mathrm{d}y\,dx

\displaystyle\geq(1-s_{0})R^{-d-s_{0}p}|\Omega|\int_{\Omega}\big|u(x)-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}u\big|^{p}\,dx.

Up to a relabeling , it suffices to take
C=\max(B,\frac{R^{d+s_{0}p}}{|\Omega|(1-s_{0})}).

∎

The following variant of Theorem
10.1
, encapsulates a sort of double robustness (bi-robustness) in parameters for the fractional type Poincaré inequality

Theorem 10.5

(Double robustness for fractional Poincaré inequality)
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
10.1
, there exist
B=B(d,p,\Omega)>0
,
r_{0}=r_{0}(d,p,\Omega)>0
and
s_{0}=s_{0}(d,p,\Omega)>0
such that, for every
r\in(0,r_{0})
,
s\in(s_{0},1)
and
u\in G
we have

\displaystyle\|u-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq B\frac{(1-s)}{r^{p(1-s)}}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathds{1}_{B_{r}(0)}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

(10.5)

Remark 10.6
.

The double robustness (bi-robustness) in (
10.5
) is well understood, since letting
s\to 1
and/or
r\to 0
in (
10.5
) one recovers the local Poincaré inequality (
10.3
). Indeed, consider

\displaystyle\rho_{r,s}(h)=p(1-s)r^{-p(1-s)}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{-1}|h|^{-d-sp}\mathds{1}_{B_{r}(0)}(h).

(10.6)

Then
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\rho_{\varepsilon,s}(h)
for fixed
s\in(0,1)
or
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\rho_{r,1-\varepsilon}(h)
for fixed
r>0
satisfies the condition (
9.1
). More importantly,
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\rho_{\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon}(h)
also satisfies the condition (
9.1
).

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of Theorem
10.1
with the slight
difference that
\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)
is replaced by
\rho_{r_{n},1-\varepsilon_{n}}
,
r_{n},\varepsilon_{n}\in(0,\frac{1}{2^{n}})
, with
\rho_{r,s}
given in (
10.6
).
Indeed, just as for
\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}
, it is easy to check that,
\rho_{r_{n},1-\varepsilon_{n}}
is Dirac p-Lévy approximation sequence in the sense of (
9.1
).
∎

10.2.
Robust Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality

The analogous robust Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality, is delicate and requires a different slightly formulation. Here we identify
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
as a natural subspace of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
. Next, we deal with the situation where
\Omega
is bounded in only one direction.

Theorem 10.7

(Robust Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is bounded in one direction, say
\Omega\subset H_{R}
with
H_{R}=\{x\in\R^{d}\,:\,|x\cdot e|\leq R\}
with
R>0
and
|e|=1
.
There exist
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(d,p,\Omega)
and
B=B(d,p,\Omega)
such that, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq B\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

(10.7)

Proof.

First proof
.
Assume no such
\varepsilon_{0}
and
B
exist. For
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}
and
B=2^{n}
,
n\geq 1
, there exist
\varepsilon_{n}\in(0,\frac{1}{2^{n}})
and
u_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
for which (
10.2
) fails, i.e.,
\|u_{n}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}>2^{n}\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})
.
