We can assume without loss of generality that
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
so that
\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})\leq\frac{1}{2^{n}}
.
Since
u_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we find
x_{n}\in\Omega
and
r_{n}>0
such that
\supp u_{n}\subset\overline{B_{2r_{n}}(x_{n})}\subset\Omega
. Next, we have
z_{n}=x_{n}+2r_{n}e\in\overline{B_{2r_{n}}(x_{n})}\subset\Omega\subset H_{R}
. We find that

\displaystyle R\geq z_{n}\cdot e=2r_{n}+x_{n}\cdot e\geq 2r_{n}-R\quad\text{hence,}\quad r_{n}\leq R.

That is, we have
\supp u_{n}\subset\overline{B_{R}(x_{n})}
. Set
\tau_{x_{n}}u_{n}(x):=u_{n}(x+x_{n})
then
\tau_{x_{n}}u_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{2R}(0))
and
\tau_{x_{n}}u_{n}=0
on
B_{2R}(0)\setminus B_{R}(0)
. Since
u_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
we have

\displaystyle\|\tau_{x_{n}}u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(B_{2R}(0))}

\displaystyle=\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(B_{2r_{n}}(x_{n}))}=\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1,

\displaystyle\cE_{B_{2R}(0)}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(\tau_{x_{n}}u_{n},\tau_{x_{n}}u_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(\tau_{x_{n}}u_{n},\tau_{x_{n}}u_{n})=\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})\leq\frac{1}{2^{n}}.

According to Theorem
9.18
there is
u\in W^{1,p}(B_{2R}(0))
and a subsequence still denoted
(\tau_{x_{n}}u_{n})_{n}
converging to
u
in
L^{p}(B_{2R}(0))
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(B_{2R}(0))}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\cE_{B_{2R}(0)}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})=0.

This implies that
\nabla u=0
almost everywhere on
B_{2R}(0)
, which is a connected set. Necessarily,
u=c
is a constant function on
B_{R}(0)
. However, since
\tau_{x_{n}}u_{n}=0
on
B_{2R}(0)\setminus B_{R}(0)
we deduce, via the convergence
\|\tau_{x_{n}}u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(B_{2R})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
, that
u=c=0
. We also have
\|u\|_{L^{p}(B_{2R}(0))}=1
since
\|\tau_{x_{n}}u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(B_{2R}(0))}=1
. We have reached a
contradiction.

Second proof
.
Claim.
First, we prove the following claim.

\displaystyle\text{For each $u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)$ there exists $x_{0}\in\Omega$ s.t. $\supp\,u\subset B_{R}(x_{0})$}.

(10.8)

Indeed, if
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we can find
x_{0}=x_{0}(u)\in\Omega
and
r=r(u)>0
so that
\supp u\subset B_{R}(x_{0})
and
\overline{B_{2r}(x_{0})}\subset\Omega
. Clearly, we have
z:=x_{0}+2re\in\overline{B_{2r}(x_{0})}\subset\Omega\subset H_{R}
. We find that

\displaystyle R\geq z\cdot e=2r+x_{0}\cdot e\geq 2r-R\quad\text{hence,}\quad r\leq R.

It follows that
\supp u\subset B_{R}(x_{0})
and hence
\tau_{x_{0}}u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{R}(0))
where we consider the shift
\tau_{x_{0}}u(x):=u(x+x_{0})
. Next, the set

G=\big\{u\in L^{p}(B_{2R}(0)):\text{$u=0$ a.e. on $B_{2R}(0)\setminus B_{R}(0)$}\big\}
is a closed subset of
L^{p}(B_{2R}(0))
such that
1\not\in G
and
G
is homogeneous, i.e.,
\lambda u\in G
whenever
u\in G
and
\lambda\in\R
.
Accordingly, by Theorem
10.1
we find
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(d,p,R)
and
B=B(d,p,R)
so that

\displaystyle\|v\|^{p}_{L^{p}(B_{2R}(0))}\leq B\cE_{B_{2R}(0)}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(v,v)\quad\text{for all $v\in G$, $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$}.

In particular, taking
v=\tau_{x_{0}}u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{R}(0))\subset G
, the result follows since

\displaystyle\|\tau_{x_{0}}u\|_{L^{p}(B_{2R}(0))}

\displaystyle=\|u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}=\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle\cE_{B_{2R}(0)}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(\tau_{x_{0}}u,\tau_{x_{0}}u)

\displaystyle\leq\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(\tau_{x_{0}}u,\tau_{x_{0}}u)=\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u,u).

∎

Theorem 10.8

(Robust Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
has finite measure, i.e.,
|\Omega|<\infty
. There exist
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(d,p,\Omega)>0
and
B=B(d,p,\Omega)
such that, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq B\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

(10.9)

Proof.

Assume
\varepsilon_{0}
and
B
do not exist. For
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}
and
B=2^{n}
,
n\geq 1
, there exist
\varepsilon_{n}\in(0,\frac{1}{2^{n}})
and
u_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
for which (
10.2
) fails, i.e.,
\|u_{n}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}>2^{n}\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})
.
We can assume without loss of generality that
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}=\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
so that
\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})\leq\frac{1}{2^{n}}
.

Consider
\Omega^{\prime}=\Omega\cup B
where
B
is, for instance, an arbitrary nonempty ball such that
B\subset\R^{d}\setminus\Omega
. Observe that
|\Omega^{\prime}|<\infty
and thus by Theorem
9.18
we find
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})

and a subsequence still denoted
(u_{n})_{n}
strongly converging to
u
in
L^{p}(\Omega^{\prime})
and hence
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega^{\prime})}=1
since
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega^{\prime})}=\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\cE_{\R^{d}}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})=0.

This implies that
u=c
is constant a.e. on
\R^{d}
. Since
u_{n}=0
on
B
as
u_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we find
\|u\|_{L^{p}(B)}\leq\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega^{\prime})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.
Wherefrom, we deduce that

u=c=0
a.e. on
\R^{d}
. This contradict
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
.
∎

By letting
\varepsilon\to 0
in Theorem
10.8
and/or Theorem
10.7
one recovers classical Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality.

Corollary 10.9

(Classical Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is bounded in one direction or has a finite measure, i.e.,
|\Omega|<\infty
then

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq BK_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $u\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)$}.

(10.10)

Next results is combined consequence of Theorem
10.7
and Theorem
10.8
.

Corollary 10.10
.

Assume that
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is bounded in one direction or that

|\Omega|<\infty
. There are
C=C(d,p,\Omega)
and
s_{0}=s_{0}(d,p,\Omega)
such that for every
s\in(s_{0},1)
and every
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq Cs(1-s)\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,dy\,dx.

(10.11)

Proof.

It suffices to put
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{p\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}|h|^{-d-(1-\varepsilon)p}
with
\varepsilon=1-s
and apply Theorem
10.7
and Theorem
10.8
.
∎

11.
Convergence of weak solutions

In this section we establish the convergence in
L^{p}(\Omega)
of weak solutions of nonlocal Dirichlet and Neumann problems to the corresponding local problems. We need the following Lemma involving the convergence from nonlocal to local of the Gauss-Green formula; see Theorem
B.8
. We point out that a new proof of the local divergence theorem which is derived from the nonlocal divergence theorem is established in
[
62
]
.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be bounded Lipschitz domain.
Let
v\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and
\varphi\in C^{2}_{b}(\R^{d})
. Assume that

\displaystyle\kappa_{\varphi}:=\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\|L_{\varepsilon}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}<\infty\quad\text{ for $1<p<2$}.

The following assertions hold true.

(1)

There is a constant
C_{\varphi}>0
independent of
\varepsilon
such that

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\varphi(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y\Big|\leq C_{\varphi}\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})}.

(2)

Assume
v\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
, recall
\partial_{n,p}\varphi(x)=|\nabla\varphi(x)|^{p-2}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot n(x)
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\varphi(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y=K_{d,p}\,\int_{\partial\Omega}\partial_{n,p}\varphi(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)\,.

Proof.

(i) In view of the estimate (
B.2
) for
p\geq 2
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\psi(u(x+h)-u(x))+\psi(u(x-h)-u(x))\Big|\leq C\|u\|^{p-1}_{C_{b}^{2}(\R^{d})}(1\land|h|^{p}),

with
\psi(t)=|t|^{p-2}t
, which implies

\displaystyle|L_{\varepsilon}\varphi|\leq C\|\varphi\|^{p-1}_{C^{2}_{b}(\R^{d})}\quad\text{for $p\geq 2$}.

Therefore taking into account the assumption, in case either we have

\displaystyle\kappa_{\varphi}:=\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\|L_{\varepsilon}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}<\infty\quad\text{ for $1<p<\infty$}.

(11.1)

Since
|\varphi(x+h)-\varphi(x)|\leq 2\|u\|_{C_{b}^{1}(\R^{d})}(1\land|h|)
we find that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi,\varphi)\leq 2^{p+1}|\Omega|\|\varphi\|^{p}_{C^{1}_{b}(\R^{d})}\quad\text{for all }\penalty\ \penalty\ \varepsilon>0\,.

(11.2)

Now, let
v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\R^{d})
. The nonlocal Gauss-Green formula (
B.6
) yields

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\varphi(y)v(y)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}y\Big|=\Big|\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi,v)-\int_{\Omega}L_{\varepsilon}\varphi(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi,\varphi)^{1/p^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(v,v)^{1/p}+\|L_{\varepsilon}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}|\Omega|^{1/p^{\prime}}\|v\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varphi}\|v\|_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})},

with
C_{\varphi}=|\Omega|^{1/p^{\prime}}\big(2^{(p+1)/p^{\prime}}\|\varphi\|^{p-1}_{C^{1}_{b}(\R^{d})}+\kappa_{\varphi}\big).
Note that
C^{\infty}_{c}(\R^{d})
is dense in
W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})
(see
[
54
, Theorem 3.70]
).
By the continuity of the linear mapping
v\mapsto\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi,v)-\int_{\Omega}L_{\varepsilon}\varphi(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x
, the Gauss-Green formula (
B.6
) is applicable for
\varphi\in C_{b}^{2}(\R^{d})
and
v\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. Therefore, the above estimate yields
(i)
.

(ii) By Theorem
9.9
,
L_{\varepsilon}\varphi(x)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}-K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}\varphi(x)
(a.e for
1<p<2
). Together with (
11.1
) and the fact that
v\in L^{p}(\Omega)\subset L^{1}(\Omega)
, the dominated convergence theorem yields

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}L_{\varepsilon}\varphi(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}-K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}\Delta_{p}\varphi(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\,.

If
v\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
, then
v|_{\Omega},\varphi|_{\Omega}\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Since
\partial\Omega
is Lipschitz, combining Theorem
9.15
, Lemma
11.1
and the fact that
\cE^{\varepsilon}(\varphi,\varphi)^{1/p^{\prime}}\leq C_{\varphi}
(by estimate (
11.2
)), we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi,v)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}K_{d,p}\,\mathcal{E}^{0}(\varphi,v).

Finally from the foregoing and the (non)local Gauss-Green formula we obtain
(ii)
as follows

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega^{c}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\varphi(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi,v)-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}L_{\varepsilon}\varphi(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\varphi(x)|^{p-2}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot\nabla v(x)\mathrm{d}x-K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}\Delta_{p}\varphi(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=K_{d,p}\int_{\partial\Omega}|\nabla\varphi(x)|^{p-2}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot n(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)\,.

∎

Theorem 11.2

(
Convergence of Neumann problem I
)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open bounded and connected with Lipschitz boundary. Let

f,f_{\varepsilon}\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
be such that
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
converges weakly sense to
f
. Let
g_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\varphi
and
g=\partial_{n,p}\varphi
where
\varphi\in C^{2}_{b}(\R^{d})
. In addition we assume

\displaystyle\kappa_{\varphi}:=\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\|L_{\varepsilon}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}<\infty\quad\text{ for $1<p<2$}.

Assume
w_{\varepsilon}\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})
is a weak solution of Neumann problem
L_{\varepsilon}u=f_{\varepsilon}
in
\Omega
and
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}u=g_{\varepsilon}
on
\Omega^{c}
that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(w_{\varepsilon},v)=\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\varepsilon}(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y\quad\text{for all }\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})\,.

Assume
w\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
is a weak solution to the problem
-K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u=f
in
\Omega
and
K_{d,p}\partial_{n,p}u=g
on
\partial\Omega
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{0}(w,v)=K_{d,p}^{-1}\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}g(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)\,\,\,\text{for all $u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)$}.

Put
u_{\varepsilon}=w_{\varepsilon}-\fint_{\Omega}w_{\varepsilon}
and
u=w-\fint_{\Omega}w
. Then
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
strongly converges to
u
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
, i.e.,
\|u_{\varepsilon}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0
. Moreover, the following weak convergence of the energies forms holds true

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},v)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}K_{d,p}\,\mathcal{E}^{0}(u,v)\quad\text{for all $v\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})$}.

Remark 11.3
.

(i) It is worth mentioning that, as proved in Section
8

u
and
u_{\varepsilon}
,
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, always exist thanks to the Poincaré type inequalities. However
w
(resp.
w_{\varepsilon}
) exists if and only if
f
and
K_{d,p}g
(resp.
f_{\varepsilon}
and
g_{\varepsilon}
) are compatible. Furthermore, the weak convergence of
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
and Lemma
11.1
implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\langle f_{\varepsilon},1\rangle+\langle g_{\varepsilon},1\rangle=\langle f,1\rangle+\langle K_{d,p}g,1\rangle.

(ii) To prove the convergence of solutions here as well as in the upcoming convergence results, we implicitly use the Gamma convergence of the nonlocal forms
(\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\cdot))_{\varepsilon}
to the local
\mathcal{E}^{0}(\cdot,\cdot)
(which follows from results established in Section
9.5
) and hence
the Gamma convergence of the respective associated functionals
(\mathcal{J}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
to the local functional
\mathcal{J}
.

Proof.

The robust Poincaré inequality (see Corollary
10.2
) implies the existence of
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
and
C=C(d,p,\Omega)>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and all
v\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\|v-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}v$}\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq C\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(v,v).

(11.3)

By the weak convergence of
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
, up to relabeling
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, we can assume

M:=\sup\limits_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}<\infty
, so that

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega^{c}}f_{\varepsilon}(x)u_{\varepsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}x\Big|\leq M\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})},

whereas, Lemma
11.1

(i)
yields

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\varepsilon}(y)u_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}y\Big|=\Big|\int_{\Omega^{c}}\cN_{\varepsilon}\varphi(y)u_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}y\Big|\leq C_{\varphi}\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})}.

Since
u_{\varepsilon}\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
, by definition of
u_{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(x)u_{\varepsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\varepsilon}(y)u_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})}(\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}+C_{\varphi})\leq C\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})}.

Combining this with (
11.3
) yields the following uniform boundedness for a generic constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon}\|^{p-1}_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega)}\leq\|u_{\varepsilon}\|^{p-1}_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq C\qquad\text{for all }\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})\,.

(11.4)

Accordingly, by the asymptotic compactness Theorem
9.18
, there is
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and subsequence
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
such that
(u_{\varepsilon_{n}})_{n}
converges to
u
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
and we have

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\cE^{0}(u,u)\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon}).

In particular we get
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)^{\perp}
since each
u_{\varepsilon}\in L^{p}(\Omega)^{\perp}
. Next, we show that
u
is, in fact, the unique weak solution to the Neumann problem
-K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u=f
in
\Omega
and
K_{d,p}\partial_{n,p}u=g
on
\partial\Omega
.
To this end, it is sufficient to show that

\displaystyle\cJ(u)

\displaystyle=\min\{\cJ(v):\,v\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)^{\perp}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}K_{d,p}\mathcal{E}^{0}(v,v)-\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x-K_{d,p}\int_{\partial\Omega}g(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}\sigma(x).

Recall that, by Proposition
8.11
, each
u_{\varepsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\cJ^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\min\{\cJ^{\varepsilon}(v):\,v\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{\varepsilon}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(v,v)-\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\varepsilon}(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Now we consider
v\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)^{\perp}
. Given that
\partial\Omega
is Lipschitz, i.e.,
\Omega
is an extension domain, we let
\overline{v}\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
be an extension of
v
. In view of Lemma
11.1
, Theorem
9.15
and the weak convergence we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{J}^{\varepsilon}(\overline{v})=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\big(\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(\overline{v},\overline{v})-\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\varepsilon}(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}K_{d,p}\mathcal{E}^{0}(v,v)-\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x-K_{d,p}\int_{\partial\Omega}g(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)=\cJ(v).

The strong convergence of
(u_{\varepsilon_{n}})_{n}
and the weak convergence of
(f_{\varepsilon_{n}})_{n}
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
yield

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x)u_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}f(x)u(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Analogously, by further taking into account the uniform boundedness of
(L_{\varepsilon_{n}}\varphi)_{n}
(see (
11.1
)), the pointwise convergence
L_{\varepsilon_{n}}\varphi(x)\to-K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}\varphi(x)
(see Theorem
9.9
) and the Gauss-Green formula (see Appendix
B.2
) we get

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\varepsilon_{n}}(y)u_{\varepsilon_{n}}(y)\mathrm{d}y=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega^{c}}\cN_{\varepsilon_{n}}\varphi(y)u_{\varepsilon_{n}}(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\cE^{\varepsilon_{n}}(\varphi,u_{\varepsilon_{n}})-\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}L_{\varepsilon_{n}}\varphi(y)u_{\varepsilon_{n}}(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=K_{d,p}\cE^{0}(\varphi,u)-K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}\Delta_{p}\varphi(x)u(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=K_{d,p}\int_{\partial\Omega}\partial_{n,p}\varphi(x)u(x)\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)=K_{d,p}\int_{\partial\Omega}g(x)u(x)\mathrm{d}\sigma(x).

Altogether with the lower estimate
K_{d,p}\cE^{0}(u,u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\cE^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{\varepsilon_{n}},u_{\varepsilon_{n}})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\cJ(u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\cJ^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{\varepsilon_{n}}).

Recall that each
u_{\varepsilon_{n}}
minimizes the functional
\cJ^{\varepsilon_{n}}
. Since,
\int_{\Omega}\overline{v}(x)\mathrm{d}x=0
and
\overline{v}\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})\subset W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})
, we find that

\displaystyle\cJ(u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\cJ^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{\varepsilon_{n}})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\cJ^{\varepsilon_{n}}(\overline{v})=\cJ(v).

It turns out that
\|u_{\varepsilon_{n}}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
and
u
minimizes
\cJ
that is,

\displaystyle\cJ(u)

\displaystyle=\min_{v\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)^{\perp}}\cJ(v).

Whence
u
is the unique weak solution to the Neumann on
W^{1,p}(\Omega)^{\perp}
that is

\displaystyle\cE^{0}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}g(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)\,\qquad\text{for all $v\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)^{\perp}$}.

The uniqueness of
u
implies that
\|u_{\varepsilon}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Moreover for
v\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
we have
v-c\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)^{\perp}\cap W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
with
c=\fint_{\Omega}v
and hence

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},v)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},v-c)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(x)(v(x)-c)\mathrm{d}x+\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\varepsilon}(y)(v(y)-c)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}f(x)(v(x)-c)\mathrm{d}x+K_{d,p}\int_{\partial\Omega}g(x)(v(x)-c)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=K_{d,p}\cE^{0}(u,v-c)=K_{d,p}\cE^{0}(u,v).

∎

The above convergence remains for weak solutions associated with the regional operators;

\displaystyle L_{\Omega,\varepsilon}u(x)=2\pv\int_{\Omega}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y.

Theorem 11.4

(
Convergence of Neumann problem II
)
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
11.2
be in force. Let
u_{\varepsilon}\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega)^{\perp}
be a weak solution to the regional Neumann problem
L_{\Omega,\varepsilon}u=f_{\varepsilon}
on
\Omega
and
\int_{\Omega}u=0
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(u_{\varepsilon},v)=\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all }\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega)^{\perp}\,.

Let
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)^{\perp}
be the weak solution of
-K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u=f
in
\Omega
and
\partial_{n,p}u=0
on
\partial\Omega
i.e.

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\,\mathcal{E}^{0}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)^{\perp}$}.

Then
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
strongly converges to
u
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
, i.e.,
\|u_{\varepsilon}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0
. Moreover, the following weak convergence of the energies forms holds true

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},v)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}K_{d,p}\,\mathcal{E}^{0}(u,v)\quad\text{for all $v\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)$}.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of Theorem
11.2
.
∎

Theorem 11.5

(
Convergence of Dirichlet problem
)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open with a continuous boundary and in addition that
|\Omega|<\infty
or that
\Omega
is bounded in one direction. Let

g\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
and
f,f_{\varepsilon}\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
such that
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
converges weakly to
f
. Let
u_{\varepsilon}\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon},0}(\Omega|\R^{d})
be the weak solution to the Dirichlet problem
L_{\varepsilon}u=f_{\varepsilon}
on
\Omega
and
u=g
on
\Omega^{c}
, i.e.,
u-g\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon},0}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},v)=\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all }\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon},0}(\Omega|\R^{d})\,.

Let
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
be the weak solution to the problem
-K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u=f
in
\Omega
and
u=g
on
\partial\Omega
i.e.,
u-g\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
and

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\mathcal{E}^{0}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $u\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)$}.

Then
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
strongly converges to
u
in
L^{p}_{\loc}(\R^{d})
, where we put
u=g
on
\Omega^{c}
, i.e., we have
\|u_{\varepsilon}-u\|_{L^{p}(B)}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0
for any bounded set
B\subset\R^{d}
. If, in addition,
|\Omega|<\infty
then we have
\|u_{\varepsilon}-u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0
. Moreover, the weak convergence of the energies forms holds, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},v)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}K_{d,p}\mathcal{E}^{0}(u,v)\qquad\text{for all $v\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)$}.

Proof.

By the robust Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality (see Theorem
10.7
and Theorem
10.8
) there exist
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
and
C=C(d,p,\Omega)>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and all
v\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon},0}(\Omega|\R^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\|v\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\leq C\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(v,v).

(11.5)

By virtue of the weak convergence of
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
, up to relabeling
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, we can assume that

M:=\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}<\infty
. Furthermore, since
g\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
and
\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=1
, by the estimate (
3.4
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|g(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|g(x)-g(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\leq 2^{p+1}\|g\|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}.

By virtue of the coercivity estimate (
11.5
), by proceeding similarly as for the proof of (
8.14
), one finds a constant
C>0
independent of
\varepsilon
such that, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})}&\leq C\big(\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}+\|g\|_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\R^{d})}\big)\\
&\leq C(M+2^{p+1}\|g\|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}).\end{split}

(11.6)

Therefore taking into account that
\|g\|_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega^{c})}\leq 2^{p+1}\|g\|_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}
and
u_{\varepsilon}=g
on
\Omega^{c}
and, we deduce from the estimate (
11.6
) that

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\R^{d})}=\big(\|g\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega^{c})}+\|u_{\varepsilon}\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\big)^{1/p}\leq C\qquad\text{for all }\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}),

for some
C=C(d,p,\Omega,M,g)>0
. Accordingly, by the asymptotic compactness Theorem
9.18
, there is
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
and subsequence
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
such that
(u_{\varepsilon_{n}})_{n}
converges to
u
in
L^{p}_{\loc}(\R^{d})
. Moreover, there holds

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\int_{\R^{d}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE^{\varepsilon}_{\R^{d}}(u_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon}),

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\cE^{0}(u,u)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon}).

In particular we have
u=g
on
\Omega^{c}
since
u_{\varepsilon}=g
on
\Omega^{c}
. Therefore, since
\partial\Omega
is continuous, we get
u-g\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
. Next, we show that
u
is, in fact, the unique weak solution to the Dirichlet problem
-K_{d,p}\Delta_{p}u=f
in
\Omega
and
u=g
on
\partial\Omega
.
To this end, it is sufficient to show that
\cJ(u)=\min\{\cJ(v):\,v-g\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}K_{d,p}\mathcal{E}^{0}(v,v)-\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Recall that, by Proposition
8.17
, each
u_{\varepsilon}
satisfies
\cJ_{0}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=\min\{\cJ_{0}^{\varepsilon}(v):\,v-g\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon},0}(\Omega|\R^{d})\}
,

\displaystyle\cJ_{0}^{\varepsilon}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\cE^{\varepsilon}(v,v)-\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Now we consider
v\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
such that
v-g\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
. Thus, we can consider
\overline{v}
be the extension of
v
by
\overline{v}=g
on
\Omega^{c}
so that
\overline{v}\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d}
(since
v
and
g
have the same trace on
\partial\Omega
) and
v-g\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon},0}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. Since, each
u_{\varepsilon_{n}}
minimizes
\cJ^{\varepsilon_{n}}_{0}
and
\overline{v}-g\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon},0}(\Omega|\R^{d})
, we have
\cJ_{0}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{\varepsilon_{n}})\leq\cJ_{0}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(\overline{v}).
In view of Theorem
9.15
and the weak convergence we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{J}^{\varepsilon}_{0}(\overline{v})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\big(\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(\overline{v},\overline{v})-\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}K_{d,p}\mathcal{E}^{0}(v,v)-\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x=\cJ_{0}(v).

The strong convergence of
(u_{\varepsilon_{n}})_{n}
and the weak convergence of
(f_{\varepsilon_{n}})_{n}
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
yield

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x)u_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}f(x)u(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Whence, we deduce that

\displaystyle\cJ_{0}(u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\cJ_{0}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(u_{\varepsilon_{n}})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\cJ_{0}^{\varepsilon_{n}}(\overline{v})=\cJ_{0}(v).

It turns out that,
\|u_{\varepsilon_{n}}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
,
u-g\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
and

\displaystyle\cJ_{0}(u)

\displaystyle=\min_{v-g\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)}\cJ_{0}(v).

In other words,
u
is the unique weak solution to the Dirichlet problem on
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
that is
u-g\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
and

\displaystyle\cE^{0}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)$}.

The uniqueness of
u
implies that
\|u_{\varepsilon}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Moreover, for
v\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
, if
v=0
on
\Omega^{c}
then we have
v\in W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon},0}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and hence

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\cE^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},v)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x=K_{d,p}\cE^{0}(u,v).

∎

Let us recall (see Section
9.4
) the fractional
p
-Laplacian and the corresponding normal derivative

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u(x)

\displaystyle:=C_{d,p,s}\pv\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{\psi(u(x)-u(y))}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y,

\displaystyle\cN_{s}u(y)

\displaystyle:=C_{d,p,s}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\psi(u(y)-u(x))}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}x.

Theorem 11.6
.

Assume the conditions of Theorem
11.2
hold. Let
f,f_{s}\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
be such that
(f_{s})_{s}
converges weakly sense to
f
as
s\to 1
and we put
g_{s}=\cN_{s}\varphi
and
\partial_{n,p}\varphi
.
Let
w_{s}\in W^{s,p}(\Omega|\R^{d})
,
s\in(0,1)
be a weak solution to the Neumann problem

(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u=f_{s}
on
\Omega
and
\cN_{s}u=g_{s}
on
\Omega^{c}
.

Let
w\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
be a weak solution to the Neumann problem

-\Delta_{p}u=f
in
\Omega
and
\partial_{n,p}u=g
on
\partial\Omega
.

Put
u_{s}=w_{s}-\fint_{\Omega}w_{s}
and
u=w-\fint_{\Omega}w
. Then
(u_{s})_{s}
strongly converges to
u
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
, i.e.,
\|u_{s}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{s\to 1}0
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to consider
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=a_{d,p,\varepsilon}|h|^{-d-(1-\varepsilon)p}
,
a_{d,p,\varepsilon}=\frac{p\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}
in Theorem
11.2
, accounting the fact that the asymptotic of the normalizing constant
C_{d,p,s}
yields

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{C_{d,p,1-\varepsilon}}{a_{d,p,\varepsilon}}=\lim_{s\to 1}\frac{C_{d,p,s}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{ps(1-s)}=\frac{2}{K_{d,p}}.

∎

Theorem 11.7
.

Assume the assumptions of Theorem
11.5
hold. Let
f,f_{s}\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
be such that
(f_{s})_{s}
converges weakly sense to
f
as
s\to 1
. Let
u_{s}\in W^{s,p}_{0}(\Omega|\R^{d})
,
s\in(0,1)
be the weak solution to the Dirichlet problem

(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}u=f_{s}
on
\Omega
and
u=g_{s}
on
\Omega^{c}
.

Let
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
be the weak solution of the Dirichlet problem

-\Delta_{p}u=f
in
\Omega
and
u=g
on
\partial\Omega
.

Then
(u_{s})_{s}
strongly converges to
u
in
L^{p}_{\loc}(\R^{d})
, where we put
u=g
on
\Omega^{c}
. If in addition
|\Omega|<\infty
then we have
\|u_{s}-u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\xrightarrow{s\to 1}0
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to consider
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=a_{d,p,\varepsilon}|h|^{-d-(1-\varepsilon)p}
,
a_{d,p,\varepsilon}=\frac{p\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}
in Theorem
11.5
, accounting the fact that the asymptotic of the normalizing constant
C_{d,p,s}
yields

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{C_{d,p,1-\varepsilon}}{a_{d,p,\varepsilon}}=\lim_{s\to 1}\frac{C_{d,p,s}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{ps(1-s)}=\frac{2}{K_{d,p}}.

∎

Appendix A

A.1.
Elementary estimates for the
p
-Laplacian and
p
-Lévy operators

We establish elementary estimates involving the mapping
x\mapsto|x|^{p-2}x
,
x\in\R^{d}
,
p\geq 1
, useful in the study of the
p
-Laplacian and
p
-Lévy operators. We adopt the convention
|x|^{p-2}x=0
if
x=0
. Although these estimates are elementary the case
1<p<2
seems to be seldom in the literature.

Lemma A.1
.

For
x,y\in\R^{d},
there hold the following inequalities

\displaystyle\big||x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y\big|\leq A_{p}|x-y|(|x|+|y|)^{p-2},

\displaystyle\big(|x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y\big)\cdot(x-y)\geq A^{\prime}_{p}|x-y|^{2}(|x|+|y|)^{p-2}\,.

The constants are given by
A_{p}=p-1
,
A^{\prime}_{p}=\min(2^{-1},2^{2-p})
if
p\geq 2
and
A_{p}=2^{2-p}(3-p)\leq 2^{3-p}
,
A^{\prime}_{p}=p-1
if
1\leq p<2
.
One easily verifies that
A_{p}\leq 2^{1+|p-2|}
and
A^{\prime}_{p}\geq\min(p-1,2^{1-p})
.

Proof.

If
p\geq 2
then by monotonicity,
(|y|^{p-2}-|x|^{p-2})(|y|^{2}-|x|^{2})\geq 0
and hence using
(a+b)^{q}\leq\max(1,2^{q-1})(a^{q}+b^{q})
for all
q,a,b\in(0,\infty)
we get

\displaystyle(|x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y)\cdot(x-y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(|x|^{p-2}+|y|^{p-2})|x-y|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}(|x|^{p-2}-|y|^{p-2})(|x|^{2}-|y|^{2})

\displaystyle\geq\min(2^{-1},2^{2-p})(|x|+|y|)^{p-2}|x-y|^{2}.

On the other hand, the fundamental theorem of calculus implies

\displaystyle|x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y=(x-y)\int_{0}^{1}|tx+(1-t)y|^{p-2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+(p-2)\int_{0}^{1}|tx+(1-t)y|^{p-4}\big[(tx+(1-t)y)\cdot(x-y)\big](tx+(1-t)y)\mathrm{d}t\,.

Thus, if
-1<p-2<0
, by Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and
|tx+(1-t)y|^{2-p}\leq(|x|+|y|)^{2-p}
,

\displaystyle(||x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y|)\cdot(x-y)

\displaystyle\geq(p-1)|x-y|^{2}\int_{0}^{1}|tx+(1-t)y|^{p-2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq(p-1)|x-y|^{2}(|x|+|y|)^{p-2}.

This completes the proof of the second inequality. Analogously, if
p\geq 2
then

\displaystyle||x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y||

\displaystyle\leq(p-1)|x-y|\int_{0}^{1}(t|x|+(1-t)|y|)^{p-2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq(p-1)|x-y|(|x|+|y|)^{p-2}\,.

It remains to prove the first inequality when
1<p<2
. Without loss of generality, assume

x\neq y
,
|y|\leq|x|
and
y=|y|e
with
|e|=1
. Put
z=x|y|^{-1}
, it suffices to bound
F(z)
where

\displaystyle F(z)=\frac{||z|^{p-2}z-e|}{|z-e|(1+|z|)^{p-2}},\quad\text{for}\,\,z\in\R^{d}\setminus\{e\},\,\,|z|\geq 1.

Put
r=|z-e|
so that
|z|\leq 1+r
. Since
|z|^{p-2}z-e=|z|^{p-2}((z-e)+(1-|z|^{2-p})e)
we get

\displaystyle\frac{||z|^{p-2}z-e|}{|z-e|(1+|z|)^{p-2}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+|z|^{-1})^{2-p}\Big(1+\frac{(|z|^{2-p}-1)}{|z-e|}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2-p}\big(1+\frac{1}{r}((1+r)^{2-p}-1)\big)

\displaystyle=2^{2-p}\big(1+\frac{(2-p)}{r}\int_{0}^{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{(1+\tau)^{p-1}}\big)\leq 2^{2-p}(3-p).

∎

A direct consequence of Lemma
A.1
is the following; see also
[
6
, Lemma 2.2]
.

Corollary A.2
.

Let
x,y\in\R^{d}
and
\beta\geq 0
. There hold the following inequalities

\displaystyle\big||x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y\big|\leq A_{p}|x-y|^{1-\beta}(|x|+|y|)^{p-2+\beta},

\displaystyle\big(|x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y\big)\cdot(x-y)\geq A^{\prime}_{p}|x-y|^{2+\beta}(|x|+|y|)^{p-2-\beta}\,.

Proof.

It suffices to see that
|x-y|(|x|+|y|)^{-1}\leq 1
and use Lemma
A.1
.
∎

Lemma A.3

(Simon inequalities)
.

The following inequalities hold true for all
x,y\in\R^{d}.

\displaystyle\hskip-8.61108pt\big||x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y\big|\leq

\displaystyle A_{p}|x-y|(|x|+|y|)^{p-2}

p\geq 2,

(A.1)

\displaystyle\hskip-8.61108pt\big||x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y\big|\leq

\displaystyle A_{p}|x-y|^{p-1}

1\leq p<2
.

(A.2)

\displaystyle\hskip-8.61108pt\big(|x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y\big)\cdot(x-y)\geq

\displaystyle\hskip-4.30554ptA^{\prime}_{p}|x-y|^{p}

p\geq 2
,

(A.3)

\displaystyle\hskip-8.61108pt\big(|x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y\big)\cdot(x-y)\geq

\displaystyle\hskip-8.61108ptA^{\prime}_{p}|x-y|^{2}(|x|+|y|)^{p-2}

1\leq p<2
.

(A.4)

Proof.

The claims follow from Corollary
A.2
by taking
\beta=0
for (
A.1
) and (
A.4
) and
\beta=|p-2|
for
(
A.2
) and (
A.3
). Note however that, (
A.2
) also follows from (
A.3
) by duality. Indeed, if
1<p<2
then
p^{\prime}>2
where
p+p^{\prime}=pp^{\prime}
. Keeping in mind that by duality,
z^{\prime}=|z|^{p-2}z
if and only if
z=|z^{\prime}|^{p^{\prime}-2}z^{\prime}
the estimate (
A.3
) implies

\displaystyle|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\max(2,2^{p^{\prime}-2})\big(|x^{\prime}|^{p^{\prime}-2}x^{\prime}-|y^{\prime}|^{p^{\prime}-2}y^{\prime}\big)\cdot(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\max(2,2^{p^{\prime}-2})|x-y||x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|.

Since
(p-1)(p^{\prime}-1)=1
and
\max(2^{p-1},2^{2-p})<2<2^{3-p}
it follows that

\displaystyle\big||x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y\big|

\displaystyle\leq\max(2^{p-1},2^{2-p})|x-y|^{p-1}\leq 2^{3-p}|x-y|^{p-1}.

(A.5)

∎

The constant
2^{2-p}
is expected in (
A.2
) as this is the case in one dimension.

Lemma A.4
.

If
1\leq p<2
there holds following inequality

\displaystyle||b|^{p-2}b-|a|^{p-2}a|\leq 2^{2-p}|b-a|^{p-1}\quad\text{for all $a,b\in\R$}.

(A.6)

Proof.

The case
p=1
is obvious. We only prove for
1<p<2
, i.e.,
\sigma=p-1\in(0,1)
. For
t\in\R
, we have

\displaystyle||t|^{\sigma-1}t-1|=\begin{cases}t^{\sigma}-1&\text{if}\,\,\,t\geq 1,\\
1-t^{\sigma}&\text{if}\,\,\,0\leq t<1,\\
(-t)^{\sigma}+1&\text{if}\,\,\,t<0.\end{cases}

Since
t\mapsto t^{\sigma-1}
is decreasing on
(0,\infty)
it follows that
t^{\sigma}=(t-1)t^{\sigma-1}+t^{\sigma-1}\leq(t-1)^{\sigma}+1
for
t\geq 1
, that is
t^{\sigma}-1\leq(t-1)^{\sigma}
and
1=(1-t)\times 1^{\sigma-1}+t\times 1^{\sigma-1}\leq(1-t)^{\sigma}+t^{\sigma}
for
0<t<1
, that is
1-t^{\sigma}\leq(1-t)^{\sigma}.
The concavity of
t\mapsto t^{\sigma}
implies
(-t)^{\sigma}+1\leq 2^{1-\sigma}(-t+1)^{\sigma}
for
t<0.
Altogether, we obtain the following inequality

\displaystyle||t|^{\sigma-1}t-1|\leq 2^{1-\sigma}|t-1|^{\sigma}\quad\text{for all $t\in\R$ and $\sigma\in[0,1]$}.

Thus (
A.6
) is inherited from the one above by taking
t=\frac{b}{a}
,
a\neq 0
.
∎

Let us see some useful consequences of Lemma
A.3
.

Corollary A.5
.

The following inequalities hold true for all
x,y\in\R^{d}.

\displaystyle|x|^{p}-|y|^{p}-p|y|^{p-2}\cdot y(x-y)

\displaystyle\leq\hskip-4.30554pt\begin{cases}A_{p}|x-y|^{p}&\hskip-17.22217pt1\leq p<2,\\
\frac{p}{2}A_{p}|x-y|^{2}(|x-y|+2|y|)^{p-2}&p\geq 2.\end{cases}

\displaystyle|x|^{p}-|y|^{p}-p|y|^{p-2}y\cdot(x-y)

\displaystyle\geq\hskip-4.30554pt\begin{cases}A^{\prime}_{p}|x-y|^{p}&p\geq 2,\\
\frac{p}{2}A^{\prime}_{p}|x-y|^{2}(|x-y|+2|y|)^{p-2}&\hskip-8.61108pt1\leq p<2.\end{cases}

Proof.

Applying the fundamental theorem of calculus on
t\mapsto|z_{t}|,

z_{t}=y+t(x-y)
implies

\displaystyle|x|^{p}-|y|^{p}-p|y|^{p-2}y\cdot(x-y)

\displaystyle=p\int_{0}^{1}\big(|z_{t}|^{p-2}z_{t}-|y|^{p-2}y\big)\cdot(z_{t}-y)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}.

The result is inherited from Lemma
A.3
and
|y|+|z_{t}|\leq(2|y|+|x-y|)
.
∎

Corollary A.6
.

For
R>0
,
q>0
there is
c_{q,R}>0
such that

|a|^{q-1}a-|a-b|^{q-1}(a-b)\leq c_{q,R}\max(b,b^{q})\quad\text{for all $|a|\leq R$, $b\geq 0$.}

Proof.

If
b>R
then since
t\mapsto|t|^{q-1}t
is increasing,
and
|a|\leq R
we have

-|a-b|^{q-1}(a-b)\leq-|a-R|^{q-1}(a-R)\leq|a-R|^{q}\leq 2^{q}R^{q}.

Therefore, since
R^{q}\leq\max(b,b^{q})
, we get

\displaystyle a^{q-1}a-|a-b|^{q-1}(a-b)\leq|a|^{q}+2^{q}R^{q}\leq 2^{q+1}R^{q}\max(b,b^{q}).

Now if
q\in(0,1]
then since
||x|^{q-1}x-|y|^{q-1}y|\leq 2^{2-q}|x-y|^{q}
(see (
A.2
)) it follows that

\displaystyle|a|^{q-1}a-|a-b|^{q-1}(a-b)\leq 2^{2-q}\max(b,b^{q}).

Last, if
q>1
and
b\leq R
then
|a-b|^{q-1}\leq 2^{q-1}R^{q-1}
.
The estimate (
A.1
) for
p=q+1
yields

\displaystyle|a|^{q-1}a-|a-b|^{q-1}(a-b)|\leq qb(|a|^{q-1}+(|a|+b)^{q-1})\leq q2^{q}R^{q-1}\max(b,b^{q}).

∎

Corollary A.7
.

For
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}\in\R
, let
b=b_{1}-b_{2},
and
a=a_{1}-a_{2}
, there holds the following inequality

\displaystyle(\psi(b)-\psi(a))

\displaystyle((b_{1}-a_{1})_{+}-(b_{2}-a_{2})_{+})

\displaystyle\geq\begin{cases}A^{\prime}_{p}|(b_{1}-a_{1})_{+}-(b_{2}-a_{2})_{+}|^{p}&p\geq 2,\\
A^{\prime}_{p}|(b_{1}-a_{1})_{+}-(b_{2}-a_{2})_{+}|^{2}(|b|+|a|)^{p-2}&1<p<2,\end{cases}

where
\psi(t)=|t|^{p-2}t
,
t_{+}=\max(t,0)
and
t_{-}=\max(-t,0)
so that
t=t_{+}-t_{-}
.

Proof.

First of all, sincxe
t\mapsto\psi(t)
is increasing (
\psi^{\prime}(t)=(p-1)|t|^{p-2}
) we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\psi(b)-\psi(a))&((b_{1}-a_{1})_{+}-(b_{2}-a_{2})_{+}))\\
&=|\psi(b)-\psi(a)||(b_{1}-a_{1})_{+}-(b_{2}-a_{2})_{+}|.\end{split}

(A.7)

For instance, if
(b_{2}-a_{2})\leq 0
and
(b_{1}-a_{1})\geq 0
then we have

\displaystyle b-a=(b_{1}-a_{1})-(b_{2}-a_{2})\geq(b_{1}-a_{1})_{+}=(b_{1}-a_{1})_{+}-(b_{2}-a_{2})_{+}\geq 0.

In particular,
\psi(a)\leq\psi(b)
and hence the relation (
A.7
) follows. The other cases can be derived analogously.
On the other hand, the estimates (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) yield

\displaystyle|\psi(b)-\psi(a)|\geq\begin{cases}A^{\prime}_{p}|b-a|^{p-1}&p\geq 2,\\
A^{\prime}_{p}|b-a|(|b|+|a|)^{p-2}&1<p<2,\end{cases}

Using the fact that
|t_{+}-s_{+}|\leq|t-s|
, the desired estimates follow from the relation (
A.7
).
∎

Another important consequence of Lemma
A.1
is the following.

Theorem A.8
.

Let
p,q\in[1,\infty)
. Under the notations of Lemma
A.1
, we get for all
x,y\in\R^{d}
that

\displaystyle\big||x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y\big|\leq\hskip-2.15277ptA_{p}\big[A^{\prime}_{\frac{p-2}{q}+2}\big]^{-q}|x-y|^{1-q}\big||x|^{\frac{p-2}{q}}x-|y|^{\frac{p-2}{q}}y\big|^{q},

\displaystyle\big(|x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y\big)\cdot(x-y)\geq\hskip-2.15277ptA^{\prime}_{p}\big[A_{\frac{p-2}{q}+2}\big]^{-q}|x-y|^{2-q}\big||x|^{\frac{p-2}{q}}x-|y|^{\frac{p-2}{q}}y\big|^{q}\,.

Proof.

Since
\frac{p-2}{q}+2\geq 1
, it suffices to apply Lemma
A.1
for
p
and
\frac{p-2}{q}+2
.

\displaystyle\big[(|x|+|y|)^{\frac{p-2}{q}}\big]^{q}\leq\big[A^{\prime}_{\frac{p-2}{q}+2}\big]^{-q}|x-y|^{-q}\big||x|^{\frac{p-2}{q}}x-|y|^{\frac{p-2}{q}}y\big|^{q},

\displaystyle\big[(|x|+|y|)^{\frac{p-2}{q}}\big]^{q}\geq\big[A_{\frac{p-2}{q}+2}\big]^{-q}|x-y|^{-q}\big||x|^{\frac{p-2}{q}}x-|y|^{\frac{p-2}{q}}y\big|^{q}.

∎

Appendix B

B.1.
Pointwise evaluation of the operator
L
and
\cN

We aim in this appendix to provide ancillary results about the
p
-Lévy operator that enters the scope at hand.
As usual we assume
\nu
is symmetric and
p
-Lévy integrable, i.e.,
\nu(h)=\nu(-h)
and
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
.

\displaystyle Lu(x)

\displaystyle=2\pv\int_{\R^{d}}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}y=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}L_{\varepsilon}u(x),

\displaystyle L_{\varepsilon}u(x)

\displaystyle=2\int_{\R^{d}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(x)}\!\!\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x\!-\!y)\mathrm{d}y\qquad\hbox{$(x\in\R^{d};\varepsilon>0)$}.

The pointwise definition of
Lu(x)
and
-\Delta_{p}u(x)
for
u\in C^{2}_{b}(\R^{d})
is warranted in the degenerate case (also often called the superquadratic case), i.e.
p\geq 2
. However, in the situation singular case (also often called the subquadratic case), i.e.,
1<p<2
,
the pointwise definition of
Lu(x)
and
-\Delta_{p}u(x)
might not exist even for a bona fide function
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
.
In general, it is difficult to characterize a set of all functions on which the operators
L
and
-\Delta_{p}
act in a reasonable pointwise sense. A reasonable alternative in both cases, i.e.,
1<p<\infty
, is rather to evaluate
Lu
and
\Delta_{p}u
in the generalized sense, i.e., in the weak sense or via their respective associated energies forms. For instance, by duality the following identifications are well-defined;

\displaystyle\langle Lu,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{\R^{d}}(u,\varphi)\qquad u,\varphi\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\R^{d}),

\displaystyle\langle\Delta_{p}u,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{d}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla\varphi(x)\mathrm{d}x\qquad u,\varphi\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d}).

From the duality point of view, one obtains that
Lu\in(W^{p}_{\nu}(\R^{d}))^{\prime}
and
\Delta_{p}u\in(W^{1,p}(\R^{d}))^{\prime}
.
It turns out that the operators
L:W^{p}_{\nu}(\R^{d})\to(W^{p}_{\nu}(\R^{d}))^{\prime}
and
\Delta_{p}:W^{1,p}(\R^{d})\to(W^{1,p}(\R^{d}))^{\prime}
are well-defined. We emphasize that

\langle Lu,\varphi\rangle
and
\langle\Delta_{p}u,\varphi\rangle
are given as above.
Morally, the nonlocal operator
L
may be as good or as bad as the local operator
-\Delta_{p}
. The operator
L
can be seen as a prototype of a nonlinear nonlocal operator of divergence just as the
-\Delta_{p}
is a prototype of a nonlinear local operator of divergence.

Next, to investigate the pointwise evaluation of
Lu
, we need to introduce consider Hölder spaces. Denote the space
C_{b}^{k+\gamma}(\R^{d})=\big\{u\in C^{k}(\R^{d})\,:\,\|u\|_{C_{b}^{k,\gamma}(\R^{d})}<\infty\big\}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
with the norm

\|u\|_{C_{b}^{k,\gamma}(\R^{d})}=\sum_{|\beta|\leq k}\sup_{x\in\R^{d}}|\partial^{\beta}u(x)|+\sum_{|\beta|=k}\sup_{x\neq y\in\R^{d}}\frac{|\partial^{\beta}u(x)-\partial^{\beta}u(y)|}{|x-y|^{\gamma}}.

For
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
, we define

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{\gamma}=\begin{cases}p+\gamma-1&\quad\text{if}\quad p\geq 2,\\
(\gamma+1)(p-1)&\quad\text{if}\quad 1<p<2.\end{cases}

(B.1)

Remark B.1
.

Note that
\widehat{p}_{\gamma}\leq p
and hence we find that
L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}})\subset L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p}).
For the particular case
\gamma=0
we have
p_{0}=p-1
and the case
\gamma=1
we have

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{1}=\begin{cases}p&\text{if }p\geq 2,\\
2(p-1)&\text{if }1<p<2.\end{cases}

For the subclass of kernels
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}})
, it is possible to evaluate
Lu
pointwise when
u
is sufficiently smooth.

Proposition B.2
.

Assume
u\in C^{1+\gamma}_{b}(\R^{d})
,
0<\gamma\leq 1
and
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}})
.

(1)

The map
x\mapsto Lu(x)
is bounded and uniformly continuous. Moreover,

\displaystyle Lu(x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\R^{d}}\big[\psi(u(x+h)-u(x))+\psi(u(x-h)-u(x))\big]\,\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\R^{d}}\big[\psi(u(x+h)-u(x))-\mathds{1}_{B_{1}(0)}(h)\psi(\nabla u(x)\cdot h)\big]\,\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h.

(2)

The map
x\mapsto L_{\varepsilon}u(x),

0<\varepsilon<1,
is uniformly continuous.

(3)

The family
(L_{\varepsilon}u(x))_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded and uniformly converges to
Lu,
i.e.

\displaystyle\|L_{\varepsilon}u-Lu\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\R^{d}
, note that
h\mapsto\psi(\nabla u(x)\cdot h)\nu(h)
has vanishing integral over
B_{1}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{1}(0)\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(0)}\psi(\nabla u(x)\cdot h)\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h=0\qquad\text{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ 0<\varepsilon<1.

This observation readily implies that

\displaystyle L_{\varepsilon}u(x)

\displaystyle=-2\int_{B^{c}_{\varepsilon}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt\big[\psi(u(x+h)-u(x))-\mathds{1}_{B_{1}(0)}(h)\psi(\nabla u(x)\cdot h)\big]\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h,

whereas, the simple change of variables
y=x\pm h
gives

\displaystyle L_{\varepsilon}u(x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{B^{c}_{\varepsilon}(0)}\big[\psi(u(x+h)-u(x))+\psi(u(x-h)-u(x))\big]\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h.

We emphasize that
L_{\varepsilon}u(x)
exists, since
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(0))
.
For
h\in\R^{d}
, by the mean value Theorem,
u(x+h)-u(x)=\nabla u(x+\tau h)\cdot h
for some
\tau\in(0,1)
. Thus the estimates (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) with
a=\nabla u(x-\tau_{1}h)\cdot h
and
b=\nabla u(x+\tau_{2}h)\cdot h
for suitable
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in[0,1]
yield

\displaystyle\Big|\psi(u(x+h)-u(x))\hskip-2.15277pt+\hskip-2.15277pt\psi(u(x-h)-u(x))\Big|\hskip-2.15277pt\leq\hskip-2.15277ptC\|u\|^{p-1}_{C_{b}^{1+\gamma}(\R^{d})}(1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}}),

(B.2)

\displaystyle\Big|\psi(u(x+h)-u(x))-\psi(\nabla u(x)\cdot h)\Big|\leq C\|u\|^{p-1}_{C_{b}^{1+\gamma}(\R^{d})}(1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}}).

(B.3)

Here,
C>0
is a generic constant only depending on
p
and
d
. In view of the estimates (
B.2
) and (
B.3
), since
h\mapsto(1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}})\nu(h)
is integrable, the boundedness of
x\mapsto Lu(x)
and the uniform boundedness of
x\mapsto L_{\varepsilon}u(x)
follow and one also gets rid of the principal value. In addition, the uniform convergence of
(L_{\varepsilon}u)_{\varepsilon}
follows since

\displaystyle\|L_{\varepsilon}u-Lu\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}\leq C\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{1+\gamma}_{b}(\R^{d})}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(0)}(1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}})\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0.

Turning to the uniform continuity, we fix
x,z\in\R^{d}
such that
|x-z|\leq\delta
with
0<\delta<1
.
For every h
\in\R^{d}
,
h\neq 0
, the estimates (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) imply

\displaystyle|\psi(u(x)-u(x\pm h))-\psi(u(z)-u(z\pm h))|\leq C\delta^{\gamma\kappa_{p}}\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{1+\gamma}_{b}(\R^{d})},

(B.4)

where
\kappa_{p}=1
if
p\geq 2
and
\kappa_{p}={p-1}
if
1<p<2
. This combined with (
B.2
) yields

\displaystyle|\psi(u(x)-u(x\pm h))-\psi(u(z)-u(z\pm h))|\leq C\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{1+\gamma}_{b}(\R^{d})}(\delta^{\gamma\kappa_{p}}\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}}).

Therefore, the integrability of
h\mapsto(1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}})\nu(h)
implies the uniform continuity as follows

\displaystyle\|Lu(x)-Lu(z)\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}\leq C\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{1+\gamma}_{b}(\R^{d})}\int_{\R^{d}}(\delta^{\gamma\kappa_{p}}\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}})\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\delta\to 0}0.

The uniform continuity of
x\mapsto L_{\varepsilon}u(x)
follows analogously.
∎

It is natural to seek for a larger functional space on which
Lu
is defined.
In an attempt to answer this question, assume in addition that
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty)
is unimodal, i.e.
\nu
is radial and almost decreasing, i.e., there is a constant
c
such that
\nu(y)\leq c\nu(x)
whenever
|y|\geq|x|
. Let us define the function

\widehat{\nu}(x)=\nu(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+|x|)).

Remark B.3
.

Assume
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(0))
,
\varepsilon>0
and
\nu
is unimodal.

(1)

It is straightforward to show that
\widehat{\nu}\in L^{1}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
.

(2)

The space
L^{p-1}(\R^{d},\widehat{\nu})
contains the spaces
C^{1+\gamma}_{\loc}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
,
L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
and
C_{b}^{1+\gamma}(\R^{d})
.

(3)

If
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
,
s\in(0,1)
, then
\widehat{\nu}(h)\asymp(1+|h|)^{-d-sp}
.

Proposition B.4
.

Assume that
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}})
with
0<\gamma\leq 1
, and that
\nu
is almost decreasing, i.e., there is
c>0
such that
\nu(x)\geq c\nu(y)
whenever
|x|\leq|y|
.

(1)

If
u\in C^{1+\gamma}_{\loc}(\R^{d})\cap L^{p-1}(\R^{d},\widehat{\nu})
then
Lu(x)
is well defined for all
x\in\R^{d}
.

(2)

If
u\in C^{1+\gamma}_{b}(\R^{d})
and
\supp u\subset B_{R}(0)
,
R\geq 1
then there is
C=C(R,d,\nu)>0

\displaystyle|Lu(x)|\leq C\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{2}_{b}(\R^{d})}\widehat{\nu}(x)\qquad\text{for all}\penalty\ x\in\R^{d}.

(B.5)

In particular, it holds that
Lu\in L^{1}(\R^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
.

Proof.

(i)
Set
R=2|x|+1
,
x\in\R^{d}
, then for
y\in B^{c}_{R}(0)
we have

|x-y|\geq\frac{1}{2}(1+|y|)
so that
\nu(x-y)\leq c\widehat{\nu}(y)
. Thus, for
u\in L^{p-1}(\R^{d},\widehat{\nu})
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{B^{c}_{R}(0)}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C|u(x)|^{p-1}\|\widehat{\nu}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}

\displaystyle+C\int_{B^{c}_{R}(0)}|u(y)|^{p-1}\widehat{\nu}(y)\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

We conclude that
Lu(x)
exists since by exploiting (
B.2
) we get

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{B_{R}(0)}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\Big|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big|\int_{B_{R}(0)}\psi(u(x)-u(x-h))+\psi(u(x)-u(x+h))\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{1+\gamma}(B_{R}(0))}\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}})\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h<\infty.

(ii)
Assume
\supp u\subset B_{R}(0)
for some
R\geq 1
. If
|x|\geq 4R
then
u(x)=0
. For
y\in B_{R}(0)
, we get
\nu(x-y)\leq c\widehat{\nu}(x)
since
|x-y|\geq\frac{|x|}{2}+R\geq\frac{1}{2}(1+|x|).
Accordingly,

\displaystyle|Lu(x)|\leq\int_{B_{R}(0)}|u(y)|^{p-1}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\leq c|B_{R}(0)|\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{1+\gamma}_{b}(\R^{d})}\widehat{\nu}(x).

Whereas, if
|x|\leq 4R
then
\frac{1}{2}(1+|x|)\leq 4R
and hence
\widehat{\nu}(x)\geq c_{1}
with
c_{1}=c\nu(4R)>0
. The proof of (
B.5
) is complete as follows using (
B.2
),

\displaystyle|Lu(x)|\leq c_{1}^{-1}C\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{\gamma}})}\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{1+\gamma}_{b}(\R^{d})}\widehat{\nu}(x).

∎

Next we show that
Lu
remains continuous under less regularity on
u
provided that
\nu
is in the subclass of kernels
L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{\gamma(p-1)})\subset L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
.

Theorem B.5
.

Let
u\in C^{\gamma}_{b}(\R^{d}),

0<\gamma\leq 1
. Assume that

\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{\gamma(p-1)})
then
Lu
bounded and uniform continuous. Moreover, for all
x,z\in\R^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle|Lu(x)-Lu(z)|\leq C\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{\gamma}(\R^{d})}\omega(|x-z|),

where
\omega:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
is an increasing modulus of continuity such that
\omega(r)\xrightarrow{r\to 0}0.

Proof.

Recall that
\kappa_{p}=1
if
p\geq 2
and
\kappa_{p}={p-1}
if
1<p<2
. For
r>0
, the estimate (
B.4
) gives

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{B^{c}_{r}(0)}\psi(u(x)-u(x+h))-\psi(u(z)-u(z+h))\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{\gamma}(\R^{d})}|x-z|^{\gamma\kappa_{p}}\int_{B^{c}_{r}(0)}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h.

Using
|\psi(u(\cdot)-u(\cdot+h))|\leq|h|^{\gamma(p-1)}
we get

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{B_{r}(0)}\psi(u(x)-u(x+h))-\psi(u(z)-u(z+h))\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{\gamma}(\R^{d})}\int_{B_{r}(0)}|h|^{\gamma(p-1)}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h.

Summing the previous inequalities yields

\displaystyle|Lu(x)-Lu(z)|\leq C\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{\gamma}(\R^{d})}\big[g(r)|x-z|^{\gamma\kappa_{p}}+h(r)\big].

where
g
and
h
are the monotone functions,

\displaystyle g(r)=\int_{B^{c}_{r}(0)}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h,\quad\quad h(r)=\int_{B_{r}(0)}|h|^{\gamma(p-1)}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h.

Note that
\rho(r)=\big(\frac{h(r)}{g(r)}\big)^{1/\gamma\kappa_{p}}
is increasing and
\rho(r)\xrightarrow{r\to 0}0
. Taking
r=\rho^{-1}(|x-z|)
we obtain

\displaystyle|Lu(x)-Lu(z)|\leq C\|u\|^{p-1}_{C^{\gamma}(\R^{d})}\omega(|x-z|),\qquad\text{$\omega(|x-z|)=h\circ\rho^{-1}(|x-z|)$}.

∎

The nonlocal normal derivative
\mathcal{N}u
of a measurable function
u:\R^{d}\to\R
can be thought of as the restriction on
\R^{d}\setminus\Omega
of the regional operator on
\Omega
, namely,

\displaystyle L_{\Omega}u(x)=-\cN u(x)=\int_{\Omega}\psi(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\qquad\text{$x\in\R^{d}\setminus\Omega$}.

It is often desirable to know when the pointwise definition
\mathcal{N}u(x)
makes sense at least almost everywhere.

Proposition B.6
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open. The following assertions are true.

(1)

If
u|_{\Omega}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
then
\mathcal{N}u(x)
exists for almost all
x\in\R^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
.

(2)

If
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
then
\cN u\in L^{q}_{\loc}(\R^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega})
for any
1\leq q\leq p^{\prime}
.

(3)

If
u\in W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
then
\cN u\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\R^{d},w^{-1}(x))
,
w(x)=\int_{\Omega}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y
.

Proof.

(i)
Observing that
\delta_{x}=\dist(x,\partial\Omega)>0
we get

\displaystyle|\cN u(x)|\leq(\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+|u(x)|)^{p-1}\int_{|h|>\delta_{x}}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h<\infty.

(ii)
Let
K\subset\R^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
be compact and put
\delta=\dist(K,\partial\Omega)>0
so
that
|x-y|>\delta
whenever
x\in K
and
y\in\Omega
. The Hölder inequality implies

\displaystyle\int_{K}|\cN u(x)|^{q}\ dx\leq\int_{K}\Big(\int_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-1}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{q}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{K}\Big(\int_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{\frac{q}{p^{\prime}}}\Big(\int_{\Omega}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{\frac{q}{p}}\ dx

\displaystyle\leq|K|^{1-\frac{q}{p^{\prime}}}\Big(\int_{|h|>\delta}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)^{\frac{q}{p}}\Big(\int_{K}\int_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{q}{p^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq|K|^{1-\frac{q}{p^{\prime}}}\Big(\int_{|h|>\delta}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)^{\frac{q}{p}}|u|^{q(p-1)}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}.

(iii)
The Hölder inequality implies

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|\cN u(x)|^{p^{\prime}}w(x)^{-1}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\R^{d}}\Big(\int_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-1}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{p^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\R^{d}}\int_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\ dx\leq|u|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}.

∎

B.2.
Gauss-Green type formula

In this section, we establish the nonlocal Gauss-Green type formula associated with the operator
L
. We start with the following general formula.

Theorem B.7

(General nonlocal integration by parts)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open bounded. Let
k:\R^{d}\times\R^{d}\setminus\diag\to[0,\infty)
be measurable, anti-symmetric, i.e.,
k(x,y)=-k(y,x)
, satisfying

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\R^{d}}\int_{\R^{d}}\vskip-8.61108pt|k(x,y)|\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

For every
v\in L^{\infty}(\R^{d})
the following identity holds

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\hskip-4.30554pt\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\hskip-4.30554pt(v(x)-v(y))k(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\hskip-4.30554pt\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}\hskip-4.30554ptv(x)k(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x-\hskip-4.30554pt\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega}\hskip-4.30554ptv(y)k(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Proof.

First, observe that
(x,y)\mapsto v(x)k(x,y)
belongs to
L^{1}(\Omega\times\R^{d})
since

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}|v(x)||k(x,y)|\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\|v\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\sup_{x\in\R^{d}}\int_{\R^{d}}|k(x,y)|\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

Thus Fubini’s theorem and the anti-symmetry
k(x,y)=-k(y,x)
imply

\displaystyle 2\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\hskip-3.01385ptv(x)k(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\hskip-3.01385pt(v(x)-v(y))k(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

Likewise, it follows that

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}v(x)k(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(v(x)-v(y))k(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}v(y)k(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega}v(y)k(x,y){\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}y}+\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(v(x)-v(y))k(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega}(v(x)-v(y))k(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

Summing up altogether yields the claim, since
\Omega\times\R^{d}=\Omega\times\Omega\cup\Omega\times\Omega^{c}
.
∎

Recall that if
p\geq 2
then
\widehat{p}_{1}=p
and hence
L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{1}})=L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
and if
1<p<2
then
\widehat{p}_{1}=2(p-1)
and
L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{1}})\subset L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
.

Theorem B.8

(
Gauss-Green formula
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open bounded,
u\in C^{2}_{b}(\R^{d})
and
v\in C^{1}_{b}(\R^{d})
. Assume either
(i)

p\geq 2
,
(ii)

1<p<2
and
(L_{\varepsilon}u)_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded or
(iii)

\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{1}})
. There holds that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}Lu(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{E}(u,v)-\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}u(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y,

(B.6)

In particular, for
v=1
, one gets the integration by part formula

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}Lu(x)\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}u(y)\mathrm{d}y.

(B.7)

Proof.

Since
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(0))
,
\varepsilon>0
and
u,v
are bounded, Theorem
B.7
implies that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}(v(x)-v(y))k_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}v(x)k_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x-\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega}v(y)k_{\varepsilon}(x,y){\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}y}\end{split}

(B.8)

where
k_{\varepsilon}(x,y)
is the anti-symmetric kernel defined by

\displaystyle k_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=2|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-2}(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\mathds{1}_{B^{c}_{\varepsilon}}(x-y).

Note that
\widehat{p}_{1}=p
,
p\geq 2
and
\widehat{p}_{1}=2(p-1)
,
1<p<2
so that
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{\widehat{p}_{1}})\subset L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
. In any case, by Proposition
B.2
,
(L_{\varepsilon}u)_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded. Whence,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}Lu(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}L_{\varepsilon}u(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}v(x)k_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

On the other hand, since
\Omega
is bounded and
u,v\in C_{b}^{1}(\R^{d})
we have

\displaystyle|(v(x)-v(y))k_{\varepsilon}(x,y)|\leq C(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\in L^{1}(\Omega\times\R^{d}).

By the convergence dominated theorem we have

\displaystyle\cE(u,v)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}(v(x)-v(y))k_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

The desired formula is obtained by letting
\varepsilon\to 0
in (
B.8
) as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}Lu(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-\cE(u,v)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}v(x)k_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}(v(x)-v(y))k_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega}v(y)k_{\varepsilon}(x,y){\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}y}=-\int_{\Omega^{c}}\cN u(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y.

∎
